Microlocal maximal hypoellipticity from the geometric viewpoint: I
Omar Mohsen

Abstract
Given some vector fields on a smooth manifold satisfying Hörmander’s condition, we define a bi-graded pseudo-differential calculus which contains the classical pseudo-differential calculus and a pseudo-differential calculus adapted to the sub-Riemannian structure induced by the vector fields. Our approach is based on geometric constructions (resolution of singularities) together with methods from operators algebras. We develop this calculus in full generality, including Sobolev spaces, the wavefront set, and the principal symbol, etc. In particular, using this calculus, we prove that invertibility of the principal symbol implies microlocal maximal hypoellipticity. This allows us to resolve affirmatively the microlocal version of a conjecture of Helffer and Nourrigat.



Introduction

Let
M
be a smooth manifold,
\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}
the bundle of half-densities on
TM
. A pseudo-differential operator
P:C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is called hypoelliptic if for any distribution
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
on
M
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{singsupp}(P(w))=\mathrm{\singsupp}(w).

Equivalently, if
P(w)
is smooth on an open subset
U\subseteq M
, then
w
is also smooth on
U
.
Similarly,
P
is called microlocally hypoelliptic on a cone (a subset that is closed under the
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
-dilation)
\Gamma\subseteq T^{*}M\backslash 0
if

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}(P(w))\cap\Gamma=\mathrm{WF}(w)\cap\Gamma,\quad\forall w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

If
P
is microlocally hypoelliptic on
T^{*}M\backslash 0
, then it is hypoelliptic.
A well-known sufficient criterion for microlocal hypoellipticity is that the classical principal symbol of
P
vanishes nowhere on
\Gamma
.

In our previous work
[
3
]
, we generalised the main regularity theorem for elliptic operators to a sub-Riemannian setting. Thus giving a general sufficient criterion for hypoellipticity of differential operators, and answering the global and local versions of the Helffer-Nourrigat conjecture.
In this article, we generalise our theorem in two simultaneous directions: First, we microlocalize the theorem and thus solving the microlocal version of Helffer-Nourrigat conjecture.
Second, we extend our theorem to what we call multi-parameter sub-Riemannian geometry.

Let us start with the context for our theorem. For simplicity, we will describe the context of our theorem in the
2
-parameter setting.
Our article treats the more general setting of
\nu
-parameters where
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
is arbitrary.

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
vector fields satisfying Hörmander’s condition, that is for any
x\in M
, the tangent space
T_{x}M
is linearly spanned
by
X_{1}(x),\cdots,X_{n}(x)
and
[X_{i},X_{j}](x)
and higher iterated Lie brackets evaluated at
x
.
Let
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be any natural numbers thought of as weights associated to each vector field
X_{i}
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a_{i}|t
for all
i
.
We define Sobolev spaces
H^{(s,t)}(M)
adapted to the vector fields
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
and their weights
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
as follows: For any
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,

\displaystyle w\in H^{(s,t)}(M)\iff Q(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)\forall Q,

where
Q(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
is any noncommutative formal polynomial of weighted degree
\leq t
where each variable
x_{i}
is given weight
a_{i}
, and whose coefficients are classical pseudo-differential operators of order
\leq s
.

In this article we extend these Sobolev spaces to
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
by an interpolation type procedure. We prove that these Sobolev spaces satisfy the following Sobolev lemma:

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=\bigcap_{s\in\mathbb{R}}H^{(s,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)=\bigcap_{t\in\mathbb{R}}H^{(s,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M),\quad\forall s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

(1)

These Sobolev spaces generalise both the classical local Sobolev spaces
H^{s}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
and the Sobolev spaces we defined in
[
3
]
,
H^{t}_{\mathrm{sub-Riemannian}}(M)
, by the relations

\displaystyle H^{s}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)=H^{(s,0)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M),\quad H^{t}_{\mathrm{sub-Riemannian}}(M)=H^{(0,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M),\quad\forall s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

We also define a bi-graded pseudo-differential calculus
\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)
, where
k,l\in\mathbb{C}
.
This calculus contains both the classical pseudo-differential calculus and a pseudo-differential calculus adapted to the sub-Riemannian structure defined by the vector fields
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
and their weights
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
.
More precisely, we have the following properties:

i.

If
k\in\mathbb{C},l\in\mathbb{N}
, then for any
Q(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
noncommutative formal polynomial of weighted degree
\leq l
whose coefficients are classical pseudo-differential operators of order
\leq k
, one has
Q(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})\in\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)
.
In particular, if
P
is a classical pseudo-differential operator of order
k\in\mathbb{C}
, then
P\in\Psi^{(k,0)}(M)
, and for any
i
,
X_{i}\in\Psi^{(0,a_{i})}(M)
.

ii.

If
P
is an operator in the calculus we defined in
[
3
]
of order
k\in\mathbb{C}
, then
P\in\Psi^{(0,k)}(M)
.
For example, for any
s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
2a_{i}|s
for all
i
, and the generalised Hörmander sum of squares

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{SubRiem}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}^{*}X_{i})^{\frac{s}{2a_{i}}}\in\Psi^{(0,s)}(M)

admits a parametrix in
\Psi^{(0,-s)}(M)
.

So, this calculus allows one to mix operators from Riemannian geometry like elliptic operators and operators in sub-Riemannian geometry like the generalised Hörmander sum of squares and its parametrix.
This mixture of calculi together with the fact that our definition is quite geometric (see
Chapter

2
)
and that our calculus has a principal symbol (which we will define shortly) makes this calculus useful in transporting questions from sub-Riemannian geometry to
classical pseudo-differential operators and vice versa, as well as apply homotopy type arguments between the two.
For example, homotopies of the form
\Delta^{t}\Delta_{\mathrm{SubRiem}}^{1-t}\in\Psi^{(2t,s(1-t))}(M)
.
The fact that we define appropriate Sobolev spaces on which such operators are Fredholm and that Fredholmness in general can be detected using our principal symbol is among the principal strengths of our calculus.
This will be exploited in forthcoming articles.

Operators in our calculus act continuously between the Sobolev spaces defined above:

\displaystyle P\in\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)\implies P\left(H^{(s+\Re(k),t+\Re(l))}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)\right)\subseteq H^{(s,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M),\quad\forall k,l\in\mathbb{C},s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

(2)

Motivated by microlocal hypoellipticity, we define a refinement of the wave-front set. Let
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
The set
\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(w)
is the cone in
T^{*}M\backslash 0
defined as follows:

\displaystyle(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(w)\iff\exists w^{\prime}\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),w^{\prime}\in H^{(s,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)\text{ and }(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}(w-w^{\prime}).

In other words,
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(M)
if
w
belongs to
H^{(s,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
microlocally around
(\xi,x)
.
The set
\mathrm{WF}_{s,t}(w)
is a closed cone in
T^{*}M\backslash 0
.
These wave-front sets recover the classical wave-front set by the relation

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}(w)=\overline{\bigcup_{s,t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(w)}.

(3)

Furthermore, as expected,
\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(w)=\emptyset
if and only if
w\in H^{(s,t)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
.
We can microlocalize (
2
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(P(w))\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{(s+\Re(k),t+\Re(l))}(w),\quad\forall P\in\Psi^{(k,l)}(M),k,l\in\mathbb{C},s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

We now come to the main definition of interest to us

Definition 0.0.1
.

One says that
P\in\Psi^{(k,l)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
is microlocally maximally hypoelliptic of order
(\Re(k),\Re(l))
on a cone
\Gamma\subseteq T^{*}M\backslash 0
if

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(P(w))\cap\Gamma=\mathrm{WF}_{(s+\Re(k),t+\Re(l))}(w)\cap\Gamma,\quad\forall w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

(4)

One says that
P
is maximally hypoelliptic of order
(\Re(k),\Re(l))
if it is microlocally maximally hypoelliptic on
T^{*}M\backslash 0
.
By (
3
), microlocal maximal hypoellipticity on
\Gamma
implies microlocal hypoellipticity on
\Gamma
.

The main strength in our calculus lies in the fact that we define a principal symbol which detects microlocal maximal hypoellipticity.
So, let us now define our principal symbol.
Since the principal symbol is a local object, we can suppose for simplicity that in Hörmander’s condition, we only need iterated commutators of depth
\leq N\in\mathbb{N}
.
This is always satisfied locally or if
M
is compact. To simplify the exposition, we will also restrict our attention in the introduction to operators
P=Q(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})\in\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)
where
Q
is noncommutative formal polynomial of weighted degree
\leq l\in\mathbb{N}

whose coefficients are classical pseudo-differential operators of order
\leq k\in\mathbb{C}
(when
k=0
, then these are exactly the operators which appear in the microlocal Helffer-Nourrigat conjecture).

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the free nilpotent Lie algebra of depth
N
with
n
generators
\tilde{X}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{X}_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
, and
G
be the simply connected nilpotent Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}
.
Let
\pi
be an irreducible unitary representation of
G
on a Hilbert space
L^{2}(\pi)
,
C^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq L^{2}(\pi)
the subspace of smooth vectors,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathrm{End}(C^{\infty}(\pi))
the differential of
\pi
.
We define the principal symbol by

\displaystyle\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,\xi,x)=Q_{\xi,x,\mathrm{max}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pi(\tilde{X}_{1})\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pi(\tilde{X}_{n})):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi),

(5)

where
Q_{\xi,x,\mathrm{max}}
is the homogeneous polynomial constructed from
Q
where we removed all lower order terms and replaced all coefficients
L
with
\sigma^{k}_{\mathrm{classical}}(L,\xi,x)\in\mathbb{C}
, the classical principal symbol of
L
at
(\xi,x)
.
There is the obvious issue: Is this well-defined?
A priori, (
5
) depends on the presentation of
P
as a polynomial in
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
. Different presentations of
P
might give different values for
\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,\xi,x)
.
In
Section

4.2
, we define for each
(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0
, a set
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)}
of irreducible unitary representations of
G
which we call the Helffer-Nourrigat cone at
(\xi,x)
.
We can now state the main theorem of this article.

Theorem A
.

Let

•

M
be a smooth manifold.

•

X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
vector fields satisfying Hörmander’s condition.

•

k,l\in\mathbb{C}
, and
P\in\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)
.

Then, for any
(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0
and
\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)}
,
\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,\xi,x):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)
is well-defined.
Let

\displaystyle\Gamma=\left\{(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0:\forall\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)},\ \sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,\xi,x):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)\text{ is injective}\right\}

The following hold:

i.

The cone
\Gamma
is open, and
\Gamma\subseteq\{(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0:(-\xi,\xi;x,x)\in\mathrm{WF}(P)\}
, where
\mathrm{WF}(P)
is the wave-front set of the Schwartz kernel of
P
.

ii.

The operator
P
is microlocally maximally hypoelliptic on
\Gamma
of order
(\Re(k),\Re(l))
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(P(w))\cap\Gamma=\mathrm{WF}_{(s+\Re(k),t+\Re(l))}(w)\cap\Gamma.

(6)

Furthermore, if
\Gamma^{\prime}
is any cone such that (
6
) holds (with
\Gamma
replaced by
\Gamma^{\prime}
) for some
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
.
So,
\Gamma
is the largest cone on which
P
is microlocally maximally hypoelliptic.

iii.

For any closed cone
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
.
There exists a continuous
P^{\prime}:C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that for any
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
and
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}_{(s,t)}(P^{\prime}(w))\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{(s-\Re(k),t-\Re(l))}(w),\quad\mathrm{WF}(P^{\prime}(P(w))-w)\cap\Gamma^{\prime}=\emptyset.

(7)

In particular, by (
3
),
\mathrm{WF}(P^{\prime}(w))\subseteq\mathrm{WF}(w)
. So,
P^{\prime}(C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))\subseteq C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

iv.

For any
P^{\prime\prime}\in\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)
which is compactly supported,
there exists
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
a classical pseudo-differential operator of order
0
,
C>0
and
R\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
a smoothing operator
such that
\chi
is elliptic on
\Gamma
and

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi(P^{\prime\prime}(w))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)}\leq C\left(\left\lVert P(w)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)}+\left\lVert R(w)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)}\right),\quad\forall w\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(8)

If
\Gamma=T^{*}M\backslash 0
, then one can suppose that
\chi=\mathrm{Id}
.

v.

If
M
is compact and
\Gamma=T^{*}M\backslash 0
, then for any
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,

P:H^{(s+\Re(k),t+\Re(l))}(M)\to H^{(s,t)}(M)
is left-invertible modulo compact operators.

vi.

If
(\xi,x)\in\Gamma
,
\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)}
, then the operators

\displaystyle\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,x):C^{-\infty}(\pi)\to C^{-\infty}(\pi)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,x):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)

admit a continuous left-inverse. Here,
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
is the topological dual of
C^{\infty}(\pi)
, which contains
L^{2}(\pi)
, and is called the space of distribution vectors of
\pi
. The map
\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,x):C^{-\infty}(\pi)\to C^{-\infty}(\pi)
is defined using duality.

Remarks 0.0.2
.

Here are some remarks on
A
.

i.

If
P
is a classical pseudo-differential operator of order
k\in\mathbb{C}
, seen as element of
\Psi^{(k,0)}(M)
, then
\sigma^{(k,0)}(P,\pi,\xi,x)=\sigma^{k}_{\mathrm{classical}}(P,\xi,x)
is the classical principal symbol.
In this case, Theorem
A
is the microlocalized main regularity theorem for elliptic operators.
If
k=0
and
\Gamma=T^{*}M\backslash 0
, then
A
is the main theorem of
[
3
]
.
It is actually quite stronger than the main theorem of
[
3
]
because we couldn’t obtain (
7
) in
[
3
]
.

ii.

I don’t know if the parametrix
P^{\prime}
belongs to
\Psi^{(-k,-l)}(M)
. This question will be investigated in a forthcoming article.

iii.

All the results of this article work equally well if one adds vector bundle coefficients everywhere, i.e., one considers two complex vector bundles
E,F
, and operators
P:C^{\infty}(M,E\otimes\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}(M,F\otimes\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
In this case, the principal symbol
\sigma^{(k,l)}(P,\pi,\xi,x)
is an operator from
C^{\infty}(\pi)\otimes E_{x}
to
C^{\infty}(\pi)\otimes F_{x}
. The theory extends in a rather straightforward way.
To simplify the notation above all, we decided to omit vector bundles.

iv.

Theorem A
.

A
.
iii
is optimal in the sense that we can’t take
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma
unless
\Gamma=T^{*}M\backslash 0
in which case
\Gamma
is closed.

Corollary 0.0.3
.

The microlocal version of the Helffer-Nourrigat conjecture
[
28
, Conjcture 2.3]
is true.

Proof.

Their conjecture is that (
8
) holds when
k=0
and
P
and
P^{\prime\prime}
are noncommutative formal polynomial of weighted degree
\leq l\in\mathbb{N}

whose coefficients are classical pseudo-differential operators of order
0
.
∎

We prove many properties of the Sobolev spaces, principal symbol, wave-front set in this calculus
see
Chapter

4
for the statements in full generality.
Many of these properties generalise well-known properties of the classical pseudo-differential calculus.
But some aren’t as expected. In fact, they have no counterpart in classical calculus, for example
4.4.6
, see the preceding paragraph.
We also refer the reader to
Section

4.7
where we compute the principal symbol in an explicit example, and where
4.4.6
has subtle and interesting consequences of diagonalizability of certain Schrodinger-type operators.

There has been some work in recent years by different authors on multi-parameter pseudo-differential calculus, see for instance Street’s books
[
51
,
50
]
and the references there.
Street’s work considers (
8
) on different Sobolev-type spaces. In particular, the following is proved:

Theorem 0.0.4

(
[
51
, Proposition 8.2.12 and Corollary 6.9.2]
)
.

Inequality (
8
) holds with the
L^{2}
-norm if and only if it holds with the
L^{2}
-norm replaced by the
L^{p}
-norm for all
p\in]1,+\infty[
.

Street also defines a multi-parameter pseudo-differential calculus.
The calculus is defined in terms of estimates on the kernels which is a more analytic approach.
We expect that our calculus is contained in Street’s calculus and that this is not too hard to prove.
We are quite certain that the inclusion is strict.

The main distinction between our approach and previous ones in the literature lies in the systematic use of groupoids, together with substantial input from techniques from
C^{*}
-algebras.
We now outline the main steps of the proof of
A
, and at least explain why our proof is long.
The argument is divided into three main parts.

i.

Construction of the groupoid.

The first step consists in constructing the space
\mathbb{G}
in
Chapter

2
and
Chapter

3
.
This space is a generalization of the groupoid introduced in
[
37
]
(which is itself a generalization of Connes’ tangent groupoid
[
15
]
, and the groupoid of van Erp and Yuncken
[
53
]
and Choi and Ponge
[
13
]
).
The groupoid
\mathbb{G}
provides a resolution of the singularities arising from variations in the rank of the distribution generated by the vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and their iterated brackets.
The novelty in this construction compared to that of
[
37
]
is that this groupoid also takes into account the variation of the rank at the microlocal level.
This part of the proof is purely geometric and does not involve pseudo-differential operators.

This step presents several technical difficulties.
One major problem is that the space
\mathbb{G}
is not a smooth manifold but rather a differential space in the sense of Sikorski
[
48
]
and Aronszajn
[
5
]
.
So, it makes sense to talk about smooth functions on
\mathbb{G}
, but smooth functions generally don’t have a Taylor series expansion (this is the main reason why I don’t know if
P^{\prime}
belongs to
\Psi^{(-k,-l)}(M)
or not).
For this reason, we introduce a special class of smooth functions, which we call
invariant smooth functions
.
These functions behave like smooth functions on a smooth manifold.

All of these difficulties together with the intricate definition of
\mathbb{G}
lead to the length of
Chapter

2
and
Chapter

3
.
We refer the reader for example to
Section

2.4
where the groupoid is constructed in the quite simple setting where
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
X_{1}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
and
X_{2}=x^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
In this setting one clearly sees how subtle the definition of the groupoid is.

The idea behind the definition of the groupoid
\mathbb{G}
is quite simple. One embeds
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
inside a large enough Grassmannian manifold which takes into account all the different
singularities which one needs to resolve, and then one takes the closure of the image of this embedding. This idea is classical in algebraic geometry, and is usually called Nash’s resolution.

ii.

Construction of the pseudo-differential calculus.

The second step consists in constructing the calculus
\Psi^{(k,l)}(M)
.
This construction is inspired by the work of Debord and Skandalis
[
18
,
19
]
and van Erp and Yuncken
[
54
]
, where an analogous approach was developed for the classical pseudo-differential calculus using Connes’ tangent groupoid
[
15
]
.
This part of the argument is rather straightforward generalization of arguments from Debord, Skandalis, van Erp, and Yuncken.
The proofs get slightly longer due to the multi-parameter structure of the calculus but in my view, there are no new conceptual difficulties.

This part is mostly in
Section

5.1
, and
Section

5.3
.

iii.

Use of
C^{*}
-algebras of Type 1 techniques.

The third and final step is to prove
A
.
This part relies heavily on arguments from
C^{*}
-algebra theory.
The main difficulty arises from fact that
\Gamma
in
A
is defined using injectivity on
C^{\infty}(\pi)
.
If one instead assumes injectivity on the space
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
, this step becomes simpler.
Passing from injectivity on
C^{\infty}(\pi)
to injectivity on
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
is in some sense a hypoellipticity statement.
One needs to show that if a vector in
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
is in the kernel of the symbol map, then the vector is actually smooth.
In fact, if one uses Kirillov’s orbit method to construct the representations of
G
, then the symbol maps are pseudo-differential operators on some Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and the above statement on the kernel consisting of smooth vectors is a hypoellipticity statement, see
Section

4.7
where this is explicitly carried out in an example.

This part of the proof relies on the structure theory of
C^{*}
-algebras Type I, and more precisely on an abstract maximal hypoellipticity theorem that we proved in
[
38
]
.
This abstract result shows that in
C^{*}
-algebras of Type I, one can bootstrap injectivity from smooth vectors to distributional vectors.

Our previous work
[
3
]
follows a similar approach. The main differences are that the groupoid here is more complicated which leads to some technical difficulties in the first two Parts of the proof.
The third part of the proof is new and differs from the arguments we used in
[
3
]
.

For the reader looking to see only the general structure of the argument, in
[
38
, Section 2]
, we added a proof of the theorem of Helffer and Nourrigat
[
27
]
(their resolution of the Rockland conjecture) which is a special case of

A
.

[
38
, Section 2]
is considerably shorter and simpler.
The main simplification is that Step 1 becomes trivial, as the space
\mathbb{G}
is replaced by a simply connected nilpotent Lie group.
Consequently, the second and third steps are substantially easier to follow, even though the general structure of the argument remains essentially the same.

In forthcoming articles, we will present further applications of our calculus.

Organization of the article:

We tried to make this article as self-contained as we possibly could.
We don’t assume any familiarity with our previous work
[
3
,
37
]
.
In fact, in contrast to our previous work
[
3
]
, in this article, we don’t rely on or assume any familiarity with the results in
[
46
,
27
,
14
,
26
]
.
Technically, we don’t even assume Hörmander’s sum of squares theorem
[
30
]
(we could but that wouldn’t simplify our proof).
We can circumvent all the results in
[
46
,
27
,
14
,
26
]
thanks to the abstract maximal hypoellipticity theorem mentioned above.

We use techniques from groupoids and
C^{*}
-algebras. These techniques were developed by the non-commutative geometry community for the last 30 years.
We have included a chapter in the beginning of our article which introduces the necessary tools from groupoids and
C^{*}
-algebras.
This chapter is mainly based on Renault’s book
[
42
]
and Connes’s book
[
15
]
.

Finally, to make the article easier to read, we divided the proofs in
Chapter

2
and
Chapter

4
into separate
Chapter

3
and
Chapter

5
.
This way, the reader can more easily find all the results in this article.
Also, we tried to make
Chapter

5
as independent as possible from
Chapter

3
.
This way, the reader interested only in the pseudo-differential calculus can read
Chapter

4
and
Chapter

5
without going through
Chapter

3
.

We also added an appendix on Kirillov’s orbit method for nilpotent Lie groups, see
Appendix

A
, and on the abstract maximal hypoellipticity result from
[
38
]
, see
Appendix

B
.

Chapter 1
Quasi-Lie groupoids

Conventions and notations:

i.

We use
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\cdots\}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\mathbb{R}_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\geq 0\}
,
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x>0\}
.

ii.

If
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
n\leq m
, then
[\![n,m]\!]:=[n,m]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.

iii.

Functions, vector spaces and vector bundles are complex unless otherwise stated.

iv.

Hilbert spaces are always separable. Inner products
\langle\xi,\eta\rangle
are linear in
\eta
and antilinear in
\xi
.

1.1
Differential structures

In this chapter, we recall the notion of a differential structure space due to Sikorski
[
48
]
and Aronszajn
[
5
]
, see
[
49
,
17
]
for a modern treatment of differential spaces.

Definition 1.1.1
.

A differential structure on a topological space
M
is a non-empty subset
C^{\infty}(M)
of the space of continuous complex-valued functions
C(M)
such that:

i.

For every
x,y\in M
with
x\neq y
, there exists
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
f(x)\neq f(y)
.

ii.

If
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, then
F(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

iii.

If
f:M\to\mathbb{C}
is a function such that for every
x\in M
, there exists a neighborhood
U
of
x
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
f_{|U}=g_{|U}
, then
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

A differential space is a Hausdorff locally compact second countable
1
1

1

Some authors only include a subset of these conditions in the definition of a differential space.
equipped with a differential structure.
A differential space is metrizable by Urysohn’s metrization theorem.
We denote by
C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
the subspace of
C^{\infty}(M)
of functions with compact support.
By Stone–Weierstrass theorem,
C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
is a dense
*
-subalgebra of
C_{0}(M)
.

Proposition 1.1.2

(
[
49
, Theorem 2.2.4]
)
.

Let
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
be an open cover of a differential space
M
. There exists a countable family
f_{n}:M\to[0,1]
of smooth functions such that:

•

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an
i\in I
, such that
\supp(f_{n})\subseteq U_{i}
.

•

The sum
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f_{n}
is locally finite and equal to
1
.

Generating set:

Let
M
be a topological space,
\mathcal{F}\subseteq C(M)
a subset which satisfies
Section

1.1.1
.
i
.
We define the differential structure generated by
\mathcal{F}
to be the set of functions
f:M\to\mathbb{C}
such that for every
x\in M
, there exists a neighborhood
U
of
x
,
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{F}
,
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
such that
f_{|U}=F(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})_{|U}
.

Subspaces:

If
A\subseteq M
is a locally closed subset of a differential space
M
, then
C^{\infty}(A)
denotes the space of functions
f:A\to\mathbb{C}
such that every
x\in A
,
there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
f_{|U\cap A}=g_{|U\cap A}
.
By
1.1.2
, if
A
is closed, then
f\in C^{\infty}(A)
if and only if there exists
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that
f=g_{|A}
.
The condition
A
is locally closed is needed to ensure that
A
is locally compact.
The reader can check that
A
is a differential space.
From now on, any locally closed subset of a differential space is equipped with this differential structure.

Definition 1.1.3
.

i.

A map
f:M\to M^{\prime}
between differential spaces is smooth if for any
g\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})
,
g\circ f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

ii.

A diffeomorphism is a smooth homeomorphism whose inverse is smooth.

iii.

A (closed, open) smooth embedding is a (closed, open) topological embedding which is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

iv.

A smooth map
f:M\to M^{\prime}
is called a local diffeomorphism at
x\in M
, if there exists an open neighborhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
such that
f_{|U}:U\to M^{\prime}
is an open smooth embedding.

By
Section

1.1.1
.
i
, a smooth map is automatically continuous.

Remark 1.1.4
.

Since a subspace
A
of a locally compact space is locally compact if and only if
A
is locally closed, it follows that the image of a topological embedding between differential spaces is locally closed.
Hence, the image inherits a differential structure. This justifies
Section

1.1.3
.
iii
.

A differential space is a smooth manifold if for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
M
is locally diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 1.1.5
.

Let
M
be a differential space. The following conditions are equivalent:

i.

There exists a closed smooth embedding
M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

ii.

There exists a finite number
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, there exists
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
such that
f=F(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})
.

iii.

There exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
x\in M
, one can find an open neighborhood
U\subseteq M
of
x
,
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, there exists
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
such that
f_{|U}=F(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})
.

We say that
M
is a finite-dimensional differential space if it satisfies the above conditions.
We say that
M
is locally finite-dimensional
2
2

2

Sometimes called sub-Cartesian space in the literature.
if every
x\in M
has an open neighborhood which is finite dimensional.

Proof.

The implications
\ref{qsljdqdslkqjskldj1}\Rightarrow\ref{qsljdqdslkqjskldj2}\Rightarrow\ref{prop:Whitney_full:3}
are trivial.
Our proof of
\ref{prop:Whitney_full:3}\Rightarrow\ref{qsljdqdslkqjskldj1}
differs from Whitney’s proof.
Classical proof of Whitney’s theorem is based on Sard’s theorem.
We base ours on Lebesgue’s covering dimension.
In fact, our proof is a variant of the proof of the topological Whitney embedding theorem, see
[
39
, Theorem 50.5]
.
Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
for which the third condition is satisfied.
By replacing the
f_{1},\cdots,f_{N}
with their real and imaginary parts, we can suppose (after doubling
N
) that
f_{1},\cdots,f_{N}
are real-valued.
We fix a proper smooth map
\rho:M\to\mathbb{R}
.
For example, if
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is as in
1.1.2
associated to the trivial open cover, then
\rho=\sum_{n}nf_{n}
is proper smooth.

Lemma 1.1.6
.

For any
x\in M
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
, and a smooth embedding
U\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Proof.

Let
U
and
f_{1},\cdots,f_{N}\in C^{\infty}(U)
as in Condition
3
.
We define

\displaystyle\phi:U\to\mathbb{R}^{N},\quad\phi(y)=(f_{1}(y),\cdots,f_{N}(y)).

The map
\phi
is a smooth embedding when restricted to any compact neighborhood
V\subseteq U
of
x
.
∎

1.1.6
together with
[
23
, Theorem 3.1.14 and Corollary 3.1.20]
imply that
M
has Lebesgue covering dimension
\leq N
.
By
1.1.6
and
[
23
, Proposition 3.2.2.b]
, we can find a locally finite countable open cover
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
M
and smooth embeddings
f_{n}:U_{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that any
N+1
intersection

U_{n_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap U_{n_{N+1}}
is empty.
To be more precise, one starts with any open cover such that its elements admit smooth embeddings into
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then by paracompactness one replaces the cover by a locally finite refinement,
then by Lindelöf one replaces the cover by a countable subcover, then one applies
[
23
, Proposition 3.2.2.b]
.
By modifying
f_{n}
, we can suppose that
\mathrm{Im}(f_{n})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}\backslash 0
.
Let

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{n}:U_{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{N+1}\backslash 0,\quad\tilde{f}_{n}(x)=\left(f_{n}(x),\left\lVert f_{n}(x)\right\rVert^{2}\right).

Notice that
\tilde{f}_{n}
has the property that

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in U_{n},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad\tilde{f}_{n}(x)=\lambda\tilde{f}_{n}(y)\implies x=y

(1.1)

Since
M
is normal, we can find an open cover
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\overline{V_{n}}\subseteq U_{n}
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by
1.1.2
, there exists
\xi_{n}:M\to[0,1]
a smooth function such that
\xi_{n}=1
on
V_{n}
and
\supp(\xi_{n})\subseteq U_{n}
.
Let

g_{n}=\xi_{n}\tilde{f}_{n}:M\to\mathbb{R}^{N+1}

extended outside
U_{n}
by
0
which is smooth by
Section

1.1.1
.
iii
.
By (
1.1
),

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in M,n\in\mathbb{N},\quad g_{n}(x)=g_{n}(y)\text{ and }g_{n}(x)\neq 0\implies x=y

(1.2)

Since
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a locally finite cover, it follows that the sums

\displaystyle h_{l}:=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}n^{l}g_{n}:M\to\mathbb{R}^{N+1},\quad l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

are locally finite, and thus smooth.
We can now define

\displaystyle\phi:M\to\mathbb{R}^{2N^{2}+2N+1},\quad\phi(x)=(h_{0}(x),\cdots,h_{2N-1}(x),\rho(x)).

We will show that
\phi
is a closed smooth embedding.
For every
x\in M
, let
S_{x}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:x\in U_{n}\}
.
By our assumptions on
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
|S_{x}|\leq N
.
Let

\displaystyle W_{x}=\bigcap_{n\in S_{x}}U_{n}\cap\bigcap_{n\notin S_{x}}\supp(g_{n})^{c}.

The set
W_{x}
is open because
(U_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a locally finite cover.
To see this, let
U\subseteq M
be an open set such that
|\{n\in\mathbb{N}:U\cap U_{n}\neq\emptyset\}|<\infty
.
Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
U\cap U_{n}=\emptyset
, we have
U\cap\supp(g_{n})^{c}=U
.
Hence,

\displaystyle U\cap W_{x}=\bigcap_{n\in S_{x}}(U\cap U_{n})\cap\bigcap_{\{n\in S_{x}^{c}:U\cap U_{n}\neq\emptyset\}}(U\cap\supp(g_{n})^{c})

This is a finite intersection.
Hence, we have proved that
W_{x}
is locally open.
So
W_{x}
is open.
By the construction of
W_{x}
,
x\in W_{x}
and
g_{n|W_{x}}=0
for all
n\notin S_{x}
.

We can now show that
\phi
is injective. Let
x,y\in M
with
\phi(x)=\phi(y)
.
We have

\displaystyle n\notin(S_{x}\cup S_{y})\implies g_{n|W_{x}\cup W_{y}}=0.

(1.3)

If
s_{1},\cdots,s_{k}
are the elements of
S_{x}\cup S_{y}
, then
using the identity

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}h_{0}(z)\\
h_{1}(z)\\
\vdots\\
h_{k-1}(z)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&1\\
s_{1}&\cdots&s_{k}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
s_{1}^{k-1}&\cdots&s_{k}^{k-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g_{s_{1}}(z)\\
g_{s_{2}}(z)\\
\vdots\\
g_{s_{k}}(z)\end{pmatrix}\quad\forall z\in W_{x}\cup W_{y},

we can find an invertible linear map
L
such that

\displaystyle L\circ\phi=(g_{s_{1}},\cdots,g_{s_{k}},h_{k},\cdots,h_{2N-1},\rho)\quad\text{on }W_{x}\cup W_{y}.

(1.4)

Since
x,y\in W_{x}\cup W_{y}
, by (
1.4
), we deduce that

g_{n}(x)=g_{n}(y)
for all
n\in S_{x}\cup S_{y}
.
Since
x
belongs to
V_{n}\subseteq U_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that
g_{n}(x)=g_{n}(y)
and
g_{n}(x)\neq 0
.
By (
1.2
),
x=y
.

Since
\rho
is proper, it follows that
\phi
is a closed topological embedding.
It remains to show that
\phi^{-1}
is smooth.
On
V_{n}\cap W_{x}
,
g_{n}=\tilde{f_{n}}
, so by (
1.4
), the inverse of
\phi
on
V_{n}\cap W_{x}
is given by
f_{n}^{-1}\circ\pi\circ L
where
\pi
is the projection onto the coordinate with
f_{n}
.
This is smooth because
f_{n}
is a smooth embedding.
∎

From now on, all differential spaces are supposed locally finite dimensional.

Product:

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be differential spaces.
We equip
M_{1}\times M_{2}
with the differential structure generated by
\{(x,y)\mapsto f(x):f\in C^{\infty}(M_{1})\}\cup\{(x,y)\mapsto g(y):g\in C^{\infty}(M_{2})\}
.
If
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are smooth manifolds, then
C^{\infty}(M_{1}\times M_{2})
agrees with the usual
C^{\infty}(M_{1}\times M_{2})
.

Disjoint union:

If
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are differential spaces, then
M_{1}\sqcup M_{2}
is a differential space with
C^{\infty}(M_{1}\sqcup M_{2})=\{f\in C(M_{1}\sqcup M_{2}):f_{|M_{1}}\in C^{\infty}(M_{1}),f_{|M_{2}}\in C^{\infty}(M_{2})\}
.

Tangent vectors, tangent bundle, and vector fields:

Let
M
be a differential space.
A tangent vector of
M
at
x\in M
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
X:C^{\infty}(M)\to\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle X(fg)=X(f)g(x)+f(x)X(g),\quad X(\overline{f})=\overline{X(f)},\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(M).

The space of all tangent vectors is denoted by
T_{x}M
.
By a standard trick using Taylor’s formula, see
[
32
, Proof of Lemma 7.1.4]
, if
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, then

\displaystyle X(F(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}(f_{1}(x),\cdots,f_{n}(x))X(f_{i}).

(1.5)

By the same trick,

\displaystyle\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M),\quad x\notin\supp(f)\implies X(f)(x)=0.

(1.6)

By (
1.5
), (
1.6
) and since
M
is locally finite dimensional,
T_{x}M
is a finite-dimensional real vector space.
We define the tangent bundle by

\displaystyle TM:=\{(X,x):x\in M,X\in T_{x}M\}.

Let
\pi:TM\to M
be the natural projection.
If
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then we define

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f:TM\to\mathbb{C},\quad\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f(X,x)=X(f).

We equip
TM
with the weakest topology such that
\pi:TM\to M
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f
are continuous for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
Let
C^{\infty}(TM)
be the differential structure generated by
\{f\circ\pi,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f:f\in C^{\infty}(M)\}
.
One can show that
TM
is a locally finitely generated differential space, see
[
49
, Section 3.3]
.
A smooth map
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
induces for every
x\in M_{1}
, a linear map

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f_{x}:T_{x}M_{1}\to T_{f(x)}M_{2},\quad\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f_{x}(X)(g)=X(g\circ f),\quad\forall g\in C^{\infty}(M_{2}),

and a smooth map
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f:TM_{1}\to TM_{2}
.

If
M_{1},M_{2}
are differential spaces, then
T(M_{1}\times M_{2})
is naturally diffeomorphic to
TM_{1}\times TM_{2}
.

A vector field on
M
is a linear map
X:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M)
such that

\displaystyle X(fg)=X(f)g+fX(g),\quad X(\overline{f})=\overline{X(f)},\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(M).

We denote the space of vector fields by
\mathcal{X}(M)
.
Since the commutator of derivations is a derivation,
\mathcal{X}(M)
is a Lie algebra.
By composing
X
by the evaluation map at every point
x\in M
, one obtains a section
M\to TM
.
This defines a bijection between vector fields and smooth sections
M\to TM
, see
[
49
, Proposition 3.3.5]
.

Example 1.1.7
.

Let
M=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:xy=0\}
.
One can check that
\dim(T_{(x,y)}M)=1
if
(x,y)\neq 0
and
\dim(T_{0}M)=2
.
A vector field on
M
has the form
X=f(x,y)x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+g(x,y)y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
where
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
It follows that if
X
is a vector field, then
X
at
(0,0)
vanishes
0
.
In particular,
\{X(x):X\in\mathcal{X}(M)\}
is usually a strict subspace of
T_{x}M
.

Definition 1.1.8

(Vector bundles)
.

A real vector bundle on a differential space
M
is a differential space
E
, a smooth map
\pi:E\to M
, a real finite dimensional vector space structure on
E_{x}:=\pi^{-1}(x)
for each
x\in M
such that for every
x\in M
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, a diffeomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times U\to\pi^{-1}(U)
such that:

•

For all
(v,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times U
,
\pi(\phi(v,x))=x
.

•

For all
x\in U
,
\phi(\cdot,x):\mathbb{R}^{k}\to E_{x}
is a linear isomorphism.

Complex vector bundles are defined analogously.
We denote by
C^{\infty}(M,E)
the space of smooth sections. We denote by
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,E)
the subspace of compactly supported smooth section.

Classical constructions like direct sum, tensor product, pullback extend immediately to vector bundles over differential spaces.
The space
TM
is generally not a real vector bundle because
\dim(T_{x}M)
generally depends on
x
.
One can show that if
\dim(T_{x}M)
is locally constant, then
TM
is a real vector bundle, see
[
49
, Proposition 3.3.15]
.

Proposition 1.1.9
.

If
E\to M
is a real vector bundle over a finite dimensional differential space
M
such that
x\in M\mapsto\dim(E_{x})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is bounded, then
C^{\infty}(M,E)
is a finitely generated
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
-module.

Proof.

The total space of
E^{*}
satisfies
Theorem

1.1.5
.
iii
.
So, by
Theorem

1.1.5
, there exists a closed smooth embedding
\phi:E^{*}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n
big enough.
The differential of
\phi
restricted to
M
gives a smooth bundle embedding of
E^{*}
into the trivial vector bundle
M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
By taking the adjoint, one obtains generators of
C^{\infty}(M,E)
.
∎

Submersions:

We say that a smooth map
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
between differential spaces is a submersion at
x\in M_{1}
if there exists a smooth map
g:M_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

such that

\displaystyle M_{1}\to M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad y\mapsto(f(y),g(y))

is a local diffeomorphism at
x\in M_{1}
, i.e.,
f
is locally a projection whose fiber is an open subset of a Euclidean space.
The map
f
is called a submersion if it is a submersion at every
x\in M_{1}
.

If
f
is a submersion,
x\in M_{1}
, then
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f_{x}:T_{x}M_{1}\to T_{f(x)}M_{2}
is surjective, and for every
y\in M_{2}
,
f^{-1}(y)
is a smooth manifold whose tangent space at
x\in f^{-1}(y)
is equal to
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f_{x})
.
Moreover,
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f)
, equipped with the subspace topology and differential structure from
TM_{1}
, is a real vector bundle over
M_{1}
.
The composition of two submersions is again a submersion.

Remark 1.1.10
.

If
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
is a vector field, then its flow at time
t
starting from
x\in M
is denoted by
e^{tX}\cdot x
.
In general the flow might not be well-defined for any
t\neq 0
.
But if
f:M\to M^{\prime}
is a submersion,
X\in C^{\infty}(M,\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f))
, then the flow exists in a neighborhood of
(x,0)
in
M\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 1.1.11
.

Let
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
and
g:M_{2}\to M_{3}
be smooth maps between differential spaces.
If
f
and
g\circ f
are submersions, and
f
is surjective, then
g
is a submersion.

Proof.

Let
x_{0}\in M_{2}
. By hypothesis, there exists
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
h:M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
a smooth map such that
h(x_{0},0)=0
and the map

\displaystyle\phi:M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M_{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\quad\phi(x,y)=(g(x),h(x,y))

is a local diffeomorphism at
(x_{0},0)
. So, its differential at
(x_{0},0)
is a linear isomorphism which restricts to a linear isomorphism
L:\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g_{x_{0}})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Let
\pi:M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M_{2}
be the natural projection,
\psi
the locally defined map

\displaystyle\psi:M_{3}\times\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g_{x_{0}})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M_{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\quad\psi(x,a,b)=\phi\Big(\pi\left(\phi^{-1}(x,L(a,0))\right),b\Big)

The map
\psi
is of the form
\psi(x,a,b)=(x,\kappa(x,a,b))
for some smooth map
\kappa
. Furthermore,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\kappa_{(g(x_{0}),0,0)}(0,a,b)=L(a,b)
.
The parameterized inverse mapping theorem (where
M_{3}
is seen as a parameter space) implies that
\psi
is a local diffeomorphism at
(g(x_{0}),0,0)
.
So, the map

\displaystyle M_{3}\times\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g_{x_{0}})\to M_{2},\quad(x,a)\mapsto\pi(\phi^{-1}(x,L(a,0)))

is a local diffeomorphism at
(g(x_{0}),0)
whose inverse is a map of the form
x\mapsto(g(x),\varphi(x))
for some smooth map
\varphi:M_{2}\to\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g_{x_{0}})
. The result follows.
∎

By taking
M_{3}=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
,
1.1.11
implies that, if
M
is a differential space such that for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth manifold, then
M
is a smooth manifold.
The following theorem is a variant of Bourbaki’s quotient theorem
[
8
, Section 5.9.5]
.

Quotient space:

Theorem 1.1.12
.

Let
M
be a differential space,
R\subseteq M\times M
an equivalence relation,
\pi:M\to M/R
the quotient map,
C^{\infty}(M/R)=\{f:M/R\to\mathbb{C}:f\circ\pi\in C^{\infty}(M/R)\}
.
Suppose the following holds:

i.

The set
R
is a closed subset of
M\times M
.

ii.

The natural projection
(x,y)\in R\mapsto x\in M
is a submersion.

Then,
M/R
equipped with the quotient topology is Hausdorff locally compact and second countable, and
C^{\infty}(M/R)
is a locally finite dimensional differential structure on
M/R
, and
\pi
is a submersion.

Proof.

Let
U\subseteq M
be an open subset,
x_{0}\in\pi^{-1}(\pi(U))
.
So,
(x_{0},y_{0})\in R
for some
y_{0}\in U
.
Since
(x,y)\in R\mapsto x\in M
is a submersion and a submersion admits local sections, there exists a locally defined smooth map
s:M\to M
such that
s(x_{0})=y_{0}
and
(x,s(x))\in R
for all
x\in\dom(s)
.
Hence,
s^{-1}(U)
is a neighborhood of
x_{0}
which is contained in
\pi^{-1}(\pi(U))
.
So,
\pi
is open.
Hence,
M/R
is Hausdorff because
R
is closed. It also follows that
M/R
is second countable and locally compact.

We now show that
C^{\infty}(M/R)
is a differential structure on
M/R
.
If
U\subseteq M
is an open set,
f:\pi(U)\to\mathbb{C}
is a function, then since
f\circ\pi\circ s=f\circ\pi
for any section
s
as above, we deduce that

\displaystyle f\circ\pi\in C^{\infty}(U)\iff f\circ\pi\in C^{\infty}(\pi^{-1}(\pi(U))).

(1.7)

It is easy to see that
Section

1.1.1
.
ii
and
Section

1.1.1
.
iii
hold.
So we need to prove
Section

1.1.1
.
i
.
Let
x_{0}\in M
be fixed.
Since
(x,y)\in R\mapsto x\in M
is a submersion at
(x_{0},x_{0})
, there exists
g:R\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
a smooth map such that
g(x_{0},x_{0})=0
and the map

\displaystyle\psi:R\to M\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad(x,y)\mapsto(x,g(x,y))

is a local diffeomorphism at
(x_{0},x_{0})
.
Since
R
is closed, we can extend
g
to a smooth map on
M\times M
.
Let
g^{\prime}(y)=g(x_{0},y)
.
We claim that the map

\displaystyle\phi:R\to M\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad(x,y)\mapsto(x,g^{\prime}(y))

is a local diffeomorphism at
(x_{0},x_{0})
. To see this, notice that the map
\phi\circ\psi^{-1}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is of the form
(x,t)\mapsto(x,k(x,t))
for some smooth map
k
which satisfies
k(x_{0},t)=t
.
The parameterized inverse mapping theorem (with
M
as a parameter space) implies that
\phi\circ\psi^{-1}
is a local diffeomorphism at
(x_{0},0)
.
Hence,
\phi
is a local diffeomorphism at
(x_{0},x_{0})
.

Let
V
be an open neighborhood of
x_{0}
such that
\phi
is an open embedding on
(V\times V)\cap R
,
U
the open neighborhood of
x_{0}
defined by

\displaystyle U=\{y\in V:(y,0)\in\phi((V\times V)\cap R)\}.

The set
\pi(V\cap g^{\prime-1}(0))
is an open neighborhood of
\pi(x_{0})
because

\pi(V\cap g^{\prime-1}(0))=\pi(U)
.
The map
\pi
is injective on
V\cap g^{\prime-1}(0)
because
\phi
is injective on
(V\times V)\cap R
.
Let
h:\pi(V\cap g^{\prime-1}(0))\to M
be the inverse of
\pi
on
V\cap g^{\prime-1}(0)
.
On
U
,
h\circ\pi
is simply defined by
\phi(y,h\circ\pi(y))=(y,0)
.
So, the map
h\circ\pi
is smooth on
U
.
By (
1.7
),
h\circ\pi\in C^{\infty}(\pi^{-1}(\pi(U)))
.
Now, suppose that
x_{1}\in M
such that
(x_{0},x_{1})\notin R
.
We replace
U
by a neighborhood of
x_{0}
small enough so that
x_{1}\notin\pi^{-1}(\pi(U))
which is possible because
\pi
is open and
R
is closed.
Since
h
is injective by replacing
U
with a smaller relatively compact neighborhood, we can suppose that
h
is a homeomorphism onto its image.
So,
h(U)
is locally closed, i.e.,
h(U)=A\cap B
, where
A\subseteq M
is open and
B\subseteq M
is closed.
Let
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
such that
g(x_{0})=1
and
\supp(g)\subseteq A
.
So,
\supp(g)\cap h(U)=\supp(g)\cap B
is compact.
Since
h
is a homeomorphism onto its image,
g\circ h
is compactly supported.
We extend
g\circ h
to
M/R
by
0
outside
\pi(U)
.
By
Section

1.1.1
.
iii
,
g\circ h\circ\pi\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
So,
g\circ h\in C^{\infty}(M/R)
.
Notice that
g\circ h(\pi(x_{0}))=1
and
g\circ h(\pi(x_{1}))=0
.
Thus,
C^{\infty}(M/R)
satisfies
Section

1.1.1
.
i
.

We now show that
\pi
is a submersion at
x_{0}
.
We claim that the map

\displaystyle M\to M/R\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad x\mapsto(\pi(x),g^{\prime}(x))

is a local diffeomorphism at
x_{0}
. In fact a local inverse is the map

\displaystyle M/R\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M,\quad(z,t)\mapsto y,\quad\text{where }(h(z),y)=\phi^{-1}(h(z),t)

Finally, since
M
is locally finite dimensional,
M/R
is locally finite dimensional.
∎

Fréchet topology:

Let
M
be a differential space,
K\subseteq M
a compact subset.
Since
K
is compact and
M
is locally finite dimensional,
K
is finite dimensional.
Let
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}(K)
such that for every
f\in C^{\infty}(K)
,
f=F(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})
for some
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
We can define the semi-norm

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{m},K}=\inf\{\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{C^{m}(\mathbb{C}^{n})}:f=F(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})\},\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}_{.}

(1.8)

It is clear that
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C^{m},K}
up to equivalence doesn’t depend on the choice of
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}
. We equip
C^{\infty}(M)
with the topology generated by
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C^{m},K}
as
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
K\subseteq M
vary.
By a standard diagonalization argument,
C^{\infty}(M)
is complete, hence a Fréchet space.
We equip
C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
with the strongest topology that makes the maps
C^{\infty}(K)\to C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
continuous for any compact subset
K
of
M
.
This makes
C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
an LF space (limit of Fréchet spaces).
If
E\to M
is a vector bundle, then we define similarly topologies on
C^{\infty}(M,E)
and
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,E)
.

Densities:

If
E\to M
is a real vector bundle on a differential space
M
and
k\in\mathbb{C}
, then
\Omega^{k}(E)
denotes the vector bundle of
k
-densities of
E
.
Its fiber over
x\in M
is the
1
-dimensional complex vector space of all functions
\phi:\Lambda^{\mathrm{rank}(E_{x})}E_{x}\backslash 0\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\phi(\lambda v)=|\lambda|^{k}\phi(v)
for all
v\in\Lambda^{\mathrm{rank}(E_{x})}E\backslash 0
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.
The following holds:

•

If
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{k}(E))
, then
|\phi|\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\Re(k)}(E))
.

•

If
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{k}(E)),\psi\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{l}(E))
, then
\phi\psi\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{k+l}(E))
.

•

If
k\in\mathbb{R}
, then we say that
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{k}(E))
is positive if for all
x\in M
,
\im(\phi_{x})\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

The proof of the following classical proposition is left to the reader.

Proposition 1.1.13
.

i.

If
E\to M
is a real vector bundle,
k,l\in\mathbb{C}
, then there are natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\Omega^{k+l}(E)\simeq\Omega^{k}(E)\otimes\Omega^{l}(E),\quad\Omega^{k}(E^{*})\simeq\Omega^{k}(E)^{*}\simeq\Omega^{-k}(E),\quad\overline{\Omega^{k}(E)}\simeq\Omega^{\overline{k}}(E)

(1.9)

ii.

If
0\to E\to F\to G\to 0
is a short exact sequence of real vector bundles,
k\in\mathbb{C}
, then there is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\Omega^{k}(F)\simeq\Omega^{k}(E)\otimes\Omega^{k}(G).

(1.10)

Corollary 1.1.14
.

If
X,Y,Z
are differential spaces,
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
are submersions
then for any
k\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(g\circ f)))\simeq\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f))\otimes f^{*}(\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g))).

Proof.

The map
g\circ f
is a submersion because
f,g
are submersions.
Since
f,g,g\circ f
are submersions,
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f),\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g),\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(g\circ f))
are real vector bundles.
The corollary follows
Section

1.1.13
.
ii
applied to the short exact sequence

0\to\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f)\to\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(g\circ f))\to f^{*}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g))\to 0\qed

Integration along fibers:

If
M
is a differential space,
f\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that the natural projection
\supp(f)\to M
is proper, then
x\mapsto\int f(x,y)dy\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
This follows easily from
Theorem

1.1.5
(or directly from
1.1.1
).

Let
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
be a submersion between differential spaces,
E
a vector bundle on
M_{2}
.
Thanks to
1.1.2
, we can define an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle f_{*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(M_{1},\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f))\otimes f^{*}(E))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(M_{2},E)

(1.11)

The map
f_{*}
is continuous with respect to the
LF
-topologies defined above.

Let
X\in C^{\infty}(M_{1},\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f))
. For any
k\in\mathbb{C}
, we define the Lie derivative

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}:C^{\infty}(M_{1},\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f))\otimes f^{*}(E))\to C^{\infty}(M_{1},\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f))\otimes f^{*}(E))

(1.12)

as follows: Locally,
M_{1}=M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f
is the projection map.
So, if
\mathrm{vol}^{k}:\Lambda^{n}\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash 0\to\mathbb{C}
denotes the canonical element which maps
e_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{n}
to
1
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}(g\mathrm{vol}^{k}\otimes s)=X(g)\mathrm{vol}^{k}\otimes s+kg\mathrm{div}(X)\mathrm{vol}^{k}\otimes s,\quad g\in C^{\infty}(M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),s\in C^{\infty}(M_{2},E),

where
\mathrm{div}(X)\in C^{\infty}(M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the divergence of
X
, i.e., if
X=\sum X_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}
with
X_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\mathrm{div}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial t_{i}}
.

Pullback of smooth maps:

We say that a diagram of smooth maps between differential spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{M}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

is a pullback diagram if it commutes and the map
(\pi_{1},f):M_{1}\to\tilde{M}_{1}\times_{\tilde{f},\pi_{2}}M_{2}
is a diffeomorphism.

Proposition 1.1.15
.

In a pullback diagram, if
\tilde{f}
is a submersion, then
f
is also a submersion and the map
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pi_{1}:\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f)\to\pi_{1}^{*}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{f}))
is an isomorphism.
Furthermore, if
E\to\tilde{M}_{2}
is a vector bundle, then

\displaystyle f_{*}(h\circ\pi_{1})=\tilde{f}_{*}(h)\circ\pi_{2},\quad\forall h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\tilde{M_{1}},\tilde{f}^{*}(E)\otimes\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{f})),

(1.13)

where
f_{*}(h\circ\pi_{1})
is well-defined because
f_{|\supp(h\circ\pi_{1})}:\supp(h\circ\pi_{1})\to M_{2}
is proper.

Proof.

Since
\tilde{f}
is a submersion, we can suppose that
\tilde{M}_{1}=\tilde{M}_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\tilde{f}
is the natural projection.
The proposition is now straightforward to prove.
∎

1.2
Quasi-Lie groupoid

A groupoid is a small category where all morphisms are invertible. We denote by

•

G
the set of morphisms.

•

G^{(0)}
the set of objects.

•

s,r:G\to G^{(0)}
the maps sending a morphism to its domain and range respectively.

•

\iota:G\to G
the inverse map. Generally, the inverse of
\gamma\in G
is denoted by
\gamma^{-1}
.

•

u:G^{(0)}\to G
the identity map. Generally, we will identify
G^{(0)}
with
u(G^{(0)})
.

•

G^{(2)}:=\{(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)\in G\times G:s(\gamma^{\prime})=r(\gamma)\}
the set of composable pairs.

•

m:G^{(2)}\to G
the composition map. Generally, we use
\gamma^{\prime}\gamma
instead of
m(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)
.

We use
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
to denote a groupoid. If
A,B\subseteq G^{(0)}
are subsets, then

\displaystyle G_{A}:=s^{-1}(A),\quad G^{B}:=r^{-1}(B),\quad G_{A}^{B}:=G_{A}\cap G^{B}.

If
A
or
B
are singletons
\{x\}
and
\{y\}
, then we use
G_{x}
,
G^{x}
and
G_{x}^{y}
for simplicity.
We also define

\displaystyle AB:=\{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}:(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in G^{(2)}\cap(A\times B)\},\quad A^{-1}:=\{\gamma^{-1}:\gamma\in A\},\quad\forall A,B\subseteq G.

Examples 1.2.1
.

i.

The trivial groupoid over a set
X
is the unique groupoid where
G=G^{(0)}=G^{(2)}=X
, and all the structure maps are the identity.

ii.

The pair groupoid over a set
X
is the unique category where
G^{(0)}=X
and for every
x,y
, there exists a unique morphism from
x
to
y
.
So,
G=X\times X
and the structure maps are

\displaystyle s(y,x)=x,\quad r(y,x)=y,\quad\iota(y,x)=(x,y),\quad u(x)=(x,x),\quad m((z,y),(y,x))=(z,x).

More generally, if

R\subseteq X\times X
is an equivalence relation, then
R\rightrightarrows X
is a groupoid where the structure maps are the restriction of the structure maps from
X\times X\rightrightarrows X
.

iii.

If
G^{(0)}=\{\bullet\}
, then a groupoid is just a group. More generally, if
s=r
, then a groupoid is a bundle of groups over
G^{(0)}
.

iv.

Let
\Gamma
be a group,
S
a set of subgroups of
\Gamma
which is closed under conjugation.
We define the groupoid

\displaystyle G=\{\gamma H\eta:\gamma,\eta\in\Gamma,H\in S\},\quad G^{(0)}=S

Notice that since
S
is closed under conjugation,

\displaystyle\gamma H\eta=\gamma\eta(\eta^{-1}H\eta)=(\gamma H\gamma^{-1})\gamma\eta

is a left coset of
\eta^{-1}H\eta\in S
and a right coset of
\gamma H\gamma^{-1}\in S
.
The structure maps are

\displaystyle s(A)=A^{-1}A,\quad r(A)=AA^{-1},\quad u(H)=H,\quad\iota(A)=A^{-1},\quad m(B,A)=BA

Notice that if
A=\gamma H\eta
, then
s(A)=\eta^{-1}H\eta
is an element of
S
.
Similar computation with
r
and
m
.
If
S=\{H\}
where
H
is a normal subgroup, then
G=\Gamma/H
is the quotient of
\Gamma
by
H
.
In general, we call the groupoid
G
,
the quotient of
\Gamma
by
S
.

This groupoid plays a fundamental role in this article.
It has also been used in a different context by Bradd, Higson, Yuncken, see
[
9
,
10
]
.

Definition 1.2.2
.

A
quasi-Lie groupoid
is a groupoid
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
together with a Hausdorff locally compact second countable topology and locally finite dimensional differential structure on
G
and on
G^{(0)}
such that

i.

The maps
u
,
r
,
s
,
\iota
and
m
are smooth where
G^{(2)}
is equipped with the differential structure inherited from
G\times G
.

ii.

The source map
s:G\to G^{(0)}
is a submersion.

Remark 1.2.3
.

If
G^{(0)}
is a smooth manifold, then
G
is also a smooth manifold because
s
is a submersion.
In this case,
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
is called a Lie groupoid.

Proposition 1.2.4
.

i.

The identity map
u:G^{(0)}\to G
is a closed smooth embedding.

ii.

The map
\iota:G\to G
is a diffeomorphism.

iii.

The maps
m:G^{(2)}\to G
and
r:G\to G^{(0)}
are submersions.

Proof.

i.

The map
u
is a smooth embedding because
s\circ u=\mathrm{Id}
.
It is closed because if
u(x_{n})\to\gamma\in G
, then
u(x_{n})u(x_{n})^{-1}\to\gamma\gamma^{-1}
.
But since
G
is Hausdorff, and
u(x_{n})u(x_{n})^{-1}=u(x_{n})
, it follows that
\gamma=\gamma\gamma^{-1}\in u(G^{(0)})
.

ii.

The map
\iota
is its own inverse.

iii.

Since
r=s\circ\iota
, it follows that
r
is a submersion.
Let
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in G^{(2)}
. Since
s
is a submersion at
\gamma_{1}
,
we can find
g:G\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\phi:x\in G\mapsto(s(x),g(x))\in G^{(0)}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a local diffeomorphism at
\gamma_{1}
.
The map

\displaystyle G^{(2)}\to G\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\mapsto(\gamma\gamma^{\prime},g(\gamma^{\prime}))

is a local diffeomorphism at
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
.
A local inverse is the map

\displaystyle G\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to G^{(2)},\quad(\gamma,l)\mapsto(\gamma\phi^{-1}(s(\gamma),l)^{-1},\phi^{-1}(s(\gamma),l)).

Hence,
m
is a submersion.∎

Morphisms of quasi-Lie groupoids:

Let
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
and
H\rightrightarrows H^{(0)}
be quasi-Lie groupoids.
A morphism of quasi-Lie groupoids is a smooth map
f:H\to G
which is also a functor of categories, i.e., if
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in H^{(2)}
, then
(f(\gamma),f(\gamma^{\prime}))\in G^{(2)}
and
f(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})=f(\gamma)f(\gamma^{\prime})
.

Quasi-Lie subgroupoid

If
H\subseteq G
, and the inclusion is a morphism of quasi-Lie groupoids, then we say that
H\rightrightarrows H^{(0)}
is a quasi-Lie subgroupoid of
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
.

Saturated subset:

A locally closed subset
A
of
G^{(0)}
is called saturated if
G_{A}=G^{A}
. In this case,
G_{A}\rightrightarrows A
is a quasi-Lie subgroupoid of
G
.

Pullback:

If
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
is a quasi-Lie groupoid,
f:X\to G^{(0)}
is a smooth map, then we can define the pullback groupoid
f^{*}(G)\rightrightarrows X
by

\displaystyle f^{*}(G)=\left\{(y,\gamma,x)\in X\times G\times X:\gamma\in G_{f(x)}^{f(y)}\right\},\quad r(y,\gamma,x)=y,\quad s(y,\gamma,x)=x

\displaystyle u(x)=(x,f(x),x),\quad(y,\gamma,x)^{-1}=(x,\gamma^{-1},y),\quad(z,\gamma,y)\cdot(y,\eta,x)=(z,\gamma\eta,x).

Action of a groupoid on a space:

Let
X
be a differential space.
A smooth action of
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
on
X
consists of smooth maps
\sigma:X\to G^{(0)}
and
(\gamma,x)\in G\times_{s,\sigma}X\mapsto\gamma\cdot x\in X
which satisfy

\displaystyle\sigma(\gamma\cdot x)=r(\gamma),\quad\eta\cdot(\gamma\cdot x)=(\eta\gamma)\cdot x,\quad\sigma(x)\cdot x=x,\quad\forall x\in X,\gamma\in G_{\sigma(x)},\eta\in G_{r(\gamma)}.

Given such a smooth action, one can define the quasi-Lie groupoid
G\ltimes X\rightrightarrows X
as

\displaystyle G\ltimes X=\{(y,\gamma,x)\in X\times G\times X:\sigma(x)=s(\gamma),y=\gamma\cdot x\}\subseteq\sigma^{*}(G)

(1.14)

equipped with the subspace topology, sub-differential structure, and subgroupoid structure.

Proper subset:

A subset
A\subseteq G
is called proper if the maps
s_{|A},r_{|A}:A\to G^{(0)}
are proper maps.
A proper subset is automatically closed.
If
E\to G
is a vector bundle, then
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,E)
denotes the space of properly supported sections.

1.3
Lie algebroid

Definition 1.3.1
.

Let
M
be a differential space. A Lie algebroid over
M
is a real vector bundle
A\to M
together with a family of
\mathbb{R}
-linear maps
\rho_{x}:A_{x}\to T_{x}M
for
x\in M

and a Lie algebra structure on
C^{\infty}(M,A)
such that

\displaystyle=f[X,Y]+\rho(X)(f)Y,

(1.15)

where
\rho:A\to TM
is the map induced from the family
(\rho_{x})_{x\in M}
which is called the anchor map.

By (
1.15
),
\rho
is a smooth map.
By looking at
[[X,Y],fZ]
and using (
1.15
), one deduces that

\displaystyle\rho([X,Y])=[\rho(X),\rho(Y)],\quad\forall X,Y\in C^{\infty}(M,A)

(1.16)

Example 1.3.2
.

If
f:M\to M^{\prime}
is a submersion, then
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f)
is a Lie algebroid, where
\rho:\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f)\to TM
is the inclusion.

Lie algebroid of a quasi-Lie groupoid:

Let
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
be a quasi-Lie groupoid.
The restriction of the vector bundle
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)
to
G^{(0)}
is called the
Lie algebroid
of
G
and is denoted by
A(G)
.

3
3

3

One can define
A(G)
to be
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)_{|G^{(0)}}
.
The choice is a matter of convention.
It should be consistent with the choice of the Lie algebra of a Lie group as that of right or left invariant vector fields.
Our convention identifies the Lie algebra of a Lie group with right invariant vector fields
which implies a sign in the BCH formula, see (
2.2
).

The anchor map
\rho:A(G)\to TG^{(0)}
is the restriction of
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
to
A(G^{0})
. We will now define a Lie algebra structure on
C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
.
For any
\gamma\in G
, let

\displaystyle R_{\gamma}:G_{r(\gamma)}\to G_{s(\gamma)},\ R_{\gamma}(\gamma^{\prime})=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma,\quad L_{\gamma}:G^{s(\gamma)}\to G^{r(\gamma)},\ L_{\gamma}(\gamma^{\prime})=\gamma\gamma^{\prime}

The maps
R_{\gamma}
and
L_{\gamma}
diffeomorphisms because their inverses are
R_{\gamma^{-1}}
and
L_{\gamma^{-1}}
.
The differential of
R_{\gamma}
and
L_{\gamma}
induces isomorphisms

\displaystyle(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R_{\gamma})_{\gamma^{\prime}}:\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)_{\gamma^{\prime}}\to\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma},\quad(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L_{\gamma^{\prime}})_{\gamma}:\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)_{\gamma}\to\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma},\quad\forall(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)\in G^{(2)}.

(1.17)

The map
\iota
induces a diffeomorphism from
G^{x}
to
G_{x}
. Hence, its differential gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)\simeq\iota^{*}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)).

(1.18)

The isomorphisms (
1.17
) and (
1.18
) induce isomorphisms

\displaystyle\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)\simeq r^{*}(A(G)),

\displaystyle\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)_{\gamma}\xrightarrow{(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R_{\gamma^{-1}})_{\gamma}}r^{*}(A(G))_{\gamma}

(1.19)

\displaystyle\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)\simeq s^{*}(A(G)),

\displaystyle\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)_{\gamma}\xrightarrow{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\iota_{s(\gamma)}\circ(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L_{\gamma^{-1}})_{\gamma}}s^{*}(A(G))_{\gamma}.

Proposition 1.3.3
.

Let
X\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
. There exists a unique
X_{R}\in C^{\infty}(G,\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s))
called the right invariant vector field associated to
X
such that:

i.

The restriction of
X_{R}
to
G^{(0)}
is equal to
X
.

ii.

For any
(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma)\in G^{(2)}
,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R_{\gamma}(X_{R}(\gamma^{\prime}))=X_{R}(\gamma^{\prime}\gamma)
, i.e.,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R_{\gamma}\circ X_{R}=X_{R}\circ R_{\gamma}
.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\circ X_{R}=\rho(X)\circ r.

(1.20)

Proof.

The vector field
X_{R}
has to be defined by the formula

X_{R}(\gamma)=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R_{\gamma}(X(r(\gamma)))
.
This proves the uniqueness. It is clear that
X_{R}
satisfies
i
and
ii
.
Since
r\circ R_{\gamma}=r
, (
1.20
) follows.
∎

Proposition 1.3.4
.

There exists a unique Lie bracket on
C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
which satisfies the following identities:

\displaystyle{}_{R}=[X_{R},Y_{R}],\quad\rho([X,Y])=[\rho(X),\rho(Y)],\quad[X,fY]=f[X,Y]+\rho(X)(f)Y

where
X,Y\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
and
f\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)})
.

Proof.

Uniqueness is clear from the first identity. For existence, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 1.3.5
.

If
f:M\to M^{\prime}
is a smooth map,
X,Y\in\mathcal{X}(M)
and
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
such that
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f\circ X=X^{\prime}\circ f
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f\circ Y=Y^{\prime}\circ f
, then

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f\circ[X,Y]=[X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}]\circ f
.

Proof.

Both sides are functions
M\to TM^{\prime}
. Both map an element
(Z,x)
to an element of the form
(Z^{\prime},f(x))
for some
Z^{\prime}\in T_{f(x)}M^{\prime}
.
Therefore, to prove that both sides are equal, it is enough to check that for any
g\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})
, both sides are equal after composing with
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g\circ\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f\circ[X,Y]=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(g\circ f)\circ[X,Y]=[X,Y](g\circ f)

\displaystyle=X(Y(g\circ f))-Y(X(g\circ f))

\displaystyle=X(Y^{\prime}(g)\circ f)-Y(X^{\prime}(g)\circ f)

\displaystyle=X^{\prime}(Y^{\prime}(g))\circ f-Y^{\prime}(X^{\prime}(g))\circ f

\displaystyle=[X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}](g)\circ f=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g\circ[X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}]\circ f.

The result follows.
∎

Let
X,Y\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
.
By
1.3.5
,
[X_{R},Y_{R}]\in C^{\infty}(G,\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s))
.
Let
[X,Y]\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
be the restriction of
[X_{R},Y_{R}]
on
G^{(0)}
.
By
1.3.3
and
1.3.5
,

[X,Y]_{R}=[X_{R},Y_{R}]
.
By taking
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
on both sides and using (
1.20
) and
1.3.5
, we deduce that

\rho([X,Y])=[\rho(X),\rho(Y)]
.
Finally,
it is clear that
(fY)_{R}=f\circ rY_{R}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle{}_{R}=[X_{R},f\circ rY_{R}]=f\circ r[X_{R},Y_{R}]+X_{R}(f\circ r)Y_{R}

\displaystyle=f\circ r[X,Y]_{R}+\rho(X)(f)\circ rY_{R}

\displaystyle=(f[X,Y])_{R}+(\rho(X)(f)Y)_{R}.

Therefore,
[X,fY]=f[X,Y]+\rho(X)(f)Y
.
∎

Let
X\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
. The section

\displaystyle X_{L}:=-\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\iota\circ X_{R}\circ\iota\in C^{\infty}(G,\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r))

(1.21)

is called
the left-invariant vector field associated to
X
.
The choice of the sign in (
1.21
) is to ensure that (
1.26
) holds.
We have

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L_{\gamma}\circ X_{L}=X_{L}\circ L_{\gamma},\quad[X,Y]_{L}=-[X_{L},Y_{L}],\quad[X_{R},Y_{L}]=0,\quad\forall\gamma\in G,X,Y\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G)).

The identities
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R_{\gamma}\circ X_{R}=X_{R}\circ R_{\gamma}
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L_{\gamma}\circ X_{L}=X_{L}\circ L_{\gamma}
imply that whenever the flows are well-defined, we have

\displaystyle m(e^{tX_{R}}\cdot r(\gamma),\gamma)=e^{tX_{R}}\cdot\gamma,\quad

\displaystyle m(\gamma,e^{tX_{L}}\cdot s(\gamma))=e^{tX_{L}}\cdot\gamma.

(1.22)

It follows that if
X
is compactly supported, then
X_{R}
and
X_{L}
are complete.

Example 1.3.6
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold,
G=M\times M\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
the pair groupoid.
In this case,
A(G)=TM
, and our sign convention implies that the Lie bracket agrees with the Lie bracket of vector fields.
If
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
, then
X_{R},X_{L}\in\mathcal{X}(M\times M)
are given by

\displaystyle X_{R}(x,y)=(X(x),0),\quad X_{L}(x,y)=(0,-X(y)).

(1.23)

1.4
Convolution algebra

Let
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
be a quasi-Lie groupoid. For any
k,l\in\mathbb{C}
, we define

\displaystyle\Omega^{k,l}(G):=\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s))\otimes\Omega^{l}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)),\quad\Omega^{k}(G):=\Omega^{k,k}(G).

By (
1.19
),

\displaystyle\Omega^{k,l}(G)\simeq r^{*}(\Omega^{k}(A(G)))\otimes s^{*}(\Omega^{l}(A(G))).

(1.24)

To simplify the notation, we will generally use
C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l})
instead of
C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l}(G))
.

Adjoint:

We define the adjoint of
f\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l})
by

\displaystyle f^{*}(\gamma):=f(\gamma^{-1})^{*}\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{\overline{l},\overline{k}}).

Convolution:

We now define the convolution of two functions on
G
.

Lemma 1.4.1
.

There is a natural isomorphism between the real vector bundles

\displaystyle m^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{1},k_{4}}(G)\right)\otimes\Omega^{k_{2}+k_{3}}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}m))\simeq\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{1},k_{2}}(G)\right)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{3},k_{4}}(G)\right),\quad\forall k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\in\mathbb{C},

where
\pi_{i}:G^{(2)}\to G
are the natural projections, and
m:G^{(2)}\to G
is the multiplication map.

Proof.

Let
l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}:G^{(2)}\to G^{(0)}
be the maps
l_{1}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=r(\gamma)
,
l_{2}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=s(\gamma)
,
l_{3}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=s(\gamma^{\prime})
.
Clearly, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{1},k_{2}}(G)\right)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{3},k_{4}}(G)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq l_{1}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{1}}A(G)\right)\otimes l_{2}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{2}+k_{3}}A(G)\right)\otimes l_{3}^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{4}}A(G)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq m^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{1},k_{4}}(G)\right)\otimes\Omega^{k_{2}+k_{3}}(l_{2}^{*}\left(A(G)\right))

It remains to show that
l_{2}^{*}(A(G))\simeq\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}m)
. For any
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in G^{(2)}
, the map

\displaystyle G_{l_{2}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})}\to m^{-1}(\gamma\gamma^{\prime}),\quad\gamma^{\prime\prime}\mapsto(\gamma\gamma^{\prime\prime-1},\gamma^{\prime\prime}\gamma^{\prime})

is a diffeomorphism whose differential at
l_{2}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})
induces the required isomorphism.
∎

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k_{1},k_{2}})
,
g\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{1-k_{2},k_{3}})
.
By
1.4.1
, we define

\displaystyle f\boxtimes g(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=f(\gamma)g(\gamma^{\prime})\in C^{\infty}\left(G^{(2)},m^{*}\left(\Omega^{k_{1},k_{3}}(G)\right)\otimes\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}m))\right).

If
f
or
g
is properly supported, then
m:\supp(f\boxtimes g)\to G
is proper.
Hence, we can define

\displaystyle f\ast g:=m_{*}(f\boxtimes g)\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k_{1},k_{3}}).

Furthermore, if
f
and
g
are properly supported, then
f\boxtimes g
is also properly supported.
The main case of interest to us is when
k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}=\frac{1}{2}
.

Proposition 1.4.2
.

The space
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a
*
-algebra, and

\displaystyle\supp(f\ast g)\subseteq\supp(f)\supp(g),\quad\supp(f^{*})=\supp(f)^{-1},\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(1.25)

Action of the Lie algebroid:

If
X\in C^{\infty}(G,\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s))
or
X\in C^{\infty}(G,\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r))
, then by (
1.24
) and (
1.12
), we have a map

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X}:C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l})\to C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l}),\quad\forall k,l\in\mathbb{C}.

Hence, if
X\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
, then we have maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{X_{R}},\mathcal{L}_{X_{L}}:C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l})\to C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l}),\quad\forall k,l\in\mathbb{C}.

It is very convenient to use the notation

\displaystyle X\ast f:=\mathcal{L}_{X_{R}}(f),\quad f\ast X:=\mathcal{L}_{X_{L}}(f),\quad\forall f\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{k,l}).

If
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then

\displaystyle X\ast(f\ast g)=(X\ast f)\ast g,\quad(f\ast X)\ast g=f\ast(X\ast g),\quad(X\ast f)^{*}=-f^{*}\ast X.

(1.26)

1.5
L^{1}
-Banach algebra

We fix for the rest of this section, a positive section

\omega\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}})

which satisfies

\displaystyle\omega(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})=\omega(\gamma_{1})\omega(\gamma_{2}),\ \forall(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in G^{(2)},\quad\omega(\gamma)=1,\ \forall\gamma\in G^{(0)}.

(1.27)

Example 1.5.1
.

If
\eta\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(A(G)))
is a positive section which vanishes nowhere, then
\omega(\gamma)=\eta(r(\gamma))\eta(s(\gamma))^{-1}
satisfies (
1.27
).
Not all
\omega\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}})
which satisfy (
1.27
) come from a section
\eta\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(A(G)))
.
We will consider such sections, see
2.5.2
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
|f|\omega\in C_{c}(G,\Omega^{1,0})
.
By (
1.19
), we can integrate
|f|\omega
along the fibers
s
.
Similarly, we can integrate
|f|\omega^{-1}
along the fibers of
r
.
We define

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}:

\displaystyle=\max\left(\left\lVert s_{*}(|f|\omega)\right\rVert_{C^{0}(G^{(0)})},\left\lVert r_{*}(|f|\omega^{-1})\right\rVert_{C^{0}(G^{(0)})}\right)

(1.28)

The completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
by the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}
is denoted by
L^{1}(G,\omega)
.
We have,

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\ast g\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)},\quad\left\lVert f^{*}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)},\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

In particular,
L^{1}(G,\omega)
is a Banach
*
-algebra.

Proposition 1.5.2

(Approximate identity)
.

There exists
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
a sequence of self-adjoint elements such that

i.

If
K\subseteq G^{(0)}
is compact, then the sequence
\supp(g_{n})\cap s^{-1}(K)
is eventually decreasing, and its set theoretic limit is equal to
K
, i.e.,
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(g_{m})\cap s^{-1}(K)=K
.

ii.

For any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
g_{n}\ast f
and
f\ast g_{n}
converge to
f
in the
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
-topology.

iii.

For any
f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
g_{n}\ast f
and
f\ast g_{n}
converge to
f
in the
C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
-topology.

iv.

For any
f\in L^{1}(G,\omega)
,
g_{n}\ast f
and
f\ast g_{n}
converge to
f
in the
L^{1}(G,\omega)
-topology.

In the continuous setting, this result is due to Renault
[
43
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Proof.

To simplify the exposition, we will only deal with the case where
G^{(0)}
is compact.
The general case is dealt with similarly using a partition of unity on
G^{(0)}
.
By
1.1.9
, there exists a finite family
X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A({G}))
which generates
C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A({G}))
.
The vector fields
X_{1R},\cdots,X_{kR}
are complete, see (
1.22
).
The map

\displaystyle\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times G^{(0)}\to G,\quad\phi(t_{1},\cdots,t_{k},x)=e^{t_{1}X_{1R}(x)+\cdots+t_{k}X_{kR}}\cdot x

is a submersion in a neighborhood of
\{0\}\times G^{(0)}
because
s
is a submersion.
To see this, let
\psi:G\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times G^{(0)}
be a local diffeomorphism of the form
\gamma\mapsto(g(\gamma),s(\gamma))
.
The map
\psi\circ\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times G^{(0)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times G^{(0)}
is of the form
(t,x)\mapsto(h(t,x),x)
for some smooth map
h
.
The differential of
h
in the direction of
t
at
(0,x)
is surjective. So, by the parameterized inverse mapping theorem, we deduce that
\psi\circ\phi
is a submersion which implies that
\phi
is a submersion.

By (
1.22
),
\phi(t,x)^{-1}=\phi(-t,r(\phi(t,x)))
.
We denote by
U
the set of
(t,x)
such that
\phi
is a well-defined submersion at
(t,x)
and at
(-t,\phi(t,x))
.
Since
s\circ\phi(t,x)=x
, by
1.1.14
, we get a canonical isomorphism
\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi))\otimes\phi^{*}(\Omega^{1,0}(G))\simeq\mathbb{C}
.
So, we have an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(U)\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{1,0}).

Let
(t,x)\in U
.
The exponential map
\gamma\in G_{x}\mapsto e^{t_{1}X_{1R}+\cdots+t_{k}X_{kR}}\cdot\gamma\in G_{x}
defines a local diffeomorphism from
x
to
\phi(t,x)
.
Hence, its differential at
x
is a linear isomorphism
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)_{x}\to\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)_{\phi(t,x)}
.
So, it defines an element of
\Omega^{1,-1}(G)_{\phi(t,x)}
.
The element
\omega^{2}(\phi(t,x))
also defines an element of
\Omega^{1,-1}(G)_{\phi(t,x)}
.
By taking the quotient, we obtain a smooth function
\kappa:U\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
.
One has

\displaystyle\phi_{*}(h)^{*}=\phi_{*}(\tilde{h}\kappa)\omega^{-2},\quad\forall h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U),

(1.29)

where
\tilde{h}(t,x)=\overline{h(-t,r(\phi(t,x)))}
. Furthermore,
\tilde{\kappa}=\frac{1}{\kappa}
and
\kappa(0,x)=1
.
Now, let
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}_{+})
be a sequence of functions such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}h_{n}(t)dt=1
,
h_{n}(t)=h_{n}(-t)
, and the sequence
(\supp(h_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is decreasing with intersection equal to
\{0\}
, and
\supp(h_{n})\times G^{(0)}\subseteq U
.
We see
h_{n}
as functions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times G^{(0)}
which don’t depend on
x
.
Let

\displaystyle g_{n}=\phi_{*}(h_{n}\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}})\omega^{-1}

Clearly,
g_{n}
is real-valued. By (
1.29
),
g^{*}=g
.
Section

1.5.2
.
i
follows from the inclusion
G^{(0)}\subseteq\supp(g_{n})\subseteq\phi(\supp(h_{n}))
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. We have

\displaystyle f(\gamma)-f\ast g_{n}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=f(\gamma)-\int_{G_{s(\gamma)}}f(\gamma\eta^{-1})\omega(\eta^{-1})g_{n}(\eta)

\displaystyle=f(\gamma)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}f(\gamma\phi(t,s(\gamma))^{-1})\omega(\phi(t,s(\gamma))^{-1})h_{n}(t)\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}(t,s(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Big(f(\gamma)-f(\gamma\phi(t,s(\gamma))^{-1})\omega(\phi(t,s(\gamma))^{-1})\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}(t,s(\gamma))\Big)h_{n}(t)

The map
\psi(t,\gamma)=f(\gamma)-f(\gamma\phi(t,s(\gamma))^{-1})\omega(\phi(t,s(\gamma))^{-1})\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}(t,s(\gamma))\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
satisfies

\lim_{t\to 0}\psi(t,\gamma)=\psi(0,\gamma)=0
, where the limit is in the topology of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
This proves
Section

1.5.2
.
ii
.

Section

1.5.2
.
iii
follows by a similar argument.
Finally,
Section

1.5.2
.
iv
follows from
Section

1.5.2
.
ii
and the fact that
\left\lVert g_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}h_{n}\kappa^{1/2}\leq\sup{\kappa^{1/2}_{|\supp(h_{1})}}
is uniformly bounded.
∎

1.6
Distributions on quasi-Lie groupoids

In this section, we define an algebra
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
of distributions on a quasi-Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
.
If
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
is a Lie groupoid, then
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
was introduced by Lescure, Manchon and Vassout
[
35
]
.

Definition 1.6.1
.

An element
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
consists of two functions
u\ast\cdot,\cdot\ast u:C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that

\displaystyle(f\ast u)\ast g=f\ast(u\ast g),\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Proposition 1.6.2
.

Let
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. The maps
u\ast\cdot
and
\cdot\ast u
are continuous
\mathbb{C}
-linear maps.
