Furthermore, the natural map
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is injective.

Proof.

Let
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an approximate identity as in
1.5.2
.
We have

\displaystyle u\ast f=\lim_{n\to+\infty}g_{n}\ast(u\ast f)=\lim_{n\to+\infty}(g_{n}\ast u)\ast f

Since
g_{n}\ast u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, the map
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\mapsto(g_{n}\ast u)\ast f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is continuous.
By the definition of the LF topology on
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, a linear map
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is continuous if and only if its restriction to
C^{\infty}(K,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is continuous for every compact
K\subseteq G
.
Now, continuity of
u\ast\cdot:C^{\infty}(K,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
follows from the uniform boundedness principle, see
[
47
, Theorem 2.8]
.
Injectivity of the inclusion
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\hookrightarrow C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
follows from
Section

1.5.2
.
iii
.
∎

The space
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is naturally a
*
-algebra with the product and adjoint defined by

\displaystyle(u\ast v)\ast f:=u\ast(v\ast f),\quad f\ast(u\ast v):=(f\ast u)\ast v,\quad u^{*}\ast f:=(f^{*}\ast u)^{*},\quad f\ast u^{*}:=(u\ast f^{*})^{*},

where
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),u,v\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Furthermore,
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a
*
-subalgebra of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
In fact, in
1.6.6
, we will prove that it is a two-sided ideal.
We equip the space
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
with the weakest topology such that for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, the maps

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad u\mapsto f\ast u,\quad u\mapsto u\ast f

are continuous.
The proof of
1.6.2
shows that
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is dense in
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Examples 1.6.3
.

i.

By (
1.26
), we have a linear map
C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))\to C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

ii.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(G^{(0)})
.
We define
\delta_{f}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
by

\displaystyle\delta_{f}\ast g:=(f\circ r)\cdot g,\quad g\ast\delta_{f}:=g\cdot(f\circ s),\quad g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

(1.30)

where
\cdot
is pointwise multiplication.
This defines a
*
-algebra inclusion

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(G^{(0)})\hookrightarrow C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(1.31)

Clearly
\delta_{1}
is the unit of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
If
f\circ r=f\circ s
, then
\delta_{f}
is in the center of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
If
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
is a Lie groupoid, then
\delta_{f}
is the distribution equal to
f
supported on
G^{(0)}
.

iii.

If
G=M\times M\rightrightarrows M
is the pair groupoid over a smooth manifold
M
, then an element of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a distribution
u
on
M\times M
with values in
\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(T(M\times M))
such that

\displaystyle x\mapsto\int_{M}u(x,y)f(y)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(TM)),\quad y\mapsto\int_{M}u(x,y)f(x)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(TM)),

for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(TM))
. Such distributions are also called semiregular
[
52
, p.532]
.

Proposition 1.6.4
.

There is a unique way to associate to each
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
a proper subset
\supp(u)\subseteq G
such that the following holds:

i.

If
u\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\supp(u)
is equal to the support of
u
as a smooth section.

ii.

If
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
converges to
u
, then
\supp(u)\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(u_{m})}
.

iii.

If
u,v\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\supp(u\ast v)\subseteq\supp(u)\supp(v)
and
\supp(u^{*})=\supp(u)^{-1}
.

Proof.

Let
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
as in
1.5.2
.
If it is possible to define
\supp(u)
which satisfies the above properties, then by
Section

1.5.2
.
i
, one has

\displaystyle\supp(u)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(u\ast g_{m})}.

(1.32)

This proves uniqueness. To prove existence, we define
\supp(u)
using (
1.32
).

Section

1.6.4
.
i
is straightforward to prove.
We now show that
s_{|\supp(u)}:\supp(u)\to G^{(0)}
is proper.
Let
K\subseteq G^{(0)}
be compact and
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G^{(0)})
which is equal to
1
on
K
.
It suffices to show that
\supp(u\ast g_{n}\ast\delta_{h})
is uniformly compactly supported.
By
Section

1.5.2
.
i
,
g_{n}\ast\delta_{h}
is uniformly compactly supported. So, the result follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 1.6.5
.

If
T:C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a continuous linear map, then for any
K\subseteq G
compact, there exists
K^{\prime}\subseteq G
compact such that
\supp(Tf)\subseteq K^{\prime}
for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
with
\supp(f)\subseteq K
.

Proof.

If not, then we can find a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly compactly supported but
(T(f_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
aren’t uniformly compactly supported.
By a standard diagonal argument, we can find a sequence
c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
such that
\sum_{n}c_{n}f_{n}
converges in
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
but
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}c_{n}T(f_{n})
isn’t compactly supported.
This contradicts the continuity of
T
.
∎

We now show that
\supp(u)
is the smallest closed subset
A
of
G
such that
\supp(u\ast f)\subseteq A\supp(f)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
For one inclusion, let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. Then,
u\ast g_{n}\ast f\to u\ast f
.
So,

\displaystyle\supp(u\ast f)\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(u\ast g_{n}\ast f)}\subseteq\left(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(u\ast g_{n})}\right)\supp(f),

where we used the fact that
\supp(f)
is compact in the second inclusion.
For the other inclusion, let
A
be a closed subset of
G
such that
\supp(u\ast f)\subseteq A\supp(f)
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\supp(u\ast g_{n})\subseteq A\supp(g_{n})
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(u\ast g_{n})}\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}A\supp(g_{n})}\subseteq A\left(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(g_{n})}\right)=A

where we used
Section

1.5.2
.
i
.
From this new description of the support,
Section

1.6.4
.
ii
follows easily, as well as that
\supp(u\ast v)\subseteq\supp(u)\supp(v)
.
Here, we use the fact that
\supp(u)\supp(v)
is closed which follows from properness of
s:\supp(v)\to G^{(0)}
.
One can prove by similar arguments that
\supp(u)=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\bigcup_{m\geq n}\supp(g_{m}\ast u)}
.
Hence,
\supp(u^{*})=\supp(u)
and
\supp(u)
is proper.
∎

Proposition 1.6.6
.

The space
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a two-sided ideal of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Proof.

Let
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G^{(0)})
. Since
f
is properly supported,
f\ast h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So,
(u\ast f)\ast\delta_{h}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By taking different functions
h
, one deduces that
u\ast f\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. It is properly supported by
1.6.4
.
∎

We denote by
C^{-\infty}_{c,r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
the subspace of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
of compactly supported distributions.

Restriction to saturated subsets:

Let
A\subseteq G
be a saturated subset. Since for any
x\in A
,
(G_{A})_{x}=G_{x}
and
(G_{A})^{x}=G^{x}
, it follows that
\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(G_{A})
is equal to the restriction of
\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(G)
to
G_{A}
.
By taking the restriction to
G_{A}
, we get a
*
-algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{proper}}(G_{A},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad f\mapsto f_{|G_{A}}.

This map extends to a
*
-algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G_{A},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad u\mapsto u_{|G_{A}},

(1.33)

where
u_{|G_{A}}\ast f=(u\ast g)_{|G_{A}}
and
f\ast u_{|G_{A}}=(g\ast u)_{|G_{A}}
, where
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{A},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is any extension of
f
.
The fact that
u_{|G_{A}}\ast f
and
f\ast u_{|G_{A}}
don’t depend on the choice of
g
follows easily using the distributions
Section

1.6.3
.
ii
, see proof of
1.6.6
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\supp(u_{|G_{A}})=\supp(u)\cap G_{|A},\quad\forall u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(1.34)

1.7
Representation theory of quasi-Lie groupoids

Conventions and notations:

The following will be used throughout this article.

•

If
V,W
are topological vector spaces, then
\mathcal{L}(V,W)
denotes the space of continuous linear maps
V\to W
.
We denote by
\mathcal{L}(V)
the space of continuous endomorphisms of
V
.

•

If
M
is a smooth manifold, then
L^{2}(M)
denotes the Hilbert space completion of the space
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(TM))
by the inner product
\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{M}\bar{f}g
.

Definition 1.7.1
.

Let
H
a Hilbert space.
A unitary representation of a quasi-Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
over
H
is a
*
-algebra homomorphism

\pi:C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to\mathcal{L}(H)
which is continuous when
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is equipped with the LF-topology, and
\mathcal{L}(H)
with the strong operator topology.
We say that
\pi
is non-degenerate if

\displaystyle\pi(C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))H:=\left\{\sum_{i\in I}\pi(f_{i})\xi_{i}:I\text{ a finite index set },(f_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),(\xi_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq H\right\}

is dense in
H
.
We will restrict our attention to non-degenerate representations.
It is convenient to denote the space
H
by
L^{2}(\pi)
.

Example 1.7.2
.

Let
x\in G^{(0)}
.
We define the regular representation
\Xi_{x}
of
G
acting on
L^{2}(G_{x})
by

\Xi_{x}(f)g=f\ast g
.

Remark 1.7.3
.

Let
\gamma\in G
. The smooth diffeomorphism
R_{\gamma}:G_{r(\gamma)}\to G_{s(\gamma)}
induces a unitary equivalence between
\Xi_{s(\gamma)}
and
\Xi_{r(\gamma)}
.

Smooth vectors:

If
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
\xi\in L^{2}(\pi)
, then
\pi(u)\xi
denotes the unique vector in
L^{2}(\pi)
, if it exists, such that

\displaystyle\langle\pi(u)\xi,\pi(f)\eta\rangle=\langle\xi,\pi(u^{*}\ast f)\eta\rangle,\quad\forall\eta\in L^{2}(\pi),f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(1.35)

Uniqueness follows from the non-degeneracy of
\pi
.
Let

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi):=\left\{\xi\in L^{2}(\pi):\forall u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\ \pi(u)\xi\text{ exists}\right\}.

The space
C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
is equipped with the complete locally convex topology given by the family of semi-norms
\xi\mapsto\left\lVert\pi(u)\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi)}
for
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
The space
C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
is dense in
L^{2}(\pi)
because
\pi(C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))L^{2}(\pi)\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
.

Definition 1.7.4
.

We denote by
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
the space of continuous anti-linear maps
C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)\to\mathbb{C}
.
The action of
\xi\in C^{-\infty}(\pi)
on
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
is denoted by
\langle\eta,\xi\rangle
.

Remark 1.7.5
.

One can also define
C^{\infty}(\pi):=\left\{\xi\in L^{2}(\pi):\forall u\in C^{-\infty}_{c,r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\ \pi(u)\xi\text{ exists}\right\}
, and
C^{-\infty}_{c}(\pi)
as its continuous dual.

We have obvious inclusions

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)\subseteq L^{2}(\pi)\subseteq C^{-\infty}(\pi).

If
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\pi(u)C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
.
By duality, we define

\displaystyle\pi(u):C^{-\infty}(\pi)\to C^{-\infty}(\pi),\quad\langle\eta,\pi(u)\xi\rangle:=\langle\pi(u^{*})\eta,\xi\rangle,\quad\forall\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi),\xi\in C^{-\infty}_{c}(\pi).

(1.36)

By a Riesz representation theorem argument, if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\pi(f)C^{-\infty}(\pi)\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
.
We now state the Dixmier-Malliavin theorem
[
21
]
.
The original version of Dixmier-Malliavin theorem is stated for Lie groups. It was generalised to Lie groupoids by Francis
[
24
]
.
The proof also works for quasi-Lie groupoids.

Theorem 1.7.6

(Dixmier-Malliavin theorem)
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
U
is an open neighborhood of
G^{(0)}
, then
f
can be written as sum of the form
g_{1}\ast h_{1}+\cdots+g_{n}\ast f_{n}
, where

g_{i},h_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
\supp(g_{i})\subseteq\supp(f)
and
\supp(h_{i})\subseteq U
for all
i\in[\![1,n]\!]
.

Theorem 1.7.7

(Dixmier-Malliavin theorem)
.

Let
\xi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
. There exists
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\pi)
and
f_{1},\cdots,f_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
\xi=\pi(f_{1})\xi_{1}+\cdots+\pi(f_{n})\xi_{n}
.

The following theorem in the continuous setting is due to Renault
[
42
]
.
We refer the reader to
[
4
]
for a modern proof using
C^{*}
-modules.

Theorem 1.7.8
.

Let
\omega\in C^{\infty}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}})
be a positive section which satisfies (
1.27
).
For any representation
\pi
of
G
, and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
\left\lVert\pi(f)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)}
.

1.8
Full and reduced
C^{*}
-algebras

For any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, we define

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}G}

\displaystyle:=\sup\left\{\left\lVert\pi(f)\right\rVert:\pi\text{ non-degenerate representation of }G\right\}

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}G}

\displaystyle:=\sup\{\left\lVert\Xi_{x}(f)\right\rVert:x\in G^{(0)}\}.

Theorem

1.7.8
implies that
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}G}<+\infty
for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
The completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
by the norms
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C^{*}G}
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}G}
are denoted by
C^{*}G
and
C^{*}_{r}G
respectively.
By
Theorem

1.7.8
, for any
\omega
satisfying (
1.27
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}G}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}G}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\omega)},\quad\forall f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(1.37)

So, we have natural
*
-homomorphisms

\displaystyle L^{1}(G,\omega)\xrightarrow{i}C^{*}G\xrightarrow{j}C^{*}_{r}G.

The map
j
is surjective because its image is dense, and it is a
*
-homomorphism between
C^{*}
-algebras.
The map
j\circ i
injective by
1.5.2
.

Proposition 1.8.1
.

Let
A\subseteq G^{(0)}
be a dense subset. For any
f\in C^{*}_{r}G
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}G}=\sup\left\{\left\lVert\Xi_{x}(f)\right\rVert:x\in A\right\}

Proof.

Let
B
be the
C^{*}
-algebra completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
with respect to the norm given by
\sup\left\{\left\lVert\Xi_{x}(f)\right\rVert:x\in A\right\}
.
We have a surjective
*
-homomorphism
C^{*}_{r}G\to B
.
We need to show that this map is injective.

Lemma 1.8.2
.

If
f\in C^{*}_{r}G
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},0})
, then

\displaystyle G^{(0)}\to\mathbb{R}_{+},\quad x\mapsto\left\lVert\Xi_{x}(f)g_{|G_{x}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(G_{x})}

(1.38)

is a continuous function.

Proof.

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then continuity of
f\ast g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},0})
and the dominated convergence theorem imply continuity of (
1.38
).
The result follows from density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
in
C^{*}_{r}(G)
. ∎

Let
f\in C^{*}_{r}G
such that
\Xi_{x}(f)=0
for all
x\in A
.
If
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},0})
, then by
1.8.2
and density of
A
,
\Xi_{x}(f)g_{|G_{x}}
vanishes for all
x\in G^{(0)}
.
So,
\Xi_{x}(f)
vanishes for all
x\in G^{(0)}
by density of elements of the form
g_{|G_{x}}
in
L^{2}(G_{x})
.
So,
f=0
.
∎

Let
A\subseteq G^{(0)}
be a closed saturated subset.
The restriction map
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{A},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and the inclusion map
C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{A^{c}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
fit into a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to C^{*}G_{A^{c}}\to C^{*}G\to C^{*}G_{A}\to 0.

(1.39)

They also extend to an inclusion and a surjection

\displaystyle C^{*}_{r}G_{A^{c}}\hookrightarrow C^{*}_{r}G\twoheadrightarrow C^{*}_{r}G_{A}.

(1.40)

Generally (
1.40
) is
not
exact in the middle.

Definition 1.8.3
.

We say that a quasi-Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
has the weak containment property (WCP) if the natural surjective map
C^{*}G\to C^{*}_{r}G
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 1.8.4
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
be a quasi-Lie groupoid,
A\subseteq G^{(0)}
a closed saturated subset. If
G_{A}
and
G_{A^{c}}
have the WCP, then
G
has the WCP.

Proof.

This follows immediately from (
1.39
) and (
1.40
).
∎

The converse of
1.8.4
is false, see
[
55
]
.
For this reason, we say that a quasi-Lie groupoid has the stable weak containment property (SWCP) if
G_{A}
has the WCP for any locally compact saturated subset
A\subseteq G^{(0)}
.
It is conjectured by C. Anantharaman-Delaroche that the SWCP is equivalent to topological amenability, see
[
2
]
.
The following proposition is an obvious corollary of
1.8.4
.

Proposition 1.8.5
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
be a quasi-Lie groupoid,
A\subseteq G^{(0)}
a closed saturated subset. The groupoids
G_{A}
and
G_{A^{c}}
have the SWCP if and only if
G
has the SWCP.

The following proposition will be used in
Section

5.2
.

Proposition 1.8.6
.

Let
G
be an amenable Lie group which acts on a differential space
X
,
H\rightrightarrows X
a quasi-Lie groupoid.
If there exists a surjective submersion
f:G\rtimes X\to H
which is a morphism of quasi-Lie groupoid that is equal to the identity on
X
, then

H\rightrightarrows X
has the SWCP property.

Proof.

A saturated subset of
X
for the groupoid
H
is also a saturated subset for the groupoid
G\rtimes X
because
f
is surjective.
A saturated subset is just a
G
-invariant subset of
X
. So, without loss of generality we only need to show that
H
has the WCP.
Topological amenability implies the WCP property, see
[
1
, Proposition 6.1.8 and Proposition 3.3.5]
. The groupoid
G\rtimes X
is topologically amenable by
[
1
, Corollary 2.2.10]
.
It follows from
[
1
, Proposition 5.1.2]
that
H\rightrightarrows X
is also topologically amenable.
∎

Fiberation of
C^{*}
-algebras and multipliers.

We will use of the theory of multipliers of
C^{*}
-algebras. We refer the reader to
[
56
, Section 2.3]
or
[
34
]
for more details.
If
A
is a
C^{*}
-algebra, then we denote by
\mathcal{M}(A)
its multiplier
C^{*}
-algebra.
Recall that a multiplier of
A
is a linear map
T:A\to A
such that there exists a map
T^{*}:A\to A
(called the adjoint) which satisfy

\displaystyle T(ab)=T(a)b,\quad T(a)^{*}b=a^{*}T^{*}(b),\quad T^{*}(ab)=T^{*}(a)b,\quad\forall a,b\in A.

The map
T^{*}
is unique. Its existence implies by the closed graph theorem that
T
is bounded. The algebra
\mathcal{M}(A)
with the operator norm of
T
is a
C^{*}
-algebra.

We will also make use of the theory of fibered
C^{*}
-algebras in the sense of Kasparov
[
33
]
.
We refer the reader to
[
7
]
and
[
56
, Appendix]
for an introduction.
A fibration of a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
over a locally compact space
X
is a unital
*
-homomorphism
C_{b}(X)\to\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{M}(A)
, where
C_{b}(X)
is the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded continuous functions on
X
,
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{M}(A)
is the center of the multiplier algebra.
The fiber of
A
at
x\in X
is the
C^{*}
-algebra
A_{x}:=A/I_{x}A
, where
I_{x}=\{f\in C_{0}(X):f(x)=0\}
,
I_{x}A
is the closure of the linear span of elements of the form
fa
where
f\in I_{x}
,
a\in A
.

Fibration of groupoids:

Let
G\rightrightarrows G^{(0)}
be a quasi-Lie groupoid,
X
a differential space,
\rho:G^{(0)}\to X
a smooth map which satisfies
\rho\circ r=\rho\circ s
.
In this case for each locally closed subset
A\subseteq X
,
\rho^{-1}(A)
is a saturated subset.
So, we can form the quasi-Lie groupoid
G_{\rho^{-1}(A)}\rightrightarrows\rho^{-1}(A)
.

The map

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c}(X)\to\mathcal{L}(C^{\infty}_{c}(G)),\quad f\mapsto(g\mapsto\delta_{f\circ\rho}\ast g)

extends to a unital
*
-homomorphism

C_{b}(X)\to\mathcal{M}(C^{*}G)

So,
C^{*}G
is a
C_{0}(X)
-
C^{*}
-algebra.

Proposition 1.8.7
.

If
G\rightrightarrows G^{0}
has the SWCP, then for any
x\in X
, the fiber (as a
C_{0}(X)
-
C^{*}
-algebra) of
C^{*}G
at
x
is equal to
C^{*}G_{\rho^{-1}(x)}
.

Proof.

By (
1.39
), it suffices to show that
I_{x}C^{*}G=C^{*}G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}
.
The groupoid
G_{\rho^{-1}(F^{c})}
has the WCP.
The obvious inclusion

C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})})\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
maps into
I_{x}C^{*}G
. By the definition, of the maximal
C^{*}
-algebra, this inclusion extends to a
*
-homomorphism
C^{*}G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}\to I_{x}C^{*}G
.
It is surjective because it has dense image.
The inclusion
C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})})\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
also extends to an injective
*
-homomorphism
C^{*}_{r}G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}\to C^{*}_{r}G
.
Since
G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}
has the WCP,
C^{*}G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}=C^{*}_{r}G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}
. So, the map
C^{*}G_{\rho^{-1}(\{x\}^{c})}\to I_{x}C^{*}G
is injective
∎

1.8.7
is false in general without the SWCP.

1.9
Automorphisms

An automorphism of a quasi-Lie groupoid
G
is a morphism of quasi-Lie groupoids
\alpha:G\to G
which is a diffeomorphism.
The differential of
\alpha
induces an action on
A(G)

\displaystyle\alpha_{*}:A(G)_{x}\to A(G)_{\alpha(x)},\quad\alpha_{*}(X)=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{x}(X)

(1.41)

and an action on
C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))
by

\displaystyle\alpha_{*}:C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G))\to C^{\infty}(G^{(0)},A(G)),\quad\alpha_{*}(X)(x)=\alpha_{*}(X(\alpha^{-1}(x))).

By (
1.41
), for any
k,l\in\mathbb{C}
, we get a map

\displaystyle\alpha_{*}:\Omega^{k,l}(G)_{\gamma}\to\Omega^{k,l}(G)_{\alpha(\gamma)},\quad\gamma\in G

which induces an action on
C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
by

\displaystyle\alpha_{*}(f)(\gamma)=\alpha_{*}(f(\alpha^{-1}(\gamma))).

This action extends to
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
by the formula

\displaystyle\alpha_{*}(u)\ast f=\alpha_{*}(u\ast(\alpha^{-1})_{*}(f)),\quad f\ast\alpha_{*}(u)=\alpha_{*}((\alpha^{-1})_{*}(f)\ast u).

The map
\alpha_{*}:C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a
*
-algebra automorphism which satisfies

\displaystyle\supp(\alpha_{*}(u))=\alpha(\supp(u)),\quad\forall u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(1.42)

For any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\alpha_{*}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}G}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}G},\quad\left\lVert\alpha_{*}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}G}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}G}.

(1.43)

Hence,
\alpha_{*}
extends to a
*
-algebra automorphisms
\alpha_{*}:C^{*}_{r}G\to C^{*}_{r}G
and
\alpha_{*}:C^{*}G\to C^{*}G
.

Chapter 2
Multi-graded sub-Riemannian geometry

Notations and conventions:

We fix for the rest of this article a smooth manifold
M
without boundary and a natural number
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
which denotes the number of multi-parameters.

i.

If
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
,
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, then

\displaystyle t^{k}:=t_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots t_{\nu}^{k_{\nu}}\in\mathbb{C}

(2.1)

with the convention
0^{0}=1
and
0^{z}=0
for all
z\neq 0
.
The notation (
2.1
) is only used either in the case where
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, or in the case where
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
and
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
.
The key fact which relies on this convention is that if
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then the map
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\mapsto t^{k}\in\mathbb{C}
is smooth.

ii.

Let
k,l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
.
We use the partial order
k\preceq l
if
l-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.
This induces the relation
k\prec l
if
k\preceq l
and
k\neq l
.
The symbols
\preceq
and
\prec
are reserved for this partial order.

iii.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra and
a,b\in\mathfrak{g}
, then we denote by

\displaystyle\BCH(a,b):=a+b-\frac{[a,b]}{2}+\frac{1}{12}[a,[a,b]]-\frac{1}{12}[b,[a,b]]+\cdots

(2.2)

the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff product of
a
and
b
which is a finite sum because
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent.
Often, we use the notation
a\cdot b
instead of
\BCH(a,b)
.
Our BCH formula differs by a sign from the classical formula, i.e., it is the BCH formula for the opposite Lie algebra.
See (
2.3
) for the motivation behind our choice.
The inverse of
a
which is
-a
is often denoted by
a^{-1}
.

iv.

We recall that if
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
and
x\in M
, then
e^{X}\cdot x
denotes the flow, if it exists, of
X
at time
1
starting from
x
.
So, the flow of
X
at time
t
is equal to
e^{tX}\cdot x
.
We stress that we never use time dependent flows.

Throughout the article, we will have real vector spaces
V
equipped with a linear map
\natural:V\to\mathcal{X}(M)
.
To ease the notation, we will use
e^{v}\cdot x
instead of
e^{\natural(v)}\cdot x
.
This shouldn’t cause any confusion because the map
\natural
will be clear from context.
If
v,w\in V
, then we write
e^{v}e^{w}\cdot x
instead of
e^{v}\cdot(e^{w}\cdot x)
.
We remark that in some situations
V
will be a nilpotent Lie algebra.
Our sign convention in the BCH formula implies that if
\natural:V\to\mathcal{X}(M)
is a Lie algebra homomorphism, then

\displaystyle e^{\BCH(v,w)}\cdot x=e^{v}e^{w}\cdot x.

(2.3)

Generally,
\natural:V\to\mathcal{X}(M)
won’t be a Lie algebra homomorphism.
It will only preserve some Lie brackets up to some error term, see (
3.4
).
This adds an error term to (
2.3
), which is ultimately resolved by
3.2.3
, see (
3.18
).

2.1
Multi-graded sub-Riemannian structure

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure on
M
of depth
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\nu}
is a family
(\mathcal{F}^{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}
of finitely generated
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
-submodules of
\mathcal{X}(M)
such that:

i.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, if
k\preceq l
, then
\mathcal{F}^{k}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{l}
.

ii.

One has
\mathcal{F}^{0}=0
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(N_{1},0,\cdots,0)}=\mathcal{F}^{(0,N_{2},0,\cdots,0)}=\cdots=\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,N_{\nu})}=\mathcal{X}(M).

(2.4)

iii.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,

[\mathcal{F}^{k},\mathcal{F}^{l}]\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k+l}.

(2.5)

Notice that
\mathcal{F}^{k}=\mathcal{X}(M)
if
N_{i}\leq k_{i}
for some
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
.
So, to define a
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure of depth
N
, it is enough to define
\mathcal{F}^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
with
k_{i}<N_{i}
for all
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
and
k\neq 0
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

For any examples of
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structures to exist,
\mathcal{X}(M)
needs to be finitely generated as a
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
-module.
This is always true, see
1.1.9
.

Examples 2.1.3
.

i.

The theory of elliptic operators corresponds to the trivial filtration where
\nu=1
and
N=1
.

ii.

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{d}
be a family of vector fields satisfying Hörmander’s condition of depth
N\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e., for any
x\in M
,
T_{x}M
is linearly spanned by iterated commutators of
X_{i}
of length
\leq N
.
We define a
1
-graded sub-Riemannian structure of depth
N
by declaring
\mathcal{F}^{i}
to be the module generated by all iterated Lie brackets of length
\leq i
using the vector fields
X_{\bullet}
.

iii.

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{d}
and
Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{d^{\prime}}
be two families of vector fields such that each family satisfies Hörmander’s condition of depth
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
respectively.
We define a
2
-graded sub-Riemannian structure of depth
(N,N^{\prime})
by declaring
\mathcal{F}^{(i,j)}
to be the module generated by the iterated Lie brackets of length
\leq i+j
using the vector fields
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
and such that the vector fields
X_{\bullet}
appear
\leq i
times and the vector fields
Y_{\bullet}
appear
\leq j
times.

Pullback:

One can pull back
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structures along a submersion.
Let
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
be a smooth submersion,
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(M)
a
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
-submodule.
We define
\phi^{*}(\mathcal{F})
to be the
C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\mathbb{R})
-submodule of
\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
consisting of all
X\in\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
such that there exists
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}
and
g_{1},\cdots,g_{n}\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}X_{i}\circ\phi\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\phi^{*}(TM)).

Proposition 2.1.4
.

If
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is a
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure on
M
of depth
N
, then
\phi^{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
is a
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure on
M^{\prime}
of depth
N
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
be generators.
We will show that
\phi^{*}(\mathcal{F}^{k})
is finitely generated.
Since
\phi
is a submersion, for each
i\in[\![1,n]\!]
, there exists
X^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
such that
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi(X^{\prime}_{i})=X_{i}\circ\phi
.
By
1.1.9
,
C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi))
is a finitely generated
C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\mathbb{R})
-module.
Let
Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{m}\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi))
be some generators.
Clearly,
X^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,X^{\prime}_{n},Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{m}
generate
\phi^{*}(\mathcal{F}^{k})
.
Finally, it is enough to check (
2.5
) on generators which follows from
1.3.5
.
∎

We fix for the rest of this chapter a
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
on
M
.

Multi-graded basis:

The modules
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
are finitely generated.
It is well known that linear algebra is simpler when one avoids using linear bases.
Nevertheless, we still need to make use of generators of
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
.
So, to avoid complicated notation involving indices, we introduce the following notion.

Definition 2.1.5
.

A
\nu
-graded basis is a pair
(V,\natural)
, where

V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}V^{k}

is a
\nu
-graded finite dimensional real vector space,
\natural:V\to\mathcal{X}(M)
an
\mathbb{R}
-linear map such that:

i.

One has
V^{0}=0
.

ii.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
\natural(V^{k})\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k}
.

iii.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
\mathcal{F}^{k}
is equal to the
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
-module generated by

\natural\left(\bigoplus_{l\preceq k}V^{l}\right)
.

Dilations on
\nu
-graded basis:

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
. We define the dilation

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:V\to V,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}v_{k}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}\lambda^{k}v_{k},\quad\text{where }v_{k}\in V^{k}\text{ and }\lambda^{k}\text{ is defined in }\eqref{eqn:tk}.

(2.6)

Remark 2.1.6
.

Various different dilations will be defined throughout this article. All of them will be denoted by
\alpha_{\lambda}
as there is no risk of confusion.
It is crucial to notice that some are well-defined for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
while others are only well-defined for
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, see for example
Section

2.6
and more precisely
2.6.6
where this distinction plays an important role.

Evaluation map:

For any
x\in M
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
, we define

\displaystyle\natural_{x,t}:V\to T_{x}M,\quad\natural_{x,t}(v):=\natural(\alpha_{t}(v))(x).

The map
\natural_{x,0}
which is more subtle to define is given in the next section.
By (
2.4
), the map
\natural_{x,t}
is surjective for any
x\in M
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
.
Hence,
\ker(\natural_{x,t})
defines an element of
\mathrm{Grass}(V)
, the Grassmannian manifold of linear subspaces of
V
of codimension equal to
\dim(M)
.
Since the map
\natural_{x,t}
depends smoothly on
x
and
t
, the following inclusion is smooth:

\displaystyle M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad(x,t)\mapsto(\ker(\natural_{x,t}),x,t).

(2.7)

We can’t extend this inclusion to
t=0
because

\lim_{t\to 0}\ker(\natural_{x,t})
exists for each fixed
x
but in general it is not uniform in
x
.
We will resolve this issue by a blowup procedure in the next section.
Essentially, we will take the closure of the image of (
2.7
) and call it
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, our blow-up space.

2.2
Tangent bundle in multi-graded sub-Riemannian geometry

Summary:

In this section, we define the tangent bundle
A(\mathcal{G})
in
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian geometry which plays a fundamental role in the study of multi-parameter maximally hypoelliptic differential operators. For example:

i.

The dual of
A(\mathcal{G})
is naturally connected to the domain of the principal symbol of multi-parameter maximally hypoelliptic differential operators, see (
4.11
).

ii.

The Pontryagin characteristic classes of
A(\mathcal{G})
enter into our index formula in a way similar to that of Atiyah-Singer index formula but more subtle due to the noncommutativity of the principal symbol. This is the content of Part II in this series of articles.

iii.

Trivializing the density bundle of
A(\mathcal{G})
leads to the leading term in the asymptotics of the heat kernel and Weyl Law of multi-parameter maximally hypoelliptic differential operators. This is the content of Part III in this series of articles.

The peculiar thing about
A(\mathcal{G})
is that its base space is not
M
but a topological blowup of
M
which we denote by
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.
We also construct a real vector bundle
A(\mathbb{G})\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
which creates a deformation between the bundles
TM\to M
and
A(\mathcal{G})\to\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.
As the notation suggests, the bundles
A(\mathcal{G})
and
A(\mathbb{G})
are the Lie algebroids of quasi-Lie groupoids
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
and
\mathbb{G}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
which we construct in
Section

2.3
.
This will endow the space of smooth sections of
A(\mathcal{G})
and
A(\mathbb{G})
with a Lie bracket as expected of a tangent bundle.

Multi-graded osculating group:

It is useful to introduce the notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}:=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},l\prec k}\mathcal{F}^{l},\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k}
, and
\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}
is a finitely generated
C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
-module.
By convention,
\mathcal{F}^{\prec 0}=0
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

The
\nu
-graded osculating Lie algebra
of
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
at
x
is the vector space

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{x}:=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}\frac{\mathcal{F}^{k}}{\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}+C^{\infty}_{x}(M,\mathbb{R})\mathcal{F}^{k}},\text{ where }C^{\infty}_{x}(M,\mathbb{R})=\{f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}):f(x)=0\}.

(2.8)

If
X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
, then
[X]_{k,x}
denotes its class in
\mathcal{F}^{k}/(\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}+C^{\infty}_{x}(M,\mathbb{R})\mathcal{F}^{k})
.
If
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
, then
[fX]_{k,x}=f(x)[X]_{k,x}
.
This identity together with the fact that the module
\mathcal{F}^{k}
is finitely generated implies that
\mathcal{F}^{k}/(\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}+C^{\infty}_{x}(M,\mathbb{R})\mathcal{F}^{k})
is a finite dimensional real vector space.
Since
\mathcal{F}^{k}=\mathcal{X}(M)
if
k_{i}\geq N_{i}
for some
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
, we deduce that
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a finite dimensional vector space.
We define a Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
by the formula

\displaystyle{}_{k,x},[Y]_{l,x}]:=[[X,Y]]_{k+l,x},\quad\forall X\in\mathcal{F}^{k},Y\in\mathcal{F}^{l}.

(2.9)

The space
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra, because the quotient
\mathcal{F}^{k}/(\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}+C^{\infty}_{x}(M,\mathbb{R})\mathcal{F}^{k})
vanishes if
k_{i}>N_{i}
for some
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
.
Here, we make use of our assumption
\mathcal{F}^{0}=0
.
The space
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
becomes a Lie group with the product of
a,b\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}
equal to
\BCH(a,b)
.
Since
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a
\nu
-graded vector space, we can define
\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathfrak{g}_{x}\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}
like in (
2.6
). By (
2.9
) and (
2.2
),
\alpha_{\lambda}
is a Lie algebra and a Lie group homomorphism.

Tangent cones:

Let
x\in M
,
(V,\natural)
a
\nu
-graded basis.
We define the linear map

\displaystyle\natural_{x,0}:V\to\mathfrak{g}_{x},\quad\natural_{x,0}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}v_{k}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}[\natural(v_{k})]_{k,x},\quad v_{k}\in V^{k}.

(2.10)

By
Section

2.1.5
.
iii
,
\natural_{x,0}
is surjective.
It also commutes with the dilation action.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
L\in\mathrm{Grass}(V)
,
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
be a sequence such that

\displaystyle(\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}),x_{n},t_{n})\to(L,x,0)\in\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}.

Then,
\ker(\natural_{x,0})\subseteq L
.

For a linear subspace
L
of
V
,
\ker(\natural_{x,0})\subseteq L
if and only if for some subspace
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}
,
L=\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L^{\prime})
.
In this case,
L^{\prime}=\natural_{x,0}(L)
.
We can now define tangent cones

Definition 2.2.3
.

A linear subspace
L\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}
of codimension
\dim(M)
is a
tangent cone
at
x
, if there exists a sequence
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}),x_{n},t_{n})\to(\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L),x,0)
in
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
We denote the set of tangent cones at
x
by
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

In
2.2.3
, it is rather important to impose that
t_{n}
are non-negative. If we instead consider
t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\backslash 0
, we usually get more tangent cones, see for example the condition on
\zeta
after (
2.21
).

Since
\mathrm{Grass}(V)
is compact, for any sequence
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
such that
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
, there exists a subsequence such that

\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})
converges to some subspace of
V
.
2.2.2
implies that the limit of
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})
has to be of the form
\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
for some
L\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}
linear subspace of codimension
\dim(M)
.
So, for all
x\in M
,
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}
is nonempty.
The set of all tangent cones is denoted by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(0)}:=\{(L,x):x\in M,L\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}\}

We also define the set

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}^{(0)}:=M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\sqcup\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}.

For any
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
, we have the following injective map

\displaystyle\mathrm{Grass}(\natural):\mathbb{G}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}

(2.11)

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\ker(\natural_{x,t}),x,t),

\displaystyle(x,t)\in M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle(L,x,0)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L),x,0),

\displaystyle(L,x,0)\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}

whose image is closed by the definition of tangent cones.
We equip
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
with the subspace topology and sub-differential structure from
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
A priori,
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
depends on the choice of the
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
. This isn’t the case as the next theorem shows.

Theorem 2.2.5
.

i.

For any
x\in M
, the set
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
doesn’t depend on the choice of the
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
.
Furthermore, the topology and the differential structure on
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
don’t depend on the choice of the
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
.

ii.

The natural projection

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}:\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(x,t)=(x,t),\quad\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(L,x,0)=(x,0)

(2.12)

is a proper smooth surjective map.

iii.

The inclusion
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is an open smooth embedding with dense image.

Throughout the article, the notation
\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
is reserved for the projection map (
2.12
).
We now describe the topology of
\mathbb{G}
in terms of sequences which follows immediately from
Section

2.2.5
.
i
.

Proposition 2.2.6
.

i.

A sequence
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
converges to
(L,x,0)
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
if and only if
for any (or for some)
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
,
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
,
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.

ii.

A sequence
(L_{n},x_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
converges to
(L,x)
in
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
if and only if
for any (or for some)
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
,
x_{n}\to x
,
\natural_{x_{n},0}^{-1}(L_{n})\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.

Remark 2.2.7
.

In general
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is not a smooth manifold.
In fact
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
can exhibit pathological properties.
If
M
is connected, then
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is connected because it is the closure of
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
.
But even though
M
is path-connected,
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
generally isn’t path-connected.

Tangent bundle:

Let
A(\mathbb{G})
be the real vector bundle over
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
whose fiber over
(x,t)\in M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
is equal to
T_{x}M
, and whose fiber over
(L,x,0)
is equal to
\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{L}
.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis.
There is a natural morphism of vector bundles
A(\natural):V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to A(\mathbb{G})
defined as follows:

\displaystyle A(\natural)(v,x,t)=(\natural_{x,t}(v),x,t),\quad A(\natural)(v,L,x,0)=(\natural_{x,0}(v)\bmod L,x,0).

(2.13)

The map
A(\natural)
is clearly surjective.
We equip
A(\mathbb{G})
with the quotient topology.
We also define
C^{\infty}(A(\mathbb{G}))
to be the space of
f:A(\mathbb{G})\to\mathbb{C}
such that
f\circ A(\natural)
is smooth.

Theorem 2.2.8
.

i.

The topological space
A(\mathbb{G})
is metrizable locally compact second countable, and
C^{\infty}(A(\mathbb{G}))
is a finite dimensional differential structure,
and
A(\natural)
is a submersion.

ii.

The differential space
A(\mathbb{G})
is a real vector bundle over
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, see
1.1.8
.

iii.

The topology and the differential structure on
A(\mathbb{G})
don’t depend on the choice of
(V,\natural)
.

We now give some natural examples of smooth sections of the bundle
A(\mathbb{G})
.

Proposition 2.2.9
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
, the section
\theta_{k}(X)
of
A(\mathbb{G})
defined by

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(X)(x,t)=t^{k}X(x)\in T_{x}M,\quad\theta_{k}(X)(L,x,0)=[X]_{k,x}\bmod L\in\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{L}

is smooth.
Also, the family
(\theta_{k}(X))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}}
generates
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},A(\mathbb{G}))
as a
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},\mathbb{R})
-module.

We denote by
A(\mathcal{G})
the restriction of
A(\mathbb{G})
to
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.

Tangent cones are Lie subalgebras:

Recall that
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a Lie algebra by (
2.9
) and a Lie group by (
2.2
).

Proposition 2.2.10
.

Let
x\in M
,
L\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}
be a tangent cone.

i.

The linear subspace
L
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
(and hence also a closed Lie subgroup of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
).

ii.

If
g\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, then

gLg^{-1}

is a tangent cone at
x
.

2.3
Tangent groupoid

By
2.2.10
, for each
x\in M
, following
Section

1.2.1
.
iv
, we can define the quotient groupoid of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
by
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}
.
We take the disjoint union of all such groupoids as
x
varies, i.e.,
let

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\{(A,x):x\in M,A=gLh\text{ for some }g,h\in\mathfrak{g}_{x},L\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}\}

We also define the space

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}:=M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\sqcup\mathcal{G}\times\{0\}.

In this section, we equip
\mathbb{G}
with a groupoid structure, a topology, and a differential structure making it a quasi-Lie groupoid.

Groupoid structure:

The space of objects of
\mathbb{G}
is
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
.
The structural morphisms are

\displaystyle s:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)},

\displaystyle s(y,x,t)=(x,t),

\displaystyle s(A,x,0)=(A^{-1}A,x,0)

\displaystyle r:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)},

\displaystyle r(y,x,t)=(y,t),

\displaystyle r(A,x,0)=(AA^{-1},x,0)

\displaystyle u:\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G},

\displaystyle u(x,t)=(x,x,t),

\displaystyle u(L,x,0)=(L,x,0)

\displaystyle\iota:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G},

\displaystyle\iota(y,x,t)=(x,y,t),

\displaystyle\iota(A,x,0)=(A^{-1},x,0)

\displaystyle m:\mathbb{G}^{(2)}\to\mathbb{G},

\displaystyle m((z,y,t),(y,x,t))=(z,x,t),

\displaystyle m((B,x,0),(A,x,0))=(BA,x,0)

In other words, the groupoid
\mathbb{G}
is the disjoint union of a copy of the pair groupoid
M\times M
for each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
, and
a copy of the quotient groupoid of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
by
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}

for each
x\in M
.

Topology and differential structure:

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis.
The following map plays a central role in this article

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\subseteq V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}

\displaystyle(v,x,t)\mapsto(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,x,t)

\displaystyle(v,L,x,0)\mapsto(\natural_{x,0}(v)L,x,0).

Its domain is defined as follows: Consider the map

\displaystyle V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0\to M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\quad(v,x,t)\mapsto(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,x,t)

(2.14)

Let
U
be the open subset of
V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
such that (
2.14
) is a well-defined submersion,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})

\displaystyle=U\sqcup V\times M\times\{0\}

\displaystyle\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})

\displaystyle=U\sqcup V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}

\displaystyle\subseteq V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}.

The set
\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
is open because for any
v\in V,x\in M
, when
t
is close enough to
0
but non-zero,
(v,x,t)\in U
by (
2.4
).
The set
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
is open because
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})=\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}))
,
where

\displaystyle\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}:V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(v,\gamma)=(v,\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(\gamma))

(2.16)

is the natural projection which is continuous by
Section

2.2.5
.
ii
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

i.

A subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is open if and only if the following holds:

•

The subset
U\cap(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\})
is open.

•

For any
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
,
\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{-1}(U)
is an open subset of
V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
.

In other words, if
T
is a topological space, then a map
f:\mathbb{G}\to T
is continuous if and only if

f_{|M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}}
and
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
are continuous for every
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
.

ii.

A function
f:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{C}
is smooth if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The map
f_{|M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}}
is smooth.

•

For any
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
, the map
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\to\mathbb{C}
is smooth, where
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\subseteq V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
inherits a differential structure from
V
and
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
.

Theorem 2.3.2
.

i.

In
2.3.1
, one can replace "For any
\nu
-graded basis" by "For some
\nu
-graded basis".
Neither the topology nor the space of smooth functions change.

ii.

The topological space
\mathbb{G}
is metrizable locally compact second countable.

iii.

The smooth structure
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
defines a finite dimensional differential structure on
\mathbb{G}
.

iv.

The inclusion
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}
is an open smooth embedding with dense image.

v.

For any
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
, the map
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is a submersion.

Let us describe the topology in terms of sequences.

Theorem 2.3.3
.

i.

A sequence
(y_{n},x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
converges to a point
(A,x,0)
in
\mathbb{G}
if and only if the following hold:

I.

The sequence
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
converges to
(A^{-1}A,x,0)
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
.

II.

For any (or for some)
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
, there exists
1
1

1

To be very precise in the forward implication
\implies
, the elements of the sequence
v_{n}
only exist for
n
big enough. To simplify the exposition, we will ignore this.

(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
and
v\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(A)
such that
v_{n}\to v
and
e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=y_{n}
.

Furthermore, if
(y_{n},x_{n},t_{n})
converges to
(A,x,0)
, then for any
v\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(A)
, there exists
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
such that
v_{n}\to v
and
e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=y_{n}
.

ii.

A sequence
(A_{n},x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
converges to
(A,x)
in
\mathcal{G}
if and only if the following hold:

I.

The sequence
(A_{n}^{-1}A_{n},x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
converges to
(A^{-1}A,x)
in the topology of
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.

(V,\natural)

(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V

such that
v_{n}\to v
and

Furthermore, if
(A_{n},x_{n})
