, there exists
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
such that
v_{n}\to v
and
v_{n}\in\natural_{x_{n},0}^{-1}(A_{n})
.

It is rather interesting to compare
2.3.3
to
3.0.1
.
In practice, it is often more useful to use the following theorem to verify the convergence in
\mathbb{G}
.

Theorem 2.3.4
.

Let
x\in M
,
L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
a tangent cone,
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis, and
S\subseteq V
a linear subspace such that the map

(v,l)\in S\times L\mapsto\natural_{x,0}(v)l\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}

is a diffeomorphism. A sequence
(y_{n},x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
converges to a point
(\natural_{x,0}(v)L,x,0)
in
\mathbb{G}
where
v\in S
if and only if the following hold:

I.

The sequence
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
converges to
(L,x,0)
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
.

II.

For
n
big enough,
there exists
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq S
such that
v_{n}\to v
and
e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=y_{n}
.

Theorem 2.3.5
.

The groupoid
\mathbb{G}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is a quasi-Lie groupoid whose Lie algebroid is
A(\mathbb{G})
.

By
2.3.5
, we mean that the differential structure and topology induced on
A(\mathbb{G})
from the differential structure and topology on
\mathbb{G}
coincide with the differential structure and topology described in
Section

2.2
.

The following proposition gives a natural isomorphism of vector bundles which will be used repeatedly throughout this article.

Proposition 2.3.6
.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{C}
, we have a natural smooth isomorphism

\displaystyle\Omega^{k+l}(V)\simeq\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{V}(\Omega^{k,l}(\mathbb{G}))\otimes\Omega^{k+l}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V}))

(2.17)

Lie bracket:

Since
A(\mathbb{G})
is the Lie algebroid of a quasi-Lie groupoid, it has a Lie bracket

\displaystyle:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},A(\mathbb{G}))\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},A(\mathbb{G}))\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},A(\mathbb{G}))

On
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
, the groupoid
\mathbb{G}
is the pair groupoid. So, the Lie bracket when restricted to
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
is the usual Lie bracket on
TM
.
By the density of
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle=\theta_{k+l}([X,Y]),\quad\forall k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},X\in\mathcal{F}^{k},Y\in\mathcal{F}^{l}.

(2.18)

2.4
Example of a tangent groupoid

In this section, we consider an example of a
2
-graded sub-Riemannian structure. We will present the tangent cones and the tangent groupoid.
We leave the verification to the reader.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
greater than or equal to
2
be fixed.
Consider the
2
-graded structure of depth
(1,N)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{0,k}=\{f\partial_{x}+gx^{N-k}\partial_{y}:f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})\},\quad k\in[\![1,N]\!].

This is the structure defined in
Section

2.1.3
.
iii
associated to the families
\{\partial_{x},\partial_{y}\}
and
\{\partial_{x},x^{N-1}\partial_{y}\}
.

Osculating Lie algebras:

We will describe the osculating Lie algebra by giving a linear basis.
In the following equations, span means linearly spanned, and the generators are linearly independent.
To ease the notation, we will use

\displaystyle X_{1}=[\partial_{x}]_{(1,0),(x,y)},

\displaystyle X_{2}=[\partial_{y}]_{(1,0),(x,y)},

\displaystyle Y=[\partial_{x}]_{(0,1),(x,y)},

(2.19)

\displaystyle Z_{1}=[x^{N-1}\partial_{y}]_{(0,1),(x,y)},

\displaystyle Z_{2}=[x^{N-2}\partial_{y}]_{(0,2),(x,y)},\cdots,

\displaystyle Z_{N}=[\partial_{y}]_{(0,N),(x,y)}

which are elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{(x,y)}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{(x,y)}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{span}\left(X_{1},X_{2},Y,Z_{1}\right),&\text{if }x\neq 0,\\
\mathrm{span}\left(X_{1},X_{2},Y,Z_{1},Z_{2},\cdots,Z_{N}\right),&\text{if }x=0.\end{cases}

(2.20)

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{(x,y)}
is commutative if
x\neq 0
.
If
x=0
, then the only non-trivial Lie bracket are

\displaystyle=(N-k)Z_{k+1},\quad k\in[\![1,N-1]\!].

Tangent cones at
x\neq 0
:

The tangent cones at
(x,y)
with
x\neq 0
are the linear subspaces:

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}:=\mathrm{span}\{\lambda X_{1}-Y,\lambda x^{N-1}X_{2}-Z_{1}\},\quad L_{\infty}:=\mathrm{span}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
A sequence
((x_{n},y_{n}),(t_{n},s_{n}))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash 0)
converges
(L_{\lambda},(x,y),(0,0))
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
if
x_{n}\to x
,
y_{n}\to y
,
t_{n}\to 0
,
s_{n}\to 0
and
\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\lambda\in[0,+\infty]
.

Tangent cones at
x=0
:

The tangent cones at
(0,y)
are the linear subspaces:

\displaystyle L_{\infty,\mu,\eta}=\mathrm{span}(X_{1},X_{2}-\eta Z_{N},Z_{1}-\mu Z_{2},\cdots,Z_{N-1}-\mu Z_{N}),

\displaystyle L_{\infty}=\mathrm{span}(X_{1},Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{N}),

(2.21)

\displaystyle L_{\infty,\zeta}=\mathrm{span}(X_{1},X_{2}-\zeta Z_{1},Z_{2},\cdots,Z_{N}),

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}:=\mathrm{span}(\lambda X_{1}-Y,Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{N}),

where
\eta,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
or
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
depending on the parity of
N
. A sequence
((x_{n},y_{n}),(t_{n},s_{n}))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash 0)
converges
(L,(0,y),(0,0))
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
if
x_{n}\to 0
,
y_{n}\to y
,
t_{n}\to 0
,
s_{n}\to 0
, and

•

\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\lambda
, if
L=L_{\lambda}
.

•

\frac{t_{n}}{s_{n}^{N}}\to\eta
and
\frac{x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to\mu
, if
L=L_{\infty,\mu,\eta}
.

•

\frac{t_{n}}{s_{n}x_{n}^{N-1}}\to\zeta
and
\frac{x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to\infty
, if
L=L_{\infty,\zeta}
.

•

\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\infty
and
\frac{s_{n}^{N-k}x_{n}^{k}}{t_{n}}\to 0
for all
k\in[\![0,N-1]\!]
, if
L=L_{\infty}
.

In the above conditions, we use the convention that
\frac{1}{0}=\infty
and
\frac{0}{0}=0
.

Topology on
\mathbb{G}
:

The following computation is a direct corollary of
2.3.4
.
A sequence
((x_{n}^{\prime},y_{n}^{\prime}),(x_{n},y_{n}),(t_{n},s_{n}))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash 0)
converges to
(A,(x,y),(0,0))
in
\mathbb{G}
with
x\neq 0
if and only if
(x_{n},y_{n})\to(x,y),(x_{n}^{\prime},y_{n}^{\prime})\to(x,y),(t_{n},s_{n})\to 0
, and the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\frac{x_{n}^{\prime}-x_{n}}{t_{n}}\to a
and
\frac{y_{n}^{\prime}-y_{n}}{t_{n}}\to b
and
\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\lambda\in[0,+\infty[
, if
A=(aX_{1}+bX_{2})L_{\lambda}
.

•

\frac{x_{n}^{\prime}-x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to a
and
\frac{y_{n}^{\prime}-y_{n}}{s_{n}}\to bx^{N-1}
and
\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\infty
, if
A=(aY+bZ_{1})L_{\infty}
.

A sequence
((x_{n}^{\prime},y_{n}^{\prime}),(x_{n},y_{n}),(t_{n},s_{n}))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash 0)
converges to
(A,(0,y),(0,0))
in
\mathbb{G}
if and only if
(x_{n},y_{n})\to(0,y),(x_{n}^{\prime},y_{n}^{\prime})\to(0,y),(t_{n},s_{n})\to 0
, and the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\frac{x_{n}^{\prime}-x_{n}}{t_{n}}\to a
,
\frac{y_{n}^{\prime}-y_{n}}{t_{n}}\to b
and
\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\lambda\in[0,+\infty[
, if
A=(aX_{1}+bX_{2})L_{\lambda}
.

•

\frac{x_{n}^{\prime}-x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to a
,
\frac{y_{n}^{\prime}-y_{n}}{s_{n}^{N}}\to b
,
\frac{t_{n}}{s_{n}^{N}}\to\eta
and
\frac{x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to\mu
, if
A=(aY+bZ_{N})L_{\infty,\mu,\eta}
.

•

\frac{x_{n}^{\prime}-x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to a
,
\frac{y_{n}^{\prime}-y_{n}}{s_{n}x_{n}^{N-1}}\to b
,
\frac{t_{n}}{s_{n}x_{n}^{N-1}}\to\zeta
and
\frac{x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to\infty
, if
A=(aY+bZ_{1})L_{\infty,\zeta}
.

•

\frac{x_{n}^{\prime}-x_{n}}{s_{n}}\to a
,
\frac{y_{n}^{\prime}-y_{n}}{t_{n}}\to b
,
\frac{s_{n}}{t_{n}}\to\infty
,
\frac{s_{n}^{N-k}x_{n}^{k}}{t_{n}}\to 0
for all
k\in[\![0,N-1]\!]
, if
A=(aY+bX_{2})L_{\infty}
.

The above computation illustrates how complicated the groupoid
\mathbb{G}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
can be even in very simple examples.

2.5
Debord-Skandalis actions

In (
2.6
), we defined
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
-dilations on
V
where
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded basis.
In this section, we define some dilations that will be used throughout this article.
Let

\displaystyle\lambda\mu:=(\lambda_{1}\mu_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}\mu_{\nu})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\forall\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\lambda^{-1}:=(\lambda_{1}^{-1},\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}^{-1})\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},

\displaystyle\forall\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}.

This notation will be used throughout the article.

Proposition 2.5.1
.

Let
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
The map

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G},\quad\alpha_{\lambda}(y,x,t)=(y,x,\lambda^{-1}t),\quad\alpha_{\lambda}(A,x,0)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(A),x,0)

is a quasi-Lie groupoid automorphism of
\mathbb{G}
.

By
Section

1.9
,
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
acts on

C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
C^{*}\mathbb{G}
by
*
-algebra automorphisms.

Equivariance of the
C^{*}
-norm:

Since
\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}
is an automorphism of quasi-Lie groupoids, by (
1.43
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}},\quad\forall f\in C^{*}\mathbb{G},\ \lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}.

(2.23)

Equivariance of the
L^{1}
-norm:

Fix a Riemannian metric on
M
. For each
x\in M
, let
\mathrm{vol}_{x}\in\Omega^{1}(T_{x}M)
be the associated
1
-density.
Let
\omega
be the section on
\mathbb{G}
of
\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G})

defined by

\displaystyle\omega(y,x,t)=\mathrm{vol}_{y}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\mathrm{vol}_{x}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\forall(y,x,t)\in M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},

and whose value at
(A,x)\in\mathcal{G}\times\{0\}
is defined as follows:
For some
g\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}
and
L\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}
,
A=gL
.
Recall that
A(\mathbb{G})_{s(A,x,0)}=\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{L}
and
A(\mathbb{G})_{r(A,x,0)}=\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{gLg^{-1}}
.
The linear map

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{L}\to\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{gLg^{-1}},\quad v\mapsto gvg^{-1}

is an isomorphism. Thus, it induces a section of
\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{gLg^{-1}}\right)\otimes\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{g}_{x}}{L}\right)=\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G})_{(A,x,0)}

which is
\omega(A,x,0)
.
Notice that
\omega(A,x,0)
doesn’t depend on the choice of
g
because for any
l\in L
, the map

\mathfrak{g}_{x}/L\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}/L
given by
v\mapsto lvl^{-1}

has determinant
1
because
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra.

Proposition 2.5.2
.

The section
\omega
is smooth, i.e.,
\omega\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}})
and satisfies (
1.27
).

Since
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
is dense in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, it follows that
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{G},\omega)}
is given by

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{G},\omega)}=\sup\left\{\max\left(\int_{M}|f|(y,x,t)\mathrm{vol}_{y}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{vol}_{x}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\int_{M}|f|(x,y,t)\mathrm{vol}_{y}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{vol}_{x}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right\},

(2.24)

where the supremum is over
(x,t)\in M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
.
By (
2.24
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{G},\omega)}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{G},\omega)},\quad\forall\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},\ f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(2.25)

Remark 2.5.3
.

An amusing consequence of (
2.24
) is that if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then the RHS of (
2.24
) is finite.
This is not obvious at all at first sight. We warn the reader though that if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
is not density-valued, then

\sup\left\{\int_{M}|f|(y,x,t)\mathrm{vol}_{y}:(x,t)\in M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\right\}

is usually infinite. Finiteness of the RHS of (
2.24
) is an interplay between the topologies on
\mathbb{G}
and on
A(\mathbb{G})
.

2.6
Invariant smooth functions

Summary:

In
Chapter

4
, we will define
\nu
-graded pseudo-differential operators as integrals.
To study such integrals, it is necessary that if
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then the maps

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad\lambda\mapsto g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\quad\text{and }\quad\lambda\mapsto\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast g

(2.26)

extend to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
. The extension in general doesn’t exist.
In this section, we define a subalgebra of the convolution algebra
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
which we call the space of invariant functions.
The main property of interest is that for such functions (
2.26
) extend smoothly to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.

Invariant functions:

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis.
By (
2.17
) with
k=l=\frac{1}{2}
and (
1.11
), we have an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Since a submersion is open, by
Section

2.3.2
.
v
,
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is open.
So,
\im(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
is an open neighborhood of
\mathcal{G}\times\{0\}
. Since
\mathbb{G}=\im(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\cup(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\})
, by
1.1.2
, for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, there exists
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))

such that

f-\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Definition 2.6.1
.

The space of invariant
2
2

2

Invariant functions where essentially defined by Androulidakis and Skandalis in
[
4
]
. We remark that the map
\tilde{f}\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
is essentially an
X
-ray transform.
Such transforms in the case of Lie groups have been extensively studied, see for example
[
29
]
functions
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is the space of
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

such that

\displaystyle f-\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\text{ for some }\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V)).

(2.27)

We equip
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
with the quotient topology from the surjective map

\displaystyle q_{V}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))\times C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

(2.28)

\displaystyle(\tilde{f},\accentset{\approx}{f})\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})+\accentset{\approx}{f}.

Theorem 2.6.2
.

i.

The space
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and its topology don’t depend on the choice of a
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
.

ii.

The space
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is an LF-space, and the inclusion
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is continuous.

iii.

The space
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a
*
-subalgebra of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

iv.

If
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Definition 2.6.3
.

We denote by
C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
the space of all
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that

\displaystyle u\ast C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\ast u\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(2.29)

The space
C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a
*
-subalgebra of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. Furthermore, if
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
and
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\alpha_{\lambda}(u)\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Remark 2.6.4
.

If
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
u\ast\cdot:C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is continuous by the closed graph theorem and
1.6.2
, and
Section

2.6.2
.
ii
.

Theorem 2.6.5
.

i.

If
u\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})
, then
\delta_{u\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

ii.

If
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
, then
\theta_{k}(X)\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

In
2.6.5
, we are using the notation of
1.6.3
.

Theorem 2.6.6
.

For any
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, the functions

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad\lambda\mapsto\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast g\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda\mapsto g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)

extend to smooth functions
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
whose values at
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
are denoted by

\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast g
and
g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)
.
Furthermore, if
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then the extensions are smooth functions
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So,
\alpha_{\lambda}(f)
defines an element of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(f)=\delta_{u\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}},\quad u(x,t)=\int_{V}\tilde{f}(v,x,0)\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}),

(2.30)

where
\tilde{f}
is any function as in (
2.27
).

2.7
Multi-grading on the algebra of differential operators

Notation and convention:

The space
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
denotes
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(TM))
, and
C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
denotes the topological dual of
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
This convention will be used throughout the article.

Grading on differential operators:

Let
\mathrm{DO}(M)
be the algebra of finite order differential operators
D:C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By finite order, we mean that there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
x\in M
,
D(f)(x)

only depends on the partial derivatives of
f
at
x
of order
\leq n
.
If
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then the differential operator
g\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\mapsto fg\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
will be denoted by
\delta_{f}
to be consistent with the notation introduced in
Section

1.6.3
.
ii
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, we denote by
\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
the
\mathbb{C}
-linear span of monomials
X_{1}\cdots X_{n}
such that
X_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{a(i)}
with
a(1)+\cdots+a(n)\preceq k
.
A monomial of length
0
is by convention
\delta_{f}
where
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, i.e.,
\mathrm{DO}^{0}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)
.
By our assumption of finite order and (
2.4
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{DO}(M)=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M).

Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{DO}_{c}(M)=C_{c}^{\infty}(M)\mathrm{DO}(M),\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{DO}^{\prec k}(M):=\sum_{l\prec k}\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)

\displaystyle\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{c}(M)=C^{\infty}_{c}(M)\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M),\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{DO}^{\prec k}_{c}(M)=C^{\infty}_{c}(M)\mathrm{DO}^{\prec k}(M).

The proof of the following proposition is straightforward and left to the reader.

Proposition 2.7.1
.

i.

If
k\preceq l
, then
\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)\subseteq\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
.

ii.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)\subseteq\mathrm{DO}^{k+l}(M)
.

iii.

If
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then
[D,\delta_{f}]\in\mathrm{DO}^{\prec k}(M)
.

iv.

If
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
, then its formal adjoint
D^{*}
and formal transpose
D^{t}
belong to
\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
.

v.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
is a finitely generated
C^{\infty}(M)
-module.

In
Section

2.7.1
.
iv
, the formal adjoint and formal transpose are canonically defined because we are working with half-densities.
So,
X^{t}=X^{*}=-X
for any vector field
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
.

Differential operators as distributions on
\mathbb{G}
:

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.
By
Section

2.6.5
.
ii
, we have a linear map

\theta_{k}:\mathcal{F}^{k}\to C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We extend
\theta_{k}
to a linear map

\displaystyle\theta_{k}:\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)\to C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

For monomials of length
0
, i.e.,
\delta_{f}
where
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we set

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(\delta_{f}):=\delta_{g\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad g(x,t):=t^{k}f(x)\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}).

Here, we use
Section

2.6.5
.
i
.
For monomials of non-zero length, we use the formula

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(X_{1}\cdots X_{n})=\theta_{k-\sum_{i}a(i)}(\delta_{1})\ast\theta_{a(1)}(X_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\theta_{a(n)}(X_{n}),

We define
\theta_{k}
for general differential operators using linearity by writing
D
as sum of monomials.

Theorem 2.7.2
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, the map
\theta_{k}
is well-defined, i.e.,
\theta_{k}(D)
doesn’t depend on the presentation of
D
as a sum of monomials.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\theta_{k_{1}+k_{2}}(D_{1}D_{2})=\theta_{k_{1}}(D_{1})\ast\theta_{k_{2}}(D_{2}).

(2.31)

It is useful to remark that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(\theta_{k}(D))=\lambda^{k}\theta_{k}(D),\quad\theta_{k}(D)^{*}=\theta_{k}(D^{*}),\quad\forall D\in\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M).

(2.32)

2.7.2
is far from trivial. When restricted to
\mathcal{G}
,
\theta_{k}(D)
is in some sense the principal symbol of
D
.
One can define this restriction without talking at all about the groupoid
\mathbb{G}
.
Independence of this symbol on the presentation of
D
as a sum of monomial is on the other hand not at all clear unless one has access to the groupoid
\mathbb{G}
in which case the independence is trivial to prove!

Invariant functions whose Fourier transform vanish at the origin:

In the study of the principal symbol of pseudo-differential operators, one often needs to consider the space of compactly supported smooth functions whose Fourier transform vanishes at the origin to a certain order.
We now define an analogue of such spaces for our differential space
\mathbb{G}
.

Definition 2.7.3
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, let

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{k_{i}}(D_{i})\ast f_{i}:k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},k\preceq k_{i},D_{i}\in\mathrm{DO}^{k_{i}}(M),f_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\right\}

(2.33)

Since
\theta_{0}(\delta_{1})=\delta_{1}
,
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},0}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Notice that by (
2.31
), if
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M))\ast C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

(2.34)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

\displaystyle\text{if }k\preceq l.

Theorem 2.7.4
.

i.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

ii.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, we have

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\ast C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})^{*}=C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

iii.

If
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\ast\theta_{k_{i}}(D_{i}):k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},k\preceq k_{i},D_{i}\in\mathrm{DO}^{k_{i}}(M),f_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\right\}

(2.35)

Remark 2.7.5
.

If
\nu\geq 2
, then functions in
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
satisfy some vanishing conditions away from the fiber at
t=0
.
For example, if
k_{1}\neq 0
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
f(y,x,(0,t))=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu-1}_{+}\backslash 0
and
y,x\in M
.
In particular,
C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\nsubseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

The following proposition will be used in
Chapter

4
.

Proposition 2.7.6
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(k_{1},\cdots,k_{i-1},\max(1,k_{i}),k_{i+1},\cdots,k_{\nu})}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

2.8
Weakly commutative sub-Riemannian structure

The following condition was introduced by Street
[
51
]
.

Definition 2.8.1
.

A
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is called weakly commutative if

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{k}=\mathcal{F}^{(k_{1},0,\cdots,0)}+\cdots+\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,k_{\nu})},\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}

(2.36)

We suppose for the rest of this section that
\mathcal{F}
is weakly commutative.
If
V=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}V^{k}
is a
\nu
-graded vector space, then we denote by

\displaystyle V^{\hat{i}}:=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}V^{(\overbrace{0,\cdots,0}^{i-1},j,\overbrace{0\cdots,0}^{\nu-i})},\quad i\in[\![1,\nu]\!].

By (
2.36
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\hat{i}},\quad\forall x\in M.

(2.37)

Furthermore,
[\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\hat{i}},\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{\hat{j}}]=0
for all
i,j\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
with
i\neq j
.

Examples 2.8.2
.

i.

Any
1
-graded sub-Riemannian structure is weakly commutative. Similarly, any
2
-graded sub-Riemannian structure of depth
(1,N)
is weakly commutative.

ii.

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{d}
and
Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{d^{\prime}}
be two families of vector fields such that each family satisfies Hörmander’s condition of depth
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
respectively,
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}

the
2
-graded sub-Riemannian structure of depth
(N,N^{\prime})
generated by the two families as in
Section

2.1.3
.
iii
.
Then,
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is weakly commutative if and only if

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}f_{i}X_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}g_{j}Y_{j},\quad\forall k\in[\![1,d]\!],l\in[\![1,d^{\prime}]\!],

(2.38)

for some
f_{i},g_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
.
This is automatic for example if one of the two families spans the tangent space without need for commutators.
Another example is
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the families are
\{\partial_{x},x^{k}\partial_{y}\}
and
\{\partial_{y},y^{l}\partial_{x}\}
.

The following theorem is false without the weak-commutativity condition.
It is needed to ensure that our pseudo-differential calculus defined in
Chapter

4
is closed under composition.

Theorem 2.8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
be a weakly commutative
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure,
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
For any
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
, if
\lambda+\mu\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, then

\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast\alpha_{\mu}(g)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Furthermore, the following function is smooth:

\displaystyle\{(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2\nu}:\lambda+\nu\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\}\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\quad(\lambda,\mu)\mapsto\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast\alpha_{\mu}(g).

(2.39)

Weak-commutativity also allows one to build smooth functions on
\mathbb{G}
from smaller groupoids as follows:
Let
\tilde{\nu},\accentset{\approx}{\nu}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\tilde{\nu}+\accentset{\approx}{\nu}=\nu
.
We define a
\tilde{\nu}
-graded
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
and a
\accentset{\approx}{\nu}
-graded
\accentset{\approx}{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet}
sub-Riemannian structures on
M
by the formulae

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{k}:=\mathcal{F}^{(k,0,\cdots,0)},\quad\accentset{\approx}{\mathcal{F}}^{l}:=\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,l)},\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\tilde{\nu}},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}.

(2.40)

So, by (
2.36
),
\mathcal{F}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\accentset{\approx}{\mathcal{F}}
are connected by the equation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(k,l)}=\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{k}+\accentset{\approx}{\mathcal{F}}^{l},\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\tilde{\nu}},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}.

(2.41)

Let
\tilde{\mathbb{G}}\rightrightarrows\tilde{\mathbb{G}}^{(0)}
and
\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}}\rightrightarrows\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}}^{(0)}
be the associated quasi-Lie groupoids.

Theorem 2.8.4
.

There exists a natural
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear map (thought of as a convolution product)

\displaystyle\Phi:C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\times C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

which is uniquely determined by the identity

\displaystyle\Phi(f,g)(z,x,t,s):=\int_{M}f(z,y,t)g(y,x,s),\quad\forall(z,x,t,s)\in M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\tilde{\nu}}\backslash 0)\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}\backslash 0),

(2.42)

where
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\alpha_{(\lambda,\mu)}(\Phi(f,g))=\Phi(\alpha_{\lambda}(f),\alpha_{\mu}(g)),\quad\Phi(C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(k,l)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

(2.43)

where
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\tilde{\nu}},\mu\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}
,
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\tilde{\nu}},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}
.

By Symmetry, we can also define a map (still denoted by
\Phi
)

\displaystyle\Phi:C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\times C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

which is uniquely determined by the identity

\displaystyle\Phi(g,f)(z,x,t,s):=\int_{M}g(z,y,s)f(y,x,t),\quad\forall(z,x,t,s)\in M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\tilde{\nu}}\backslash 0)\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}\backslash 0),

(2.44)

where
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, and which satisfies
similar identities to those in (
2.43
).
The next result says that
\Phi(f,g)
and
\Phi(g,f)
are equal up to lower order terms

Theorem 2.8.5
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
there exists invariant functions
h_{1},\cdots,h_{\tilde{\nu}},h^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,h^{\prime}_{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,g)-\Phi(g,f)=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{\nu}}t_{i}h_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}s_{i}h_{i}^{\prime}

(2.45)

Chapter 3
Proofs of the results in Chapter
2

The following proposition will be used throughout this chapter.
Its proof is left to the reader.

Proposition 3.0.1
.

Let
V
be a vector space,
d\in[\![0,\dim(V)]\!]
,
\mathrm{Grass}_{d}(V)
the Grassmannian manifold of linear subspaces of
V
of dimension
d
,
(L_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathrm{Grass}_{d}(V)
a sequence,
L\in\mathrm{Grass}_{d}(V)
.
The following are equivalent:

i.

The sequence
(L_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
L
in the Grassmannian topology.

ii.

For every sequence
(l_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
such that
l_{n}\in L_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
l_{n}
converges to a vector
v\in V
, then
v\in L
.

Furthermore, if
L_{n}\to L
in the Grassmannian topology, then for any
l\in L
, there exists a sequence
l_{n}\in L_{n}
such that
l_{n}\to l
.

If
(V,\natural)
and
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})
are
\nu
-graded basis, then we write
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})\subseteq(V,\natural)
if
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
\natural_{|V^{\prime}}=\natural^{\prime}
.
We call an element
v\in V
pure if
v\in V^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.

3.1
Proofs of the results in Section
2.2

Proof of
2.2.2
.

The map
\natural_{x,0}
preserves the
\nu
-grading on
V
and
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
. Hence,
\ker(\natural_{x,0})
is a
\nu
-graded subspace of
V
.
So, it is enough to show that if
v\in V^{k}\cap\ker(\natural_{x,0})
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then
v\in L
.
Since
\natural_{x,0}(v)=0
,
\natural(v)\in\mathcal{F}^{\prec k}+C^{\infty}_{x}(M,\mathbb{R})\mathcal{F}^{k}
.
By the definition of a
\nu
-graded basis, we can write
\natural(v)
as a sum over some finite index sets
I
and
J

\displaystyle\natural(v)=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\natural(w_{i})+\sum_{j\in J}f_{j}^{\prime}\natural(w_{j}^{\prime})

where
w_{i}\in V^{a(i)}
for some
a(i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
with
a(i)\prec k
, and
w^{\prime}_{j}\in V^{k}
,
f_{i},f^{\prime}_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
such that
f^{\prime}_{j}(x)=0
.
It follows that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle v_{n}:=v-\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}(x_{n})t_{n}^{k-a(i)}w_{i}-\sum_{j\in J}f^{\prime}_{j}(x_{n})w^{\prime}_{j}\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}).

Since
v_{n}\to v
, by
3.0.1
, we deduce that
v\in L
.

∎

Proof of
2.2.5
.

The map
\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
is obviously continuous.
It is surjective because
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}_{x}\neq\emptyset
for every
x\in M
.
It is proper because
\mathrm{Grass}(V)
is compact.
The inclusion
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is easily seen to be an open smooth embedding.
Its image is dense by the definition of tangent cones.
It remains to show
Section

2.2.5
.
i
.
It is clear that
(V\oplus V^{\prime},\natural\oplus\natural^{\prime})
is a
\nu
-graded basis.
We will construct closed smooth embeddings

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to\mathrm{Grass}(V\oplus V^{\prime})\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}:\mathrm{Grass}(V^{\prime})\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to\mathrm{Grass}(V\oplus V^{\prime})\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}

such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grass}(V\oplus V^{\prime})\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grass}(V^{\prime})\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grass}(\natural^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Grass}(\natural\oplus\natural^{\prime})}

(3.1)

commutes. The existence of
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
finishes the proof of
Section

2.2.5
.
i
.

By symmetry, it is enough to construct
\Phi^{\prime}
.
We claim that we can find a smooth map
T:V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V^{\prime}
which is linear in
V
such that

\displaystyle\natural^{\prime}_{x,t}(T(v,x,t))=\natural_{x,t}(v),\quad\forall(v,x,t)\in V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}.

(3.2)

Notice that in (
3.2
),
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
So, (
3.2
) is an equality between elements of
T_{x}M
if
t\neq 0
and between elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
if
t=0
.
It is enough to define
T
on a linear basis of
V
and then extend
T
by linearity.
We fix a basis of
V
of pure elements.
If
v\in V^{k}
is a basis element, then by the definition of a
\nu
-graded basis, there exists a finite family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
,
(v_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq V^{\prime}
such that
v_{i}\in V^{\prime a(i)}
with
a(i)\preceq k
and

\natural(v)=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\natural^{\prime}(v_{i})
.
We define
T(v,x,t)
by the formula

\displaystyle T(v,x,t)=\sum_{i\in I}t^{k-a(i)}f_{i}(x)v_{i},

(3.3)

We leave it to the reader to check that (
3.2
) holds.
We extend
T
to a map
T:(V\oplus V^{\prime})\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V^{\prime}
which is linear in
V\oplus V^{\prime}
by the formula

T(v+v^{\prime},x,t)=T(v,x,t)+v^{\prime}
.
The map
T(\cdot,x,t)
is surjective for any
x,t
.
Hence, we can define the map

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(L,x,t)=(T(\cdot,x,t)^{-1}(L),x,t)

where
T(\cdot,x,t)^{-1}(L):=\{v+v^{\prime}\in V\oplus V^{\prime}:T(v+v^{\prime},x,t)\in L\}
.
Surjectivity of
T(\cdot,x,t)
ensures that
\Phi^{\prime}
is a well-defined closed embedding.
Commutativity of (
3.1
) follows from the identity

\natural_{x,t}^{\prime}(T(v+v^{\prime},x,t))=\natural_{x,t}(v)+\natural^{\prime}_{x,t}(v^{\prime})
.

∎

Proof of
2.2.8
.

We embed the space
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
inside
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
using
\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)
.
There is a natural real vector bundle
E\to\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
whose fiber over
(L,x,t)
is
V/L
.
Furthermore, there is an obvious smooth submersion
V\times\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to E
.
The restriction of
E
to
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is
A(\mathbb{G})
and the obvious projection map from
V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to A(\mathbb{G}^{(0)})
gets identified with
A(\natural)
.
From this we deduce
Section

2.2.8
.
i
and
Section

2.2.8
.
ii
.

Section

2.2.8
.
iii
is proved by an obvious modification of the proof of
Section

2.2.5
.
i
.
The details are left to the reader.

∎

Proof of
2.2.9
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis. By the definition of a
\nu
-graded basis,
X=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\natural(v_{i})
where
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
,
v_{i}\in V^{a(i)}
with
a(i)\preceq k
.
So, for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{G}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(X)(\gamma)=\sum_{i\in I}\big(g_{i}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(\gamma)\big)\theta_{a(i)}(v_{i})(\gamma)=\sum_{i\in I}\big(g_{i}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(\gamma)\big)A(\natural)(v_{i},\gamma),

where
g_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})
are defined by
g_{i}(x,t)=t^{k-a(i)}f_{i}(x)
.
Hence,
\theta_{k}(X)
is a smooth section of
A(\mathbb{G}^{(0)})
.
It is clear from the description of
A(\mathbb{G})
in the proof of
2.2.8
that the family
(\theta_{k}(v))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},v\in V^{k}}
generates the
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},\mathbb{R})
-module
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}^{(0)},A(\mathbb{G}))
.

∎

Multi-graded Lie basis:

Throughout this article, we sometimes need to lift the Lie bracket from the osculating Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
to
\nu
-graded basis. For this reason, we introduce the following notion:
A
\nu
-graded Lie basis is a
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
such that
V
is equipped with a Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]:V\times V\to V
such that

[V^{k},V^{l}]\subseteq V^{k+l}
,
and

\displaystyle-\natural([v,w])\in\mathcal{F}^{\prec k+l},\quad\forall v\in V^{k},w\in V^{l}.

(3.4)

Notice that (
3.4
) is trivially true if
k+l\npreceq N
because by (
2.4
),
\mathcal{F}^{\prec k+l}=\mathcal{X}(M)
.
Since
V
is finite dimensional,
V^{k}\neq 0
for only a finite number of
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.
Together with
V^{0}=0
, we deduce that the Lie algebra
V
is nilpotent

Lemma 3.1.1
.

If
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})
is a
\nu
-graded basis, then there exists a
\nu
-graded Lie basis
(V,\natural)
such that
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})\subseteq(V,\natural)
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a linear basis of
V^{\prime}
consisting of pure elements.
Let
W
be the free Lie algebra on
S
and
\natural_{W}:W\to\mathcal{X}(M)
the Lie algebra extension of the function
\natural_{|S}:S\to\mathcal{X}(M)
.
We also have a natural inclusion
V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow W
.
The space
W
inherits a
\nu
-grading from
S
making it an infinite dimensional
\nu
-graded Lie algebra
W=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}W^{k}
.
By (
2.5
),
\natural(W^{k})\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k}
.
Since
V^{\prime}
is finite dimensional, there exists
L\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
such that
V^{\prime}\subseteq\oplus_{k\preceq L}W^{k}
.
By replacing
L
with
L+N
, we can assume that
N\preceq L
.
Let
V=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},k\preceq L}W^{k}
.
We equip
V
with a Lie bracket by identifying it with
W/\oplus_{k\npreceq L}W^{k}
, and using the fact that
\oplus_{k\npreceq L}W^{k}
is an ideal of
W
.
Equivalently, the lie bracket in
V
of
v\in W^{k}
and
w\in W^{l}
is defined by the formula

\displaystyle{}_{V}=\begin{cases}[v,w]_{W}&\text{ if }k+l\preceq L\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

The pair
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
It is a
\nu
-graded basis because
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})\subseteq(V,\natural)
.
It satisfies a stronger form of (
3.4
), namely that

[\natural(v),\natural(w)]_{V}=\natural([v,w]_{V}),\quad\forall v\in V^{k},w\in V^{l}\text{ such that }k+l\preceq L.\qed

Remark 3.1.2
.

If
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis, then for any
x\in M
, by (
3.4
), the linear map
\natural_{x,0}:V\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}
a Lie algebra and a Lie group homomorphism.

Proof of
2.2.10
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
We claim that there exists a smooth map
T:V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V
which is bilinear in
V\times V
such that

\displaystyle\natural_{x,t}(T(v_{1},v_{2},x,t))=[\natural(\alpha_{t}(v_{1})),\natural(\alpha_{t}(v_{2}))](x),\quad

\displaystyle\forall(v_{1},v_{2},x,t)\in V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}

(3.5)

\displaystyle T(v_{1},v_{2},x,0)=[v_{1},v_{2}],\quad

\displaystyle\forall(v_{1},v_{2},x)\in V\times V\times M.

By bilinearity, it is enough to define
T
on a basis of
V
. We fix a basis of pure elements.
Let
v_{1}\in V^{k_{1}}
and
v_{2}\in V^{k_{2}}
be basis elements. By (
3.4
), there exists
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
and
w_{i}\in V^{a(i)}
such that
a(i)\prec k_{1}+k_{2}
and

\displaystyle=\natural([v_{1},v_{2}])+\sum_{i}f_{i}\natural(w_{i})

We define

\displaystyle T(v_{1},v_{2},x,t)=[v_{1},v_{2}]+\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)t^{k_{1}+k_{2}-a(i)}w_{i}

(3.6)

It is straightforward to check that
T
satisfies (
3.5
).

Let
L\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}
be a tangent cone at
x
.
So, there exists
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
such that
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
and

\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.
Since
\natural_{x,0}:V\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a Lie algebra homomorphism, it suffices to prove that
\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
is a Lie subalgebra of
V
.
Let
v,w\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.
By
3.0.1
, there exists sequences
v_{n},w_{n}\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})
such that
v_{n}\to v
and
w_{n}\to w
.
The vector fields
\natural(\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n}))
and
\natural(\alpha_{t_{n}}(w_{n}))
vanish at
x_{n}
.
So, their Lie bracket vanishes at
x_{n}
.
Hence,
T(v_{n},w_{n},x_{n},t_{n})\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})
.
By continuity of
T
and
3.0.1
,
[v,w]=T(v,w,x,0)\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.

We now prove
Section

2.2.10
.
ii
.
Recall that the tangent space
T_{L}\mathrm{Grass}(V)
of the Grassmannian manifold
\mathrm{Grass}(V)
at a subspace
L\subseteq V
naturally identifies with
\Hom(L,V/L)
as follows: Let
(L_{t})_{t\in]-\epsilon,\epsilon[}\subseteq\mathrm{Grass}(V)
be a smooth path of subspaces for some
\epsilon>0
with
L_{0}=L
.
The derivative
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}L_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rvert_{t=0}
is the linear map
L\to V/L
defined by

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}L_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rvert_{t=0}(l)=c^{\prime}(0)\bmod L,

(3.7)

where
c:]-\epsilon,\epsilon[\to V
is any smooth path such that
c(t)\in L_{t}
for all
t\in]-\epsilon,\epsilon[
and
c(0)=l
.
Notice that
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}L_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rvert_{t=0}(l)
doesn’t depend on the choice of
c
because if
c
is a smooth path such that
c(t)\in L_{t}
for all
t
and
c(0)=0
, then
c^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{c(t)}{t}\in L
by
3.0.1
.

Now, let
v\in V
be fixed. On the manifold
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
, we define the vector field

\displaystyle Z(L,x,t)=(w\mapsto T(w,v,x,t)\bmod L,\natural(\alpha_{t}(v))(x),0)\in T_{(L,x,t)}\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}.

On
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\{0\}
,
Z(L,x,0)=(w\mapsto[w,v]\bmod L,0,0)
.
So,
1
1

1

Here, the sign convention from (
2.2
) is used.

\displaystyle e^{Z}\cdot(L,x,0)=(vLv^{-1},x,0).

We now see the space
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
as a subspace of
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
using the map
\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)
from (
2.11
).
To prove
Section

2.2.10
.
ii
, it suffices to show that the flow of
Z
leaves invariant the subset
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
.
The set
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
is the limit set of
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that the flow of
Z
preserves
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
.
In fact
Z
is tangent to
M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
.
To see this, we compute the differential of
\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)
at a point
(x,t)\in M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
.
We claim that for any
\xi\in T_{x}M
,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)_{(x,t)}(\xi,0)=(\phi,\xi,0)
where
\phi:\ker(\natural_{x,t})\to V/\ker(\natural_{x,t})
is the unique linear map which satisfies

\displaystyle\natural_{x,t}(\phi(w))=[\natural(\alpha_{t}(w)),X](x),\quad\forall w\in\ker(\natural_{x,t})

where
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
is any vector field which satisfies
X(x)=\xi
.
The claim finishes the proof of
Section

2.2.10
.
ii
because by (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)_{(x,t)}(\natural(\alpha_{t}(v))(x),0)=Z(\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)(x,t)).

Let us prove our claim.
We fix
x_{0}\in M
,
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0
,
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
.
Let
v_{1},\cdots,v_{\dim(M)}\in V
such that
\natural_{x_{0},t_{0}}(v_{1}),\cdots,\natural_{x_{0},t_{0}}(v_{\dim(M)})
form a linear basis of
T_{x_{0}}M
.
So, the vector fields
\natural(\alpha_{t_{0}}(v_{1})),\cdots,\natural(\alpha_{t_{0}}(v_{\dim(M)}))
linearly span the tangent bundle in a neighborhood
U
of
x_{0}
.
For any
w\in V
,
\natural(\alpha_{t_{0}}(w))=\sum_{i}f_{i}\natural(\alpha_{t_{0}}(v_{i}))
for some unique smooth functions
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(U)
.
For any
x\in U
, a generic element of
\ker(\natural_{x,t_{0}})
is then given by
w-\sum_{i}f_{i}(x)v_{i}
.
By the identification of the tangent space of Grassmannian manifolds in (
3.7
),
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)_{(x_{0},t_{0})}(X(x_{0}),0)=(\phi,X(x_{0}),0)
where
\phi
is the linear map

\displaystyle w-\sum_{i}f_{i}(x_{0})v_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(w-\sum_{i}f_{i}(e^{tX}\cdot x_{0})v_{i}\right)\right\rvert_{t=0}=-\sum_{i}X(f_{i})(x_{0})v_{i}\mod\ker(\natural_{x_{0},t_{0}}).

We have

\displaystyle\left[\natural\left(\alpha_{t_{0}}\left(w-\sum_{i}f_{i}(x_{0})v_{i}\right)\right),X\right](x_{0})

\displaystyle=[\natural(\alpha_{t_{0}}(w)),X](x_{0})-\sum_{i}f_{i}(x_{0})[\natural(\alpha_{t_{0}}(v_{i})),X](x_{0})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}X(f_{i})(x_{0})\natural_{x_{0},t_{0}}(v_{i}).

The claim follows.

∎

While proving
Section

2.2.10
.
ii
, we also proved the following:

Lemma 3.1.3
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis. The following function is smooth

\displaystyle r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)}

\displaystyle(v,x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)

\displaystyle(v,L,x,0)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\natural_{x,0}(v)L\natural_{x,0}(v)^{-1},x,0).

Proof.

The map
r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
for a fixed
v
is the restriction to
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
of the flow at time
1
of
Z
.
Since
Z
depends smoothly on
v
, it follows that
r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is smooth.
∎

3.2
Key lemmas

Summary:

We will prove two Lemmas. The first allows us to pass from one
\nu
-graded basis to another. The second lifts composition on
\mathbb{G}
to
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
Both lemmas can be seen as refinements of the maps
T
constructed in the proofs of
2.2.5
and
2.2.10
.
The Lemmas are proved in the reverse order.

Notation and convention:

Throughout this Chapter and
Chapter

5
, we will make use of the following auxiliary
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-actions.
Let
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
Recall the notation from (
2.22
).
We define

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(x,t)=(x,\lambda^{-1}t)

(3.8)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(v,x,t)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),\alpha_{\lambda}(x,t))

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(v,w,x,t)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),\alpha_{\lambda}(w),\alpha_{\lambda}(x,t))

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(x,t)=(x,\lambda^{-1}t),\quad\alpha_{\lambda}(L,x,0)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(L),x,0)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(v,\gamma)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),\alpha_{\lambda}(\gamma))

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}:V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(v,w,\gamma)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),\alpha_{\lambda}(w),\alpha_{\lambda}(\gamma))

To summarize,
\alpha_{\lambda}
acts diagonally on all variables. It acts trivially on the variable
M
, on
V
by the dilation from (
2.6
), on
\mathrm{Grass}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})
using the dilation on
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
by
t\mapsto\lambda^{-1}t
.

Remark 3.2.1
.

The map
\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is well-defined diffeomorphism. To check that it is well-defined, we need to show that if
L\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is a tangent cone at
x
, then
\alpha_{\lambda}(L)
is also a tangent cone at
x
.
This is because if
(x_{n},t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
is a sequence such that
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
,
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
, then

\ker(\natural_{x_{n},\lambda^{-1}t_{n}})=\alpha_{\lambda}(\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}))\to\alpha_{\lambda}(L)
.
The map
\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is a diffeomorphism because it is the restriction of the diffeomorphism
\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
given by
(L,x,t)\mapsto(\alpha_{\lambda}(L),x,\lambda^{-1}t)
.

We will usually have partially defined maps
f:\dom(f)\subseteq X\to Y
between spaces
X
and
Y
which are equipped with
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-actions.
We will say that
f
is equivariant if
\dom(f)
is equivariant and
f(\alpha_{\lambda}(x))=\alpha_{\lambda}(f(x))
for all
x\in\dom(f)
and
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
(V,\natural)
and
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})
be
\nu
-graded basis such that
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})\subseteq(V,\natural)
. There exists smooth equivariant submersions

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}:\dom(\tilde{\phi})\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad\phi:\dom(\phi)\subseteq V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)},

defined on open neighborhoods of
V\times M\times\{0\}
and
V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
respectively such that:

i.

If
(v,x)\in V^{\prime}\times M
, then
\tilde{\phi}(v,x,0)=(v,x,0)
.
2
2

2

In fact, the map we construct satisfies the stronger condition that
\tilde{\phi}(v,x,t)=(v,x,t)
for all
(v,x,t)\in(V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})\cap\dom(\tilde{\phi})
. We won’t need this.

ii.

One has

\displaystyle\dom(\tilde{\phi})\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\quad\im(\tilde{\phi})\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}),\quad\dom(\phi)\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\quad\im(\phi)\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}})

(3.9)

and the following diagram commutes

(3.10)

iii.

One has
\dom(\phi)=\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\dom(\tilde{\phi}))
, and the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom(\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom(\tilde{\phi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\phi}}

(3.11)

is a pullback diagram.

To summarize, the map
\phi
allows one to pass from
\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
. Furthermore, it is the pullback of some submersion
\tilde{\phi}
which doesn’t live on the blow up (and thus we can use standard differential geometry tools like the inverse mapping theorem on
\tilde{\phi}
).

To state the following lemma, we need a few preliminaries: Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
We wish to lift the product map
\mathbb{G}^{(2)}\to\mathbb{G}
through
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\{(v,\gamma),(w,\eta)\in\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\times\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}):(\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,\gamma),\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\eta))\in\mathbb{G}^{(2)}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(v,\gamma),(w,\eta)\in\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\times\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}):\gamma=r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\eta)\}.

So, this set can be identified with the following subset of
V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})=\{(v,w,\eta)\in V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}:(v,r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\eta)),(w,\eta)\in\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\}.

Recall that
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})=\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}))
. Similarly, if we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})

\displaystyle=\{(v,w,x,t)\in V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,t),(w,x,t)\in\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\},

then
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})=\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}))
where
\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}:V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
is the natural projection, see (
2.16
).
The set
\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})
is open because
\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
is open.
Since
\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
is continuous,
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})
is open.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})\subseteq V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}

(3.12)

\displaystyle(v,w,x,t)\mapsto(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,x,t)

\displaystyle(v,w,L,x,0)\mapsto(\natural_{x,0}(v\cdot w)L,x,0),

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}(v,w,\gamma)=\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\gamma))\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\gamma)

(3.13)

The sets
\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})
,
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})
and the maps
\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}
are
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
There exists equivariant smooth submersions

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}:\dom(\tilde{\psi})\subseteq V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad\psi:\dom(\psi)\subseteq V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}

defined on some open neighborhood of
V\times V\times M\times\{0\}
and
V\times V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
such that:

i.

For any
(v,w,x)\in V\times V\times M
,
\tilde{\psi}(v,w,x,0)=(v\cdot w,x,0)
.

ii.

For any
(v,x,t)\in V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
, we have
\tilde{\psi}(v,0,x,t)=\tilde{\psi}(0,v,x,t)=(v,x,t)
whenever
(v,0,x,t)
or
(0,v,x,t)
is in the domain of
\tilde{\psi}
.

iii.

One has,

\displaystyle\dom(\tilde{\psi})\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}),\ \im(\tilde{\psi})\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\ \dom(\psi)\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}),\ \im(\psi)\subseteq\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),

(3.14)

and the following diagram commutes
