(3.15)

iv.

One has
\dom(\psi)=\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\dom(\tilde{\psi}))
and the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom(\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dom(\tilde{\psi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\psi}}

(3.16)

is a pullback diagram.

Remark 3.2.4
.

Commutativity of (
3.15
) and that Diagram (
3.16
) imply that
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
are of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(v,w,x,t)=(\hat{\psi}(v,w,x,t),x,t),\quad\psi(v,w,\gamma)=(\hat{\psi}(v,w,\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(\gamma)),\gamma)

(3.17)

for some map
\hat{\psi}:\dom(\tilde{\psi})\to V
which satisfies

\displaystyle e^{\alpha_{t}(\hat{\psi}(v,w,x,t))}\cdot x=e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,\text{ and }\hat{\psi}(v,w,x,0)=v\cdot w.

(3.18)

In some proofs later on in this section, we will make use of the map
\hat{\psi}
.

Throughout this chapter, we will be consistent in reserving
\tilde{\phi},\phi
for the maps obtained by applying
3.2.2
and
\tilde{\psi},\psi
for the maps obtained by applying
3.2.3
.

Proof of
3.2.3
.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

To prove
3.2.3
, it is enough to find an
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant open neighborhood
U\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
of
V\times M\times\{0\}
and a linear map

w\in V\mapsto A_{w}\in\mathcal{X}(U)
such that:

i.

For any
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
,
w\in V
,
\alpha_{\lambda*}(A_{w})=A_{\alpha_{\lambda}(w)}
.

ii.

If
(v,x)\in V\times M
, then
A_{w}(v,x,0)=\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\BCH(\tau w,v)\right\rvert_{\tau=0},0,0\right)
.

iii.

If
(v,x,t)\in U
,
w\in V
, then
e^{\tau A_{w}}\cdot(v,x,t)=(v_{\tau},x,t)
and
e^{\alpha_{t}(v_{\tau})}\cdot x=e^{\tau\alpha_{t}(w)}e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x
whenever the flow of
A_{w}
is well-defined at
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.

iv.

Whenever the flow is well-defined,
e^{\tau A_{w}}\cdot(0,x,t)=(\tau w,x,t)
.

Proof.

Let

\tilde{\psi}(w,v,x,t)=e^{A_{w}}\cdot(v,x,t)
whose domain is the set of points at which the flow exists.
The map
\tilde{\psi}
is of the form
\tilde{\psi}(w,v,x,t)=(\hat{\psi}(v,w,x,t),x,t)
for some map
\hat{\psi}
which satisfies (
3.18
).
We define
\psi
by (
3.17
).
By (
3.18
), the map
\tilde{\psi}
is a submersion at any point in
\{0\}\times\{0\}\times M\times\{0\}
.
Since
\tilde{\psi}
is equivariant, the set of points at which
\tilde{\psi}
is a submersion is an
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant open neighborhood of
V\times V\times M\times\{0\}
.
By reducing
\dom(\tilde{\psi})
to the set of the points at which
\tilde{\psi}
is a submersion,
we can suppose that
\tilde{\psi}

is a submersion.
By replacing
\dom(\tilde{\psi})
with
\dom(\tilde{\psi})\cap\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})\cap\tilde{\psi}^{-1}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}))
,
we can further suppose that
\dom(\tilde{\psi})\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})
and
\im(\tilde{\psi})\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
.
We now take the domain of
\psi
to be
\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\dom(\tilde{\psi}))
.
Diagram (
3.16
) is easily seen to be a pullback diagram.
So, by
1.1.15
,
\psi
is a submersion.
Since
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2})=\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}))
and
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})=\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}^{-1}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}))
, (
3.14
) follows. Commutativity of (
3.15
) follows from (
3.18
) and
3.1.2
.
Finally,
Section

3.2.3
.
ii
follows from Condition
iv
and that
A_{0}=0
.
∎

Let
T:V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V
be a bilinear map as in the proof of
2.2.10
which satisfies (
3.5
).
One can check that by the definition of
T
in (
3.6
) that
T
is
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant.
By replacing
T
, by
(w,v,x,t)\mapsto\frac{T(w,v,x,t)-T(v,w,x,t)}{2}
, we can further suppose that

\displaystyle T(w,v,x,t)=-T(v,w,x,t),\quad\forall(v,w,x,t)\in V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}.

(3.19)

Let
f_{\tau},h_{\tau}:V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to\mathcal{L}(V)
for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
be locally-defined smooth solutions of the first order differential equation (matrix differential equation)

\displaystyle f_{0}(v,x,t)w=w,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w=f_{\tau}(v,x,t)T(w,v,e^{-\tau\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t),\quad h_{\tau}=\int_{0}^{\tau}f_{s}ds.

(3.20)

Because the differential equation defining
f
is a matrix differential equation, the maps
f_{\tau}(v,x,t)
and
h_{\tau}(v,x,t)
are defined whenever
e^{-\tau\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x
is well-defined.
Let
h_{1}^{-1}(v,x,t)
be the linear inverse of
h_{1}(v,x,t)
if it exists.
By (
3.5
),
f_{\tau}(v,x,0)=e^{\tau\mathrm{ad}_{v}}
, where
\mathrm{ad}_{v}:V\to V
is the linear map
\mathrm{ad}_{v}(w)=[w,v]
.
Hence,
h_{1}(v,x,0)=\frac{e^{\mathrm{ad}_{v}}-1}{\mathrm{ad}_{v}}
.
Since
\mathrm{ad}_{v}
is a nilpotent linear map,
h_{1}(v,x,0)
is invertible.
The formula for the derivative of the exponential of matrices
[
45
, Theorem 5 Section 1.2]
implies that

\displaystyle h_{1}^{-1}(v,x,0)w=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\BCH(\tau w,v)\right\rvert_{\tau=0}.

(3.21)

Let

\displaystyle U=\left\{(v,x,t)\in V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x\text{ and }\ h_{1}^{-1}\left(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t\right)\text{ exist}\right\}

(3.22)

\displaystyle A_{w}(v,x,t)=(h_{1}^{-1}(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)w,0,0)\in\mathcal{X}(U).

The set
U
is an open neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
.
Let us check that
A_{w}
satisfies the required conditions.
Equivariance of
T
implies that

\displaystyle f_{\tau}(\alpha_{\lambda}(v,x,t))\alpha_{\lambda}(w)=\alpha_{\lambda}(f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w),\quad h_{\tau}(\alpha_{\lambda}(v,x,t))\alpha_{\lambda}(w)=\alpha_{\lambda}(h_{\tau}(v,x,t)w)

which implies that
U
is
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant and
\alpha_{\lambda*}(A_{w})=A_{\alpha_{\lambda}(w)}
. By (
3.21
), we see that

A_{w}(v,x,0)=\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\BCH(\tau w,v)\right\rvert_{\tau=0},0,0\right)
.
By (
3.19
),
f_{\tau}(sv,x,t)v=v
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
whenever
f_{\tau}
is well-defined.
It follows that
A_{w}(sw,x,t)=(w,0,0)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
from which
Section

3.2.5
.
iv
follows.

It remains to check
Section

3.2.5
.
iii
.
On the manifold
V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
, we define the vector fields:

\displaystyle Y_{w}(v,x,t)=(w,0,0),\quad X_{w}(v,x,t)=(0,\natural(\alpha_{t}(w))(x),0),\quad Z(v,x,t)=(0,\natural(\alpha_{t}(v))(x),0),

where
w\in V
is a parameter.
We have

\displaystyle[Y_{w},Z]=X_{w},\quad e^{\tau X_{w}}\cdot(v,x,t)=(v,e^{\tau\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,t),\quad e^{\tau Z}\cdot(v,x,t)=(v,e^{\tau\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)

(3.23)

By (
3.5
) and (
3.23
), we have

\displaystyle(v,x,t)=X_{T(w,v,x,t)}(v,x,t).

(3.24)

The flow of
A_{w}
is given by
e^{\tau A_{w}}\cdot(v,x,t)=(v_{\tau},x,t)
for some
v_{\tau}\in V
because
A_{w}
is tangent to
V
.
By the definition of pushforward of vector fields,

\displaystyle(v_{\tau},e^{\alpha_{t}(v_{\tau})}\cdot x,t)=e^{Z}\cdot(v_{\tau},x,t)=e^{Z}e^{\tau A_{w}}\cdot(v,x,t)

\displaystyle=e^{Z}e^{\tau A_{w}}e^{-Z}e^{Z}\cdot(v,x,t)

(3.25)

\displaystyle=e^{\tau e_{*}^{Z}(A_{w})}\cdot(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t).

We now compute the vector field
e_{*}^{Z}(A_{w})
.
We claim that

\displaystyle e^{\tau Z}_{*}(X_{w})(v,x,t)=X_{f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t),\ e^{\tau Z}_{*}(Y_{w})(v,x,t)=Y_{w}(v,x,t)+X_{h_{\tau}(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t).

(3.26)

To see this, let
K_{w,\tau}(v,x,t)=X_{f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t)
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}e^{-\tau Z}_{*}(K_{w,\tau})=e^{-\tau Z}_{*}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}K_{w,\tau}-[K_{w,\tau},Z]\right).

By (
3.24
), we have

\displaystyle(v,x,t)=X_{T(f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w,v,x,t)}(v,x,t)-X_{Z(f_{\tau})(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t).

To compute
Z(f_{\tau})
, notice that

\displaystyle f_{\tau^{\prime}+\tau}(v,e^{\tau^{\prime}\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)=f_{\tau^{\prime}}(v,e^{\tau^{\prime}\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)f_{\tau}(v,x,t).

By taking derivative at
\tau^{\prime}=0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle Z(f_{\tau})(v,x,t)w+\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w=T(f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w,v,x,t).

Hence,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}e^{-\tau Z}_{*}(K_{w,\tau})=0
. So,
e^{-\tau Z}_{*}(K_{w,\tau})=K_{w,0}=X_{w}
.
This proves the first identity of (
3.26
).
The second identity of (
3.26
) follows from the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}X_{h_{\tau}(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t)=X_{f_{\tau}(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t)=e^{\tau Z}_{*}\left(X_{w}\right)=e^{\tau Z}_{*}\left([Y_{w},Z]\right)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}e^{\tau Z}_{*}(Y_{w}).

Since
A_{w}(v,x,t)=Y_{h_{1}^{-1}(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)w}(v,x,t)
, the second identity of (
3.26
) implies that

\displaystyle e_{*}^{Z}(A_{w})(v,x,t)=Y_{h_{1}^{-1}(v,x,t)w}(v,x,t)+X_{w}(v,x,t)=(h_{1}^{-1}(v,x,t)w,\natural(\alpha_{t}(w))(x),0).

So,
e^{\tau e_{*}^{Z}(A_{w})}\cdot(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)=(\xi_{\tau},e^{\tau\alpha_{t}(w)}e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)
for some
\xi_{\tau}\in V
.
Hence, by (
3.25
),

\displaystyle(v_{\tau},e^{\alpha_{t}(v_{\tau})}\cdot x,t)=(\xi_{\tau},e^{\tau\alpha_{t}(w)}e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t).

In particular,
e^{\alpha_{t}(v_{\tau})}\cdot x=e^{\tau\alpha_{t}(w)}e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x
.

∎

Proof of
3.2.2
.

By replacing
(V,\natural)
with a bigger
\nu
-graded basis using
3.1.1
, we can suppose that
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
Let
\tilde{\psi}
be as in
3.2.3
, and
\hat{\psi}
as in (
3.17
),
T:V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V^{\prime}
as in the proof of
2.2.5
which satisfies (
3.2
).
By looking at the way
T
is constructed, we see that we can suppose that
T(v,x,0)=v
for all
v\in V^{\prime}
.
Note that the map
T
defined using (
3.3
) is
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

To prove
3.2.2
, it is enough to find an equivariant smooth map
\hat{\phi}:\dom(\hat{\phi})\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V^{\prime}
defined on an equivariant open neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}

such that:

i.

If
(v,x,t)\in\dom(\hat{\phi})
, then
(-v,\hat{\phi}(v,x,t),x,t)\in\dom(\tilde{\psi})
and
T(\tilde{\psi}(-v,\hat{\phi}(v,x,t),x,t))=0
.

ii.

If
v\in V^{\prime}
and
x\in M
, then
\hat{\phi}(v,x,0)=v
.

Proof.

If
(v,x,t)\in\dom(\hat{\phi})
, then by (
3.2
),
\hat{\psi}(-v,\hat{\phi}(v,x,t),x,t)\in\ker(\natural_{x,t})
.
By
3.1.2
and (
3.18
), we deduce

\displaystyle e^{\alpha_{t}(\hat{\phi}(v,x,t))}\cdot x=e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,\text{ and }\natural_{x,0}(\hat{\phi}(v,x,0))=\natural_{x,0}(v).

(3.27)

We now define
\tilde{\phi}(v,x,t)=(\hat{\phi}(v,x,t),x,t)
and
\phi(v,\gamma)=(\hat{\phi}(v,\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}(\gamma)),\gamma)
.
We proceed as in the proof of
3.2.5
to reduce the domain of
\tilde{\phi}
so that
\tilde{\phi}
and
\phi
are submersions,
and (
3.9
) holds, and Diagram (
3.11
) is a pullback diagram.
Finally, Diagram (
3.10
) commutes by (
3.27
).
∎

We will define
\hat{\phi}(v,x,t)
using the implicit function theorem to be the unique
v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
which solves the equation

T(\tilde{\psi}(-v,v^{\prime},x,t))=0
.
More precisely, consider the map

\displaystyle\Psi:\dom(\tilde{\psi})\cap(V\times V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})\to V\times V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\ \Psi(v,v^{\prime},x,t)=(v,T(\tilde{\psi}(v,v^{\prime},x,t)),x,t)

Since
\tilde{\psi}
and
T
are equivariant,
\Psi
is equivariant.
Furthermore, for any
x\in M
,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Psi_{(0,0,x,0)}
is bijective and
\Psi(0,0,x,0)=(0,0,x,0)
.

Theorem 3.2.7

(Global inverse mapping theorem)
.

Let
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
be a smooth map between smooth manifolds,
A\subseteq M_{1}
a closed subset.
If for every
x\in A
,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f_{x}
is bijective, and
f_{|A}:A\to M_{2}
is injective and proper, then
there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
A
such that
f_{|U}
is an open smooth embedding.

Proof.

By the inverse mapping theorem, and by replacing
M_{1}
with an open neighborhood of
A
, we can assume that
f
is a local diffeomorphism.
We only need to find an open neighborhood
U
of
A
such that
f_{|U}
is injective.
Let
K\subseteq A
be compact.
We can find
U_{n}\subseteq M_{1}
a sequence of relatively compact open neighborhoods of
K
such that
K=\bigcap_{n}U_{n}
and
\overline{U_{n+1}}\subseteq U_{n}
.
Since
\overline{U_{n}}
is compact,
f_{|\overline{U_{n}}\cup A}
is proper.
We claim that there exists
n
such that
f_{|\overline{U_{n}}\cup A}
is injective.
If not, then there exists sequences
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
x_{n}\neq y_{n}
,
x_{n}\in\overline{U_{n}}
,
y_{n}\in\overline{U_{n}}\cup A
,
f(x_{n})=f(y_{n})
.
Since
U_{n}
are decreasing and relatively compact, by passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
x_{n}\to x\in K
.
Since
f_{|\overline{U_{n}}\cup A}
is proper and
f(y_{n})
converges to
f(x)
, we can find a subsequence of
y_{n}
which converges.
So without loss of generality, we can suppose that
y_{n}\to y\in A
.
So,
f(x)=f(y)
. By injectivity of
f_{|A}
,
x=y
.
Since
f
is a local diffeomorphism at
x
,
x_{n}=y_{n}
for
n
big enough which is a contraction.

Let
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence of compact subsets of
M_{2}
such that
M_{2}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}
.
We will define by recurrence an increasing sequence
(V_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of relatively compact open subsets of
M_{1}
such that
f_{|\overline{V_{n}}\cup A}
is injective and proper, and
f^{-1}(K_{n})\cap A\subseteq V_{n}
.
The initial step follows from what we just proved applied to
f^{-1}(K_{1})\cap A
which is compact by properness of
f_{|A}
.
The induction step also follows from what we just proved by replacing
A
with
\overline{V_{n}}\cup A
and using the compact set
\overline{V_{n}}\cup(f^{-1}(K_{n+1})\cap A)
.
The set
\bigcup_{n}V_{n}
is an open neighborhood of
A
on which
f
is injective.
∎

By
Theorem

3.2.7
,
\Psi
is an open embedding on an open neighborhood
W\subseteq V\times V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
of
\{(0,0,x,0):x\in M\}
.
Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be a norm on
V
.
For any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
, let
\left\lVert t\right\rVert_{\infty}:=\max\{t_{i}:i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]\}
.
There exists
g:M\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
a continuous function such that

\displaystyle\left\{(v,v^{\prime},x,t)\in V\times V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:\max(\left\lVert v\right\rVert,\left\lVert v^{\prime}\right\rVert,\left\lVert t\right\rVert_{\infty})<2g(x)\right\}\subseteq W

Let

\displaystyle U=\{(v,v^{\prime},x,t)\in V\times V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:\max\left(\left\lVert\alpha_{t/g(x)}(v)\right\rVert,\left\lVert\alpha_{t/g(x)}(v^{\prime})\right\rVert\right)<2g(x)\}

The set
U
is
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant open neighborhood of
V\times V^{\prime}\times M\times\{0\}
.
We claim that
\Psi
is an open smooth embedding on
U
.
We will show that if
(v,v^{\prime},x,t),(w,w^{\prime},x,t)\in U
, then there exists
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
such that
\alpha_{\lambda}(v,v^{\prime},x,t)
,
\alpha_{\lambda}(w,w^{\prime},x,t)\in W
.
Equivariance of
\Psi
would imply that
\Psi
is injective on
U
and by taking
(v,v^{\prime},x,t)=(w,w^{\prime},x,t)
, we deduce that
U\subseteq\bigcup_{\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(W)

which by equivariance of
\Psi
implies that
\Psi
is an open embedding on
U
.

We now prove our claim. Let
(v,v^{\prime},x,t),(w,w^{\prime},x,t)\in U
. Let
\epsilon>0
to be chosen later.
We take
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
to be
\lambda_{i}=t_{i}/g(x)
if
t_{i}>0
and
\epsilon
if
t_{i}=0
.
We have

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(v,v^{\prime},x,t)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),\alpha_{\lambda}(v^{\prime}),x,s),\quad\alpha_{\lambda}(w,w^{\prime},x,t)=(\alpha_{\lambda}(w),\alpha_{\lambda}(w^{\prime}),x,s),

where
s_{i}=g(x)
if
t_{i}>0
and
s_{i}=0
if
t_{i}=0
.
Hence,
\left\lVert s\right\rVert_{\infty}<2g(x)
.
As
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
,
\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(v)\right\rVert\to\left\lVert\alpha_{t/g(x)}(v)\right\rVert
. Same with
v^{\prime}
,
w
,
w^{\prime}
.
So, for
\epsilon
small enough,
\alpha_{\lambda}(v,v^{\prime},x,t),\alpha_{\lambda}(w,w^{\prime},x,t)\in W
.

We can now define
\hat{\phi}
by the formula

\Psi(-v,\hat{\phi}(v,x,t),x,t)=(-v,0,x,t)

whose domain is

\displaystyle\dom(\hat{\phi}):=\{(v,x,t)\in V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:(-v,0,x,t)\in\Psi(U)\}.

By equivariance of
U,\Psi,T
, we deduce that
\hat{\phi}
is equivariant.
For any
x\in M
, since
(0,0,x,0)\in U
, we deduce that
(0,x,0)\in\dom(\hat{\phi})
which
by equivariance of
\dom(\hat{\phi})
implies that
V\times M\times\{0\}\subseteq\dom(\hat{\phi})
.
By construction,
\hat{\phi}
satisfies
Section

3.2.6
.
i
.
By (
3.18
), if
v\in V^{\prime}
, then
\Psi(-v,v,x,0)=(-v,0,x,0)
.
Section

3.2.6
.
ii
follows.

∎

3.3
Proofs of the results in Section
2.3

Proof of
2.3.2
.

Let
(V,\natural)
and
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})
be
\nu
-graded basis,
X
a topological space,
f:\mathbb{G}\to X
a function such that
f_{|M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}}
is continuous.
We will show that
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is continuous if and only if
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
is continuous.
By passing through the intermediary
\nu
-graded basis
(V\oplus V^{\prime},\natural\oplus\natural^{\prime})
, we can suppose without loss of generality that
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})\subseteq(V,\natural)
.

•

Suppose
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is continuous. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
restricted to
V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
coincides with
\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
on their common domain,
it follows that
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
is continuous on a neighborhood of
V^{\prime}\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
.
The map
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
is continuous on
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}})\cap V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
because
f_{|M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}}
is continuous.
So,
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
is continuous on
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}})
.

•

Suppose
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
is continuous.
Let
\phi
be as in
3.2.2
.
By (
3.10
),
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is continuous on
\dom(\phi)
.
The map
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is continuous on
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\cap V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
because
f_{|M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}}
is continuous.
Since
V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}\subseteq\dom(\phi)
, we deduce that
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is continuous on
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
.

By an identical argument, one shows that if
f:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{C}
is a map such that
f_{|M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}}
is smooth, then

f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is smooth if and only if
f\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}}
is smooth.
This finishes the proof of
Section

2.3.2
.
i
.

By the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem,
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{G}
.
Since
V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
is an open dense subset of
V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
,
one deduces that
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{G}
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

2.3.2
.
iv
.
Fix
(V,\natural)
any
\nu
-graded
Lie
basis.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V}\sqcup\mathrm{Id}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\sqcup(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)\to\mathbb{G}

be the obvious surjective map.
By definition,
\mathbb{G}
is equipped with the quotient topology and smooth structure.
Therefore, to prove
Section

2.3.2
.
ii
and
Section

2.3.2
.
iii
, it is enough to show that the equivalence relation on
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\sqcup(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
of having the same image under
\mathcal{Q}_{V}\sqcup\mathrm{Id}
satisfies the hypothesis of
Theorem

1.1.12
.
This would also imply
Section

2.3.2
.
v
in the case where the
\nu
-graded basis is Lie. The general case follows from the case of
\nu
-graded Lie basis by
3.1.1
together with
(
3.10
) and
1.1.11
.

Let

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\{(v,\gamma,w,\eta)\in\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\times\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}):\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,\gamma)=\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\eta)\}

\displaystyle=\{(v,x,t,w,x,t):e^{\alpha_{t}(-w)}e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x=x\}\cup\{(v,L,x,0,w,L,x,0):w^{-1}\cdot v\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)\}.

Since the map
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is a submersion at any point in
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\cap(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
, see (
2.15
), it suffices to prove:

i.

The set
R
is closed in
\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\times\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
.

ii.

The projection
(v,\gamma,w,\eta)\in R\mapsto(v,\gamma)\in V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is a submersion at every point in the set
R\cap(V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}\times V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\})
.

Lemma 3.3.1

(Period bounding lemma
[
40
]
)
.

If
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
,
K\subseteq M
a compact subset, then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for any
x\in K
,
\tau\in]0,\epsilon[
, if
e^{\tau X}\cdot x=x
, then
X(x)=0
.

Proof.

If
\epsilon
doesn’t exist, then there exists
(x_{n},\epsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq K\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
such that
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
,
X(x_{n})\neq 0
and
e^{\epsilon_{n}X}\cdot x_{n}=x_{n}
.
By passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
x_{n}\to x\in K
.
We fix some chart near
x
.
Since the map
\chi:\mathbb{R}\times M\to M
which maps
(\tau,x)
to
e^{\tau X}\cdot x
is a smooth map on its domain of definition, we can suppose that the orbit
e^{\tau X}\cdot x_{n}
for
0\leq\tau\leq\epsilon_{n}
lies in the fixed chart.
The function

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\tau\mapsto\langle e^{\tau X}\cdot x_{n},X(x_{n})\rangle

is a smooth function whose values at
0
and at
\epsilon_{n}
agree.
Here,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the inner product inside the coordinate chart we fixed.
By Rolle’s theorem, there exists
0<\tau_{n}<\epsilon_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\langle X(e^{\tau_{n}X}\cdot x_{n}),X(x_{n})\rangle=0.

Since
X(x_{n})\neq 0
, we can by taking a subsequence suppose that

\frac{X(x_{n})}{\left\lVert X(x_{n})\right\rVert}\to v\neq 0
.
Since

X(e^{\tau_{n}X}\cdot x_{n})=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\chi_{(\tau_{n},x_{n})}(0,X(x_{n}))

and since
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\chi_{(\tau_{n},x_{n})}
converges to the identity map as
n\to+\infty
, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{X(e^{\tau_{n}X}\cdot x_{n})}{\left\lVert X(x_{n})\right\rVert}\to v.

Hence,
\langle v,v\rangle=0
which is a contraction. ∎

Lemma 3.3.2

(
[
20
]
)
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
a Euclidean norm on
V
,
K\subseteq M
compact.
There exists
\epsilon>0
such that for any
x\in K
,
v\in V
, if

e^{v}\cdot x=x
, then either
\natural(v)(x)=0
or
\left\lVert v\right\rVert\geq\epsilon
.

Proof.

On
V\times M
, there is a natural vector field
X(v,x)=(0,\natural(v)(x))

whose flow is
e^{\tau X}\cdot(v,x)=(v,e^{\tau v}\cdot x)
.
The result follows from
3.3.1
applied to
X
and
\{v\in V:\left\lVert v\right\rVert\leq 1\}\times K
.
∎

We now prove that
R
is closed. It is clear that
R
is closed away from
t=0
.
By
Section

2.2.6
.
ii
,
\{(v,L,x,0,w,L,x,0):w^{-1}\cdot v\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)\}
is closed.
So, we need to show that if
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq V
,
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M
,
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
are sequences such that

\displaystyle v_{n}\to v,\ w_{n}\to w,\ x_{n}\to x,\ t_{n}\to 0,\ \ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L),\ e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(-w_{n})}e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=x_{n},

then
w^{-1}\cdot v\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.
Let
\hat{\psi}
as in (
3.17
).
Since
(-w_{n},v_{n},x_{n},t_{n})\to(-w,v,x,0)\in\dom(\hat{\psi})
, we deduce that for
n
big enough,
(-w_{n},v_{n},x_{n},t_{n})\in\dom(\hat{\psi})
.
By (
3.18
),
e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(\hat{\psi}(-w_{n},v_{n},x_{n},t_{n}))}\cdot x_{n}=x_{n}
.
Since
\hat{\psi}(-w_{n},v_{n},x_{n},t_{n})\to\hat{\psi}(-w,v,x,0)=w^{-1}\cdot v
, we deduce from
3.3.2
that for
n
big enough,
\hat{\psi}(-w_{n},v_{n},x_{n},t_{n})\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})
.
By
3.0.1
,
w^{-1}\cdot v\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
. This finishes the proof that
R
is closed.

Let
v_{0},w_{0}\in V
,
(L_{0},x_{0})\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
such that
w_{0}^{-1}\cdot v_{0}\in\natural_{x_{0},0}^{-1}(L_{0})
.
Consider the smooth manifold

\displaystyle A=\{(v,w,L,x,t)\in V\times V\times\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:(-w,v,x,t)\in\dom(\hat{\psi}),\hat{\psi}(-w,v,x,t)\in L\}.

Take any smooth map
g:V\to\mathbb{R}^{\dim(V)-\dim(M)}
which has the property that its restriction to
v_{0}\cdot\natural_{x_{0},0}^{-1}(L_{0})
is a local diffeomorphism at
w_{0}
.
The differential of the map

\displaystyle A\to V\times\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\dim(V)-\dim(M)},\quad(v,w,L,x,t)\mapsto(v,L,x,t,g(w))

at
(v_{0},w_{0},\natural_{x_{0},0}^{-1}(L_{0}),x_{0},0)
is a bijection. It follows that it is a local diffeomorphism at the point
(v_{0},w_{0},\natural_{x_{0},0}^{-1}(L_{0}),x_{0},0)
.
Therefore, by restricting to
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
using the embedding
\mathrm{Grass}(\natural)
from (
2.11
), the map
(v,\gamma,w,\eta)\in R\mapsto(v,\gamma,g(w))\in V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\times\mathbb{R}^{\dim(V)-\dim(M)}
is a local diffeomorphism at
(v_{0},L_{0},x_{0},0,w_{0},L_{0},x_{0},0)
.
This finishes the proof of
2.3.2
.

∎

Proof of
2.3.3
.

Both parts are proved similarly.
One direction follows from continuity of
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
for any
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
which follows from
Section

2.3.2
.
i
.
The other follows from the fact that
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is open which follows from
Section

2.3.2
.
v
.

∎

Proof of
2.3.4
.

By
2.3.3
, it suffices to prove that if
(y_{n},x_{n},t_{n})\to(\natural_{x,0}(v)L,x,0)
and
(x_{n},t_{n})\to(L,x,0)
, then the equation
e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=y_{n}
admits a solution in
S
for
n
big enough that satisfies
v_{n}\to v
.
By
3.1.1
, we can suppose that
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
Notice that by our hypothesis on
S
,
\dim(S)=\dim(M)
.
The map

\displaystyle S\times\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)\to V,\quad(s,w)\mapsto sw

is a diffeomorphism because of
3.1.2
, and that
\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
is a subgroup of
V
which contains the subgroup
\ker(\natural_{x,0})
.
Let
E\to\mathrm{Grass}(V)
be the tautological real vector bundle whose fiber over
L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Grass}(V)
is
L^{\prime}
, and
\hat{\psi}
as in (
3.17
).
By our hypothesis on
S
and (
3.18
), the map

\displaystyle S\times E\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to V\times\mathrm{Grass}(V)\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad(s,l,L^{\prime},y,t)\mapsto(\hat{\psi}(s,l,y,t),L^{\prime},y,t)

(3.28)

is a local diffeomorphism at
(v,0,\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L),x,0)
.
Now, let
v_{n}\in V
be a sequence such that
y_{n}=e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}
and
v_{n}\to v
which exists by
2.3.3
.
So, for
n
big enough,
(v_{n},\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}),x_{n},t_{n})
is in the image of (
3.28
).
Hence, there exists a unique
s_{n}\in S
and
l_{n}\in\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}^{-1}(\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}))
such that
\hat{\psi}(s_{n},l_{n},x_{n},t_{n})=v_{n}
.
Furthermore,
(s_{n},l_{n},\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}),x_{n},t_{n})\to(v,0,\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L),x,0)
.
So,
s_{n}\to v
.
By (
3.18
),
y_{n}=e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(s_{n})}e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(l_{n})}\cdot x_{n}=e^{\alpha_{t_{n}}(s_{n})}\cdot x_{n}
.

∎

Proof of
2.3.5
.

The map
s
is smooth because
s\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\subseteq V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is the projection onto the second coordinate. It is a submersion by
1.1.11
and
Section

2.3.2
.
v
.
The identity map
u:\mathbb{G}^{(0)}\to\mathbb{G}
is smooth because
u(\gamma)=\mathcal{Q}_{V}(0,\gamma)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is smooth.
Let us check that the inverse map
\iota:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}
is smooth.
It suffices to prove that
\iota\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is smooth.
By
3.1.3
, the following map is smooth

\displaystyle\hat{\iota}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\to V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)},\quad\hat{\iota}(v,\gamma)=(-v,r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,\gamma)).

(3.29)

(3.30)

commutes. It follows that
\iota
is smooth. Since
r=s\circ\iota
, it follows that
r
is smooth.

Let us show that the multiplication map
m:\mathbb{G}^{(2)}\to\mathbb{G}
is smooth.
The restriction of
m
to the
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
part of
\mathbb{G}
is smooth.
So, we need to show that the map

\displaystyle\{(v,\gamma,w,\eta)\in\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\times\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}):(\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,\gamma),\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\eta))\in\mathbb{G}^{(2)}\}\to\mathbb{G}

\displaystyle(v,\gamma,w,\eta)\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,\gamma)\mathcal{Q}_{V}(w,\eta)

is smooth. This map is precisely the map
\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}
from (
3.13
).
Near
t=0
, it is smooth by (
3.15
).
Away from
t=0
, it is smooth by Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem, see (
3.12
).
Hence,
m
is smooth.

It remains to show that
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)
is the same as
A(\mathbb{G})
constructed in
Section

2.2
.
They clearly are equal as sets.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis. The map
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V}:\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(s\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}))\to\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s))
is a smooth submersion because
s
and
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
are submersions.
We now restrict it to
\{0\}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, and we get the map
A(\natural)
from (
2.13
). The result follows.

∎

Proof of
2.3.6
.

By applying
1.1.14
to the submersions
\mathcal{Q}_{V}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\to\mathbb{G}
and
s:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
,
we deduce that for any
k\in\mathbb{C}
, we have a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\Omega^{k}(V)\simeq\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V}))\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\Omega^{k,0}(\mathbb{G})).

(3.31)

Let
\hat{\iota}
as in (
3.29
).
By (
3.30
), the following diagram commutes

(3.32)

Since
\hat{\iota}
is an open embedding,
\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(r\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}))\simeq\hat{\iota}^{*}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(s\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V})))\simeq\hat{\iota}^{*}(V)\simeq V
.
By
1.1.14
applied to
r
and
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Omega^{k}(V)\simeq\Omega^{k}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V}))\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\Omega^{0,k}(\mathbb{G})).

(3.33)

Taking the tensor product of (
3.31
) and (
3.33
), the result follows.

∎

3.4
Proofs of the results in Section
2.5

Proof of
2.5.1
.

The map
\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}
is well-defined because of
3.2.1
. It is easily seen to be a groupoid automorphism.
So, we only need to show that it is smooth. The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\lambda}}

(3.34)

Hence,
\alpha_{\lambda}
is smooth.

∎

Proof of
2.5.2
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis.
We need to show that
\omega\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
is a smooth section of
\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{V}(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G}))
.
By
2.3.6
, we have a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{V}(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G}))
.
We define a smooth function
f:\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\to\mathbb{C}
as follows:
If
(v,x,t)\in\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
, then
y\mapsto e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot y
is a local diffeomorphism at
x
which maps
x
to
e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x
.
So, its differential at
x
is an isomorphism
L:T_{x}M\to T_{e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}}M
.
Let

\displaystyle f(v,x,t)=L^{*}\left(\mathrm{vol}_{e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{x}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

The function
f
is clearly smooth.
One can check that
f\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}:\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\to\mathbb{C}
corresponds to
w\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}
using the isomorphism from
2.3.6
.
This shows that
\omega
is smooth.
It is obvious that
\omega
satisfies (
1.27
) on
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
.
By density, it follows that
\omega
satisfies (
1.27
).

∎

3.5
Proofs of the results in Section
2.6

Proof of
2.6.2
.

For any
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{V}:=C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))\times C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

equipped with the product topology.
Given two
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
and
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})
, we will construct a continuous linear map

T_{V^{\prime},V}:\mathcal{A}_{V}\to\mathcal{A}_{V^{\prime}}

such that
q_{V^{\prime}}\circ T_{V^{\prime},V}=q_{V}
.
Constructing the map
T_{V^{\prime},V}
finishes the proof of
Section

2.6.2
.
i
.
Using the
\nu
-graded basis
(V\oplus V^{\prime},\natural\oplus\natural^{\prime})
, we only need to define
T_{V\oplus V^{\prime},V}
and
T_{V^{\prime},V\oplus V^{\prime}}
because then we can take
T_{V^{\prime},V}:=T_{V^{\prime},V\oplus V^{\prime}}\circ T_{V\oplus V^{\prime},V}
.
So, without loss of generality we can suppose that
(V^{\prime},\natural^{\prime})\subseteq(V,\natural)
.
We will define
T_{V,V^{\prime}}
and
T_{V^{\prime},V}
.
Let
\tilde{\phi}
and
\phi
be as in
3.2.2
.
The following diagram commutes

where
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
are the obvious projections.
By
1.1.14
,

\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\phi}))\simeq\Omega^{1}(V/V^{\prime})
.
In particular, we have an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\phi),\Omega^{1}(V))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\Omega^{1}(V^{\prime}))

Since
\phi
is the pullback of
\tilde{\phi}
as in
1.1.15
, it follows that we have an integration map

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\phi),\Omega^{1}(V))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)},\Omega^{1}(V^{\prime}))

which satisfies

\displaystyle\phi_{*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})=\tilde{\phi}_{*}(g)\circ\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}},\quad\forall g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\tilde{\phi}),\Omega^{1}(V)).

(3.35)

Furthermore, by (
3.10
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}\circ\phi_{*}=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}.

(3.36)

We can now define
T_{V,V^{\prime}}
and
T_{V^{\prime},V}
.

•

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})
equal to
1
in a neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
and
\supp(\chi)\subseteq\dom(\tilde{\phi})
.
By (
3.35
) and (
3.36
),
we can take

\displaystyle T_{V^{\prime},V}(g,h)=\left(\tilde{\phi}_{*}(\chi g),h+\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(((1-\chi)g)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\right).

•

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(V^{\prime}\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})
equal to
1
in a neighborhood of
V^{\prime}\times M\times\{0\}
and
\supp(\chi)\subseteq\im(\tilde{\phi})
.
Let
k\in C^{\infty}(\dom(\tilde{\phi}),\Omega^{1}(V/V^{\prime}))
such that
\tilde{\phi}_{|\supp(k)}:\supp(k)\to\im(\tilde{\phi})
is proper and

\tilde{\phi}_{*}(k)\in C^{\infty}(\im(\tilde{\phi}))
is equal to
1
.
The function
k
exists because
\tilde{\phi}
is a submersion.
We define

\displaystyle T_{V,V^{\prime}}(g,h)=\left(k((\chi g)\circ\tilde{\phi}),h+\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}(((1-\chi)g)\circ\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\right),

where
k((\chi g)\circ\tilde{\phi})
is the pointwise multiplication of
k
and
(\chi g)\circ\tilde{\phi}\in C^{\infty}(\dom(\tilde{\phi}),\Omega^{1}(V^{\prime}))
.
To see that
q_{V}\circ T_{V^{\prime},V}=q_{V^{\prime}}
, let
l=k((\chi g)\circ\tilde{\phi})
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(l\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}\left(\phi_{*}\left(l\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{eqn:qksjdkofjqsodjfkqsjdjfkqs}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}\Big(\phi_{*}\Big((k\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})((\chi g)\circ\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\circ\phi)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{eqn:ksqldjflqdsjfmqsdjf_2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}\left(\phi_{*}\left(k\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)(\chi g)\circ\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}\left((\tilde{\phi}_{*}(k)\chi g)\circ\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{eqn:phi_hat_phi_tilde_pushforward}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V^{\prime}*}\left((\chi g)\circ\pi_{V^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right).

We have thus proved
Section

2.6.2
.
i
.
Continuity of the inclusion
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is obvious.
Since
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a quotient of an LF space, we only need to check that
\ker(q_{V})
is closed which is straightforward to see.
This finishes the proof of
Section

2.6.2
.
ii
.
By equivariance of
\mathcal{Q}_{V}
, we have

\alpha_{\lambda}(q_{V}(g,h))=q_{V}(\alpha_{\lambda}(g),\alpha_{\lambda}(h))
.
This proves
Section

2.6.2
.
iv
.

Lemma 3.5.1
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis,
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
If
U\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
is an open neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
, then there exists
(\tilde{f},\accentset{\approx}{f})\in q_{V}^{-1}(f)
such that

\supp(\tilde{f})\subseteq U
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})
which is equal to
1
in a neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
and
\supp(\chi)\subseteq U
.
If
(\tilde{f},\accentset{\approx}{f})\in q_{V}^{-1}(f)

then,
\mathcal{Q}_{V*}((\tilde{f}(1-\chi))\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So,
(\tilde{f}\chi,\accentset{\approx}{f}+\mathcal{Q}_{V*}((\tilde{f}(1-\chi))\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))\in q_{V}^{-1}(f)
.
∎

We fix a
\nu
-graded Lie basis
(V,\natural)
.
The space
C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is two-sided ideal of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So, to prove
Section

2.6.2
.
iii
, we only need to consider elements of the form
\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
.
By
3.5.1
, we can suppose that
\supp(g)\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
.
Let
\hat{\iota}
as in (
3.29
).
We also define

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}:\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})\to V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\quad\tilde{\iota}(v,x,t)=(-v,e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t).

Both
\tilde{\iota}
and
\hat{\iota}
are open embeddings, and
\hat{\iota}
is the pullback of
\tilde{\iota}
as in
1.1.15
.
So, by (
1.13
) and (
3.30
),

\displaystyle\iota_{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\hat{\iota}_{*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{\iota}_{*}(g)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}).

Hence,
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is closed under taking adjoints.

Let us prove that
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is closed under taking convolution.
Let
\tilde{\psi},\psi
as in
3.2.3
.
By (
3.17
), the following diagram commutes:

where

are the obvious projections. By

, we deduce that

.
Hence, we have an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\tilde{\psi}),\Omega^{2}(V))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\Omega^{1}(V)).

Since
\psi
is the pullback of
\tilde{\psi}
, by
1.1.15
, we have an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle\psi_{*}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\psi),\Omega^{2}(V))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)},\Omega^{1}(V))

which satisfies

\displaystyle\psi_{*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})=\tilde{\psi}_{*}(g)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}},\quad\forall g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\tilde{\psi}),\Omega^{2}(V)).

(3.37)

By (
3.15
) and
1.1.14
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}}))\simeq\psi^{*}(\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V})))\otimes\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\psi))\simeq\psi^{*}(\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V})))\otimes\Omega^{1}(V).

We deduce from pulling back (
2.17
) with
k=l=\frac{1}{2}
using
\psi
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2*}(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G}))\otimes\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}}))

\displaystyle\simeq\psi^{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G})))\otimes\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}}))

\displaystyle\simeq\psi^{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G})))\otimes\psi^{*}(\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V})))\otimes\Omega^{1}(V)

\displaystyle\simeq\psi^{*}(\Omega^{1}(V))\otimes\Omega^{1}(V)\simeq\Omega^{2}(V).

In particular, we have an integration along the fibers map

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}^{2}:C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{2}),\Omega^{2}(V))\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

which by (
3.15
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}^{2}=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\circ\psi_{*}

(3.38)

In the following lemma, we insist on equivariance. More precisely, we prove that
U
satisfies (
3.39
).
This won’t be needed in the proof of this theorem, but it will be needed later on in the proof of
2.6.6
and
2.8.3
.

Lemma 3.5.2
.

For any
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant open neighborhood
L\subseteq V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
of
V\times V\times M\times\{0\}
,
there exists an open neighborhood
U\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
of
V\times M\times\{0\}
such that if
(w,x,t)\in U
and
(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,t)\in U
, then
(v,w,x,t)\in L
. Furthermore,
U
satisfies the following:

\displaystyle\forall v\in V,x\in M,\lambda,t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\text{ if }(v,x,\lambda t)\in U,\text{ then }(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),x,t)\in U.

(3.39)

In particular,
U
is
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant.

Proof.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be a Euclidean norm on
V
.
Since
\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
and
L
are equivariant open neighborhoods of
\{0\}\times M\times\{0\}\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
and
\{0\}\times\{0\}\times M\times\{0\}\subseteq V\times V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
respectively, it
follows that there exists a continuous function
\chi:M\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
such that

\displaystyle\{(v,x,t)\in V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:\left\lVert\alpha_{t}(v)\right\rVert<2\chi(x)\}\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})

\displaystyle\{(v,w,x,t)\in V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}:\max(\left\lVert\alpha_{t}(v)\right\rVert,\left\lVert\alpha_{t}(w)\right\rVert)<2\chi(x)\}\subseteq L.

By a compactness argument, there exists a continuous function
\kappa:M\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
such that for any
x\in M
,
v\in V
if
\left\lVert v\right\rVert<\kappa(x)
, then
\chi(e^{v}\cdot x)<2\chi(x)
.
We take
U=\{(v,x,t):\left\lVert\alpha_{t}(v)\right\rVert<\min(\chi(x),\kappa(x))\}
.
∎

By
3.5.2
applied to
L=\dom(\tilde{\psi})
, we get an equivariant open set
U\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
.
Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
such that
\supp(g_{1})\subseteq U
and
\supp(g_{2})\subseteq U
.
We define

\displaystyle\quad h(v,w,x,t)=g_{1}(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,t)g_{2}(w,x,t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\dom(\tilde{\psi}),\Omega^{2}(V))

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g_{1}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g_{2}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}^{2}(h\circ\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{eqn:QV2_is_QV_times_QV}

(3.40)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\psi_{*}(h\circ\pi_{V\times V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{eqn:klqsjdkfjqsmd}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{\psi}_{*}(h)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})

\displaystyle\text{by }\eqref{eqn:integration_pullback_hat_psi}

So,
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is closed under convolution.

∎

Remark 3.5.3
.

In the proof of
Section

2.6.2
.
iii
, using (
3.18
) and (
3.17
), we proved that if
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis, then

•

for any
g_{1},g_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
, we can find a
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g_{1}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g_{2}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})-\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

\displaystyle g(v,x,0)=\int_{w\in V}g_{1}(w,x,0)g_{2}(w^{-1}v,x,0),\quad\forall v\in V,x\in M

•

for any
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
, we can find an
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})^{*}-\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(h\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle h(v,x,0)=\overline{g(-v,x,0)},\quad\forall v\in V,x\in M

Proof of
2.6.5
.

Since
\delta_{u\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}^{*}=\delta_{\overline{u}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}
and
\theta_{k}(X)^{*}=-\theta_{k}(X)
, and using
Section

2.6.2
.
iii
,
we only need to consider
right multiplication by invariant functions.
By
Section

1.6.4
.
iii
,
C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a two-sided ideal in
C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So in the proof of
2.6.5
, we only need to consider invariant functions of the form
\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis. We have

\displaystyle\delta_{u\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(l\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}),

where
l(v,x,t)=u(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,t)g(v,x,t)
.
This proves
Section

2.6.5
.
i
.
For
Section

2.6.5
.
ii
, let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
and
X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
.
By the definition of a
\nu
-graded basis, we can find a finite family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M)
,
(v_{i})_{i\in I}\subseteq V
such that
v_{i}\in V^{a(i)}
with
a(i)\preceq k
and

\natural(X)=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\natural(v_{i})
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(X)=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{\left(f_{i}t^{k-a(i)}\right)\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}\ast\theta_{a(i)}(\natural(v_{i})).

(3.41)

So, by
Section

2.6.5
.
i
, we need to show that if
v_{0}\in V^{k}
, then
\theta_{k}(\natural(v_{0}))\ast C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let
X=\natural(v_{0})
,
\tilde{\psi}
and
\psi
be as in
3.2.3
,
\tilde{X},\hat{X}
be partially-defined vector fields on
V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
and
V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
respectively by the formulas

\displaystyle\tilde{X}(v,x,t)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\tilde{\psi}(\tau v_{0},v,x,t)\right\rvert_{\tau=0},\quad\hat{X}(v,\gamma)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\psi(\tau v_{0},v,\gamma)\right\rvert_{\tau=0}.

(3.42)

Here, we use
Section

3.2.3
.
ii
to ensure that
\tilde{X}
and
\hat{X}
are vector fields.
The vector fields
\tilde{X}
and
\hat{X}
are well-defined in open neighborhoods of
V\times M\times\{0\}
and
V\times\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
.
We claim that

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V}(\hat{X})=\theta_{k}(X)_{R}\circ\mathcal{Q}_{V}

(3.43)

where
\theta_{k}(X)_{R}
is the right-invariant vector field associated to
\theta_{k}(X)
by
1.3.3
.
By density of
V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
in
V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, it is enough to check (
3.43
) for
t\neq 0
.
We have for
t\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_{V}(\hat{X})(v,x,t)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathcal{Q}_{V}(\psi(\tau v_{0},v,x,t))\right\rvert_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}(e^{\tau t^{k}v_{0}}e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,x,t)\right\rvert_{\tau=0}=\theta_{k}(X)_{R}(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,x,t)=\theta_{k}(X)_{R}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,x,t)),

where we used (
3.15
) in the second equality and (
1.23
) in the third equality.
So, it follows that if
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
and
\supp(g)
is a subset of the domain of definition of
\tilde{X}
which we can suppose by
3.5.1
, then

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(X)\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})=\mathcal{L}_{\theta_{k}(X)_{R}}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\hat{X}}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\right)

(3.44)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}}(g)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

This finishes the proof of
Section

2.6.5
.
ii
.

∎

Remark 3.5.4
.

The proof of
Section

2.6.5
.
ii
shows that if
(V,\natural)
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis,
v_{0}\in V^{k}
, and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
, then
we can find
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})-\theta_{k}(\natural(v_{0}))\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(f\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle g(v,x,0)=\mathcal{L}_{X}(f)(v,x,0),

where
X\in\mathcal{X}(V)
is the right-invariant vector field on
V
associated to
v_{0}
.

Proof of
2.6.6
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
A critical observation in the proof is that
\alpha_{\lambda}:V\to V
is well-defined for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
By taking adjoint, it suffices to only consider the function

\lambda\mapsto g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)
. We will show that for each
C>0
, the function
\lambda\mapsto g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)
extends smoothly to
\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}
.
So, we fix
C>0
.
We first show that the family
(g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f))_{\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}}
is uniformly compactly supported.
By (
1.25
) and (
1.42
),

\supp(g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\subseteq\supp(g)\alpha_{\lambda}(\supp(f))
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that
\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(\supp(f))}
is a proper subset of
\mathbb{G}
.

Lemma 3.5.5
.

If
K\subseteq\mathbb{G}
is a compact subset, then

\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(K)}

is a proper subset of
\mathbb{G}
.
Furthermore, if
K\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
, then
\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(K)}\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
.

Proof.

By symmetry between
s
and
r
, it is enough to show that if
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(K)}
is a sequence such that
s(\gamma_{n})
converges in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, then
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
has a convergent subsequence.
Since
\mathbb{G}
is metrizable, we can without loss of generality suppose that
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(K)
.
By passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
\gamma_{n}=\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n})
with
\eta_{n}\in K
converges to
\eta\in K
and
\lambda_{n}\in]0,C]^{\nu}
converges to
\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}
.
We can also suppose that either
\eta_{n}\in M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, or
\eta_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The proof is now divided into the two cases:

i.

If
\eta_{n}=(A_{n},x_{n},0)
converges to
\eta=(A,x,0)
, then by
Section

2.3.3
.
ii
,
x_{n}\to x
,
A_{n}=\natural_{x_{n},0}(v_{n}L_{n})
,
A=\natural_{x,0}(vL)
where
L_{n}\in\mathrm{Grass}(V)
,
v_{n}\in V
,
L_{n}\to L
and
v_{n}\to v
.
Convergence of
s(\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n}))
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
implies that
\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(L_{n})
converges to some subspace
L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Grass}(V)
. It follows that
\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n})=(\natural_{x_{n},0}(\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(v_{n})\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(L_{n})),x_{n},0)\to(\natural_{x,0}(\alpha_{\lambda}(v)L^{\prime}),x,0)
.

ii.

If
\eta_{n}=(y_{n},x_{n},t_{n})
, then either
\eta\in M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
or
\eta\in\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\times\{0\}
.

(a)

If
\eta=(y,x,t)
, then
y_{n}\to y
,
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to t
. Convergence of
s(\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n}))
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
implies that
\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n}\to s
for some
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
This implies that
t=\lambda s
. So,
s\neq 0
. Hence,
\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(y_{n},x_{n},t_{n})=(y_{n},x_{n},\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n})\to(y,x,s)
.

(b)

If
\eta=(A,x,0)
, then by
Section

2.3.3
.
i
,
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
,
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})\to L
in
\mathrm{Grass}(V)
,
y_{n}=\exp(\alpha_{t_{n}}(v_{n}))\cdot x_{n}
for some
v_{n}\in V
,
v_{n}\to v
,
A=\natural_{x,0}(vL)
.
Convergence of
s(\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n}))
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
implies that
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n}})\to L^{\prime}
for some
L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Grass}(V)
.
Since
y_{n}=\exp(\alpha_{\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n}}(\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(v_{n})))\cdot x_{n}
and
\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(v_{n})\to\alpha_{\lambda}(v)
, it follows from
Section

2.3.3
.
i
that
(y_{n},x_{n},\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n})
converges to
(\natural_{x,0}(\alpha_{\lambda}(v)L^{\prime}),x,0)
.

If
K\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
, then only Case 2.a occurs. ∎

By
3.5.5
, we only need to show that the function
\lambda\mapsto g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)
extends to a smooth function
[0,C]^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By decomposing
f
as in (
2.27
), there are two cases to consider:

•

The first case is when
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By
3.5.5
, there exists a compact subset
K\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
such that
\supp(g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\subseteq K
for all
\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}
.
On
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
, we have

\displaystyle g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)(z,x,t)=\int_{M}g(z,y,t)f(y,x,\lambda t)

This clearly extends smoothly to
[0,C]^{\nu}
.
Furthermore,
\alpha_{0}(f)=0
. So, (
2.30
) follows.

•

The second case is when
f=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
where
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
.
We have

\displaystyle g\ast f(\gamma)=\int_{V}h(v,\gamma)g(\gamma\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,s(\gamma))^{-1})\tilde{f}(v,\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\circ s(\gamma)),

where
h(v,\gamma):\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}A(\mathbb{G})_{r(\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,s(\gamma)))}\to\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}A(\mathbb{G})_{s(\gamma)}
is the canonical isomorphism which comes from
\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},0}(\mathbb{G}))\simeq\mathcal{Q}_{V}^{*}(\Omega^{0,\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{G}))
, see
2.3.6
.
Let
\beta_{\lambda}:M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
be the map
(x,t)\mapsto(x,\lambda t)
.
Since
\alpha_{\lambda}(f)=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{f})\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
,
we have

\displaystyle g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{V}h(v,\gamma)g(\gamma\mathcal{Q}_{V}(v,s(\gamma))^{-1})\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{f})(v,\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\circ s(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\int_{V}h(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),\gamma)g(\gamma\mathcal{Q}_{V}(\alpha_{\lambda}(v),s(\gamma))^{-1})\tilde{f}(v,\beta_{\lambda}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\circ s(\gamma)),

This clearly extends to
\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}
. Furthermore,

g\ast\alpha_{0}(f)(\gamma)=g(\gamma)\int_{V}\tilde{f}(v,\beta_{0}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\circ s(\gamma))
.
This proves (
2.30
) in this case.

We have thus proved that
g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)
extends to a smooth map
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Now suppose that
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Again by decomposing
f
and
g
as in (
2.27
), we reduce to the case where

f=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
and
g=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{g}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
where
\tilde{f},\tilde{g}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
.
Let
\psi
be as in
3.2.3
,
\tilde{\psi}_{\lambda}
the map
(v,w,x,t)\mapsto\psi(v,\alpha_{\lambda}(w),x,t)
.

Lemma 3.5.6
.

There exists an open
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
-equivariant neighborhood
L\subseteq\dom(\tilde{\psi})
of
V\times V\times M\times\{0\}
such that for any
\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}
,
\tilde{\psi}_{\lambda}

is a submersion on
L_{\lambda}:=\{(v,w,x,t):(v,\alpha_{\lambda}(w),x,t)\in L\}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we suppose
1\leq C
.
Let
L
be the set of points
(v,w,x,t)
such that for any
\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}
,
(v,\alpha_{\lambda}(w),x,t)\in\dom(\tilde{\psi})
and the differential of
\tilde{\psi}
at
(v,\alpha_{\lambda}(w),x,t)
is surjective as a map
V\times\{0\}\times T_{x}M\times\mathbb{R}\to V\times T_{x}M\times\mathbb{R}
.
The set
L\subseteq\dom(\tilde{\psi})
because
1\leq C
.
It is equivariant by equivariance of
\tilde{\psi}
.
It is open by compactness of
[0,C]^{\nu}
.
By
Section

3.2.3
.
i
, it contains
V\times V\times M\times\{0\}
.
∎

We apply
3.5.2
with
L
to get an equivariant open set
U\subseteq\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V})
.
By
3.5.2
,
we can suppose that
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{g}
are supported in
U
.
Since
U
is equivariant,
\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{f})
is also supported in
U
for all
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
Consider

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}(v,w,x,t)=\tilde{g}(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,t)\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{f})(w,x,t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(L,\Omega^{2}(V)),

\displaystyle\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}

\displaystyle k_{\lambda}(v,w,x,t)=\tilde{g}(v,e^{\alpha_{\lambda t}(w)}\cdot x,t)\tilde{f}(w,x,\lambda t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(L_{\lambda},\Omega^{2}(V)),

\displaystyle\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}.

We remark that to show that
k_{\lambda}
is supported in
L_{\lambda}
, we use (
3.39
).
As in (
3.40
), we have

\displaystyle g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{\psi}_{*}(h_{\lambda})\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}).

(3.45)

So, we need to prove that
\lambda\in]0,C]^{\nu}\to C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
extends smoothly to
[0,C]\nu
. To this end,

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{*}(h_{\lambda})(v^{\prime},x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{\psi}(v,w,x,t)=(v^{\prime},x,t)}\tilde{g}(v,e^{\alpha_{t}(w)}\cdot x,t)\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{f})(w,x,t)

(3.46)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{\psi}(v,\alpha_{\lambda}(w),x,t)=(v^{\prime},x,t)}\tilde{g}(v,e^{\alpha_{\lambda t}(w)}\cdot x,t)\tilde{f}(w,x,\lambda t)=\tilde{\psi}_{\lambda*}(k_{\lambda})(v^{\prime},x,t).

The map
\tilde{\psi}_{\lambda}
and
k_{\lambda}
extend smoothly to
\lambda\in[0,C]^{\nu}
.
Since the maps
(k_{\lambda})_{[0,C]^{\nu}}
are uniformly compactly supported, the result follows.

∎

3.6
Proofs of the results in Section
2.7

Proof of
2.7.2
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By writing
D
as sum of monomials, and using
1.3.6
, we see that
\theta_{k}(D)\ast f(y,x,t)=t^{k}D_{y}(f)(y,x,t)
,
where
D_{y}
means that
D
acts on the
y
-variable.
It follows that
\theta_{k}(D)\ast f
on
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
doesn’t depend on the presentation of
D
as sum of monomials.
By density of
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
in
\mathbb{G}
, independence of the presentation of
D
as sum of monomials follows.
For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, the distribution
\theta_{k}(\delta_{1})
is in the center of
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, see
Section

1.6.3
.
ii
.
From this (
2.31
) follows.

∎

Proof of
2.7.4
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then
f=\theta_{l}(\delta_{1})\ast g
, where
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is defined by
g(y,x,t)=t^{-l}f(y,x,t)
.
This proves
Section

2.7.4
.
i
.
Let us prove
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})^{*}=C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We need to show that if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then
f\ast\theta_{k}(D)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By writing
D
as sum of monomials, we can suppose that
D
is a monomial.
By using the fact that
\theta_{l}(\delta_{1})
is in the center of
C^{-\infty}_{r,s,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
for any
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, we deduce that it suffices to show that if
X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
, then
f\ast\theta_{k}(X)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
We fix a linear basis of
V
of pure elements
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}
such that
v_{i}\in V^{\omega(i)}
for all
i
.
By the definition of a
\nu
-graded basis, we can write
\natural(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\natural(v_{i})
with
f_{i}=0
if
\omega(i)\npreceq k
.
So,
\theta_{k}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{k-\omega(i)}(\delta_{1})\ast\delta_{f_{i}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(v_{i})
. Hence,

\displaystyle f\ast\theta_{k}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{k-\omega(i)}(\delta_{1})\ast f\ast\delta_{f_{i}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(\natural(v_{i})).

By replacing
f
with
f\ast\delta_{f_{i}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}}
(here we use
Section

2.6.5
.
i
), we deduce that it suffices to show that for any
i\in[\![1,n]\!]
, if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
f\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(\natural(v_{i}))\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(i)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Let
\tilde{\psi}
be as in
3.2.3
.
For each
i\in[\![1,n]\!]
, as in (
3.42
), let
\tilde{X}_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i}
be the partially-defined vector fields on
V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{i}(v,x,t)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\tilde{\psi}(\tau v_{i},v,x,t)\right\rvert_{\tau=0},

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{i}(v,x,t)=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\tilde{\psi}(v,\tau v_{i},x,t)\right\rvert_{\tau=0}

(3.47)

where we use
Section

3.2.3
.
ii
to make sure that
\tilde{X}_{i}
and
\tilde{Y}_{i}
are vector fields.
The vector fields
\tilde{X}_{i}
and
\tilde{Y}_{i}
are tangent to
V
.
By equivariance of
\tilde{\psi}
, the vector fields are well-defined in an equivariant neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
, and
\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{X}_{i})=\lambda^{\omega(i)}\tilde{X}_{i}
and
\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{Y}_{i})=\lambda^{\omega(i)}\tilde{Y}_{i}
.
By
Section

3.2.3
.
i
, on
V\times M\times\{0\}
,

\tilde{X}_{i}
and
\tilde{Y}_{i}
are the right and left invariant vector fields on
V
associated to
v_{i}
.
So, on
V\times M\times\{0\}
, the vector fields
\tilde{X}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{X}_{n}
are linearly independent.
Let
U\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
be the set of point at which the vector fields
\tilde{X}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{X}_{n}
are well-defined and linearly independent, and the vector fields
\tilde{Y}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{Y}_{n}
are well-defined.
The set
U
is an equivariant neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
.
On
U
, the vector fields
\tilde{X}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{X}_{n}
linearly span the vector fields tangent to
V
, so there exists a unique family
(P_{i,j})_{i,j\subseteq[\![1,n]\!]}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U)
such that

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}\tilde{X}_{j}.

Again, by equivariance,
\alpha_{\lambda}(P_{i,j})=\lambda^{\omega(i)-\omega(j)}P_{i,j}
.
On
U
, the differential operators
\mathcal{L}_{P_{i,j}\tilde{X}_{j}}(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(P_{i,j}\cdot)
acting on
C^{\infty}(U,\Omega^{1}(V))
have the same classical principal symbol.
So, there exists a smooth function
H_{i,j}\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{P_{i,j}\tilde{X}_{j}}(g)=\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(P_{i,j}g)+H_{i,j}g,\quad\forall g\in C^{\infty}(U,\Omega^{1}(V)).

By equivariance,
\alpha_{\lambda}(H_{i,j})=\lambda^{\omega(i)}H_{i,j}
.
By replacing
U
with a smaller open neighborhood of
V\times M\times\{0\}
, we can suppose that
U
satisfies (
3.39
), see proof of
3.5.2
.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}
, let
\max(k,0):=(\max(k_{1},0),\cdots,\max(k_{\nu},0))\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
. If
P\in C^{\infty}(U)
satisfies
\alpha_{\lambda}(P)=\lambda^{k}(P)
for all
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, then there exists
P^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that
P(v,x,t)=t^{\max(k,0)}P^{\prime}(v,x,t)
.

Proof.

If
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, let
\tilde{t},\accentset{\approx}{t}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
be defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{t}_{i}=\begin{cases}t_{i}\quad\text{if }k_{i}\geq 0\\
1\quad\text{if }k_{i}<0\end{cases},\quad\accentset{\approx}{t}_{i}=\begin{cases}1\quad\text{if }k_{i}\geq 0\\
t_{i}\quad\text{if }k_{i}<0\end{cases}.

We have
\tilde{t}\accentset{\approx}{t}=t
. So,
P(v,x,t)=\alpha_{\tilde{t}}(P)(\alpha_{\tilde{t}}(v),x,\accentset{\approx}{t})=\tilde{t}^{k}P(\alpha_{\tilde{t}}(v),x,\accentset{\approx}{t})=t^{\max(k,0)}P(\alpha_{\tilde{t}}(v),x,\accentset{\approx}{t})
. We can thus take
P^{\prime}(v,x,t)=P(\alpha_{\tilde{t}}(v),x,\accentset{\approx}{t})

which extends smoothly to
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
Notice that
P^{\prime}
is well-defined on
U
because of (
3.39
).
∎

Now, we will show that
f\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(\natural(v_{i}))\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(i)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By decomposing
f
as in (
2.27
), we have two cases to consider:
If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then

f\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(\natural(v_{i}))(y,x,t)=t^{\omega(i)}\natural(v_{i})_{x}(f)(y,x,t)
.
Let
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
be defined by
g(y,x,t)=\natural(v_{i})_{x}(f)(y,x,t)
.
Therefore,

f\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(\natural(v_{i}))=\theta_{\omega(i)}(\delta_{1})\ast g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(i)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

The second case is when
f=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{0}})
for some
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
.
We can suppose by
3.5.1
, that
\supp(\tilde{f})\subseteq U
.
Like in (
3.44
), we have

\displaystyle f\ast\theta_{\omega(i)}(\natural(v_{i}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{Y}_{i}}(\tilde{f})\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\mathcal{L}_{\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{i,j}\tilde{X}_{j}}(\tilde{f})\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(P_{i,j}\tilde{f})+H_{i,j}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(t^{\max(\omega(i)-\omega(j),0)}P_{i,j}^{\prime}\tilde{f})+t^{\omega(i)}H^{\prime}_{i,j}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{\omega(j)}(X_{j})\ast\theta_{\max(\omega(i)-\omega(j),0)}(\delta_{1})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(P_{i,j}^{\prime}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{\omega(i)}(\delta_{1})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(H_{i,j}^{\prime}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right).

By (
2.34
), the first sum belongs to
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(j)+\max(\omega(i)-\omega(j),0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and the second belongs to
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(i)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Since,
\omega(i)\preceq\omega(j)+\max(\omega(i)-\omega(j),0)
for all
i,j\in[\![1,n]\!]
, by (
2.34
), the first sum also belongs to
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(i)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
This finishes the proof of
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})^{*}=C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.
The identity
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})^{*}=C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
together with
Section

2.6.2
.
iii
imply
Section

2.7.4
.
iii
.
The inclusion
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\ast C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
easily follows from
Section

2.7.4
.
iii

∎

Proof of
2.7.6
.

If
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then

\displaystyle\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(\theta_{l}(D)\ast f)=\lambda_{i}^{l_{i}}\theta_{l}(D)\ast f.

So,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(\theta_{l}(D)\ast f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}=\theta_{l}(D)\ast\left(l_{i}f+\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}\right).

If
l_{i}\neq 0
, then
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(\theta_{l}(D)\ast f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
which is what we need to prove.
If
l_{i}=0
, then it suffices to show that
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
By decomposing
f
as in (
2.33
), there are two cases to consider:
If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}=t_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}f=\theta_{(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(1)\ast\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

If
f=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
for some
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
, then

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(\tilde{f})\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

(3.48)

Let
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\in V
be a pure basis such that
v_{j}\in V^{\omega(j)}
,
\tilde{X}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{X}_{n}
as in (
3.47
),

(v_{j}^{*})_{j\in[\![1,n]\!]}\subseteq C^{\infty}(V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu})
the smooth functions
v_{j}^{*}(\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}v_{k},x,t)=c_{j}
.
Notice that
\alpha_{\lambda}(v_{j}^{*})=\lambda^{-\omega(j)}v_{j}^{*}
.
Let
U\subseteq V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}

be the open set at which the vector fields
\tilde{X}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{X}_{n}
are well-defined and linearly independent, and the matrix
(\tilde{X}_{j}(v_{k}^{*}))_{j,k\in[\![1,n]\!]}
is invertible.
By equivariance of
(v_{j})_{j\in[\![1,n]\!]}
and
(\tilde{X}_{j})_{j\in[\![1,n]\!]}
, it follows that
U
is equivariant.
On
V\times M\times\{0\}
, the vector fields
(\tilde{X}_{j})_{j\in[\![1,n]\!]}
are the right-invariant vector fields on
V
associated to
(v_{j})_{j\in[\![1,n]\!]}
.
Since
V
is graded nilpotent, the matrix
(\tilde{X}_{j}(v_{k}^{*}))_{j,k\in[\![1,n]\!]}
is (under some permutation of indices) an upper triangular matrix with
1
on the diagonal.
Hence,
V\times M\times\{0\}\subseteq U
.
By reducing
U
if necessary, we can suppose that
U
satisfies (
3.39
), see proof of
3.5.2
.
Since
U
satisfies (
3.39
),
3.6.1
can be used.
By
3.5.1
, we can suppose that
\supp(\tilde{f})\subseteq U
.
Let
(A_{j,k})_{j,k\in[\![1,n]\!]}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U)
be the inverse of
(\tilde{X}_{j}(v_{j}^{*}))_{j,k\in[\![1,n]\!]}
.
We have
\alpha_{\lambda}(A_{j,k})=\lambda^{\omega(j)-\omega(k)}A_{j,k}
.
On
U
, there exists unique smooth functions
(P_{i,j})_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!],j\in[\![1,n]\!]}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U)
and
(H_{i})_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U)
such that

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(g)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}=t_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}g+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(P_{i,j}g)+H_{i}g,\quad\forall g\in C^{\infty}(U,\Omega^{1}(V)),i\in[\![1,\nu]\!].

(3.49)

By replacing
g
with
v_{k}^{*}g
, we deduce that

\displaystyle P_{i,j}=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(k)_{i}v_{k}^{*}A_{k,j}.

(3.50)

So, by (
3.48
) and (
3.49
), we have

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}}\alpha_{(1,\cdots,1,\lambda_{i},1,\cdots,1)}(f)\right\rvert_{\lambda_{i}=1}

\displaystyle=\theta_{(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(\delta_{1})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\omega(k)_{i}\theta_{\omega(j)}(X_{j})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(v_{k}^{*}A_{k,j}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(H_{i}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)

It is clear that
\theta_{(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(\delta_{1})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
For the second term, since
\alpha_{\lambda}(A_{k,j})=\lambda^{\omega(k)-\omega(j)}
, by
3.6.1
,
\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(v_{k}^{*}A_{k,j}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\max(\omega(k)-\omega(j),0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Since
\omega(k)\preceq\omega(j)+\max(\omega(k)-\omega(j),0)
, by (
2.34
), we have

\displaystyle\theta_{\omega(j)}(X_{j})\ast\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(v_{k}^{*}A_{k,j}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(j)+\max(\omega(k)-\omega(j),0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\omega(k)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

So, since we multiply with the factor
\omega(k)_{i}
, only the terms with
\omega(k)_{i}>0
count, which are terms such that
(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)\preceq\omega(k)
.
So, by (
2.34
), the second sum also belongs to
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(0,\cdots,0,1,0\cdots,0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
To prove that
\mathcal{Q}_{V*}\left(\left(H_{i}\tilde{f}\right)\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}\right)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(0,\cdots,0,1,0,\cdots,0)}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
by
3.6.1
, it suffices to show that
H_{i}
can be written as a sum of functions of the form
BC
with
\alpha_{\lambda}(B)=\lambda^{l}B
and
0<l_{i}
for some
l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}
.
Let
\mu\in\Omega^{1}(V)
be a non-zero density. We see
\mu
as a constant function in
C^{\infty}(U,\Omega^{1}(V))
.
We have
\alpha_{\lambda}(\mu)=\lambda^{-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega(j)}\mu
.
So, by replacing
g
with
\mu
in (
3.49
),
we deduce that

\displaystyle H_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(P_{i,j}\mu)}{\mu}-\omega(j)_{i}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\omega(k)_{i}v_{k}^{*}\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(A_{k,j}\mu)}{\mu}+(\omega(k)_{i}-\omega(j)_{i})\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(v_{k}^{*})A_{k,j}.

For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(A_{k,j}\mu)}{\mu}\right)=\lambda^{\omega(k)}\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(A_{k,j}\mu)}{\mu}

Since we only sum over terms with
\omega(k)_{i}>0
, we take
B=\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(A_{k,j}\mu)}{\mu}
and
C=1
.
For the second term either
\omega(k)_{i}<\omega(j)_{i}
or
\omega(k)_{i}>\omega(j)_{i}
(otherwise
\omega(k)_{i}-\omega(j)_{i}=0
). In the first case we take
B=\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(v_{k}^{*})
and
C=A_{k,j}
. In the second case, we take
B=A_{k,j}
and
C=\mathcal{L}_{\tilde{X}_{j}}(v_{k}^{*})
.

∎

3.7
Proofs of the results in Section
2.8

In this section, we suppose that
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is weakly commutative.

Proof of
2.8.3
.

Lemma 3.7.1
.

There exists a
\nu
-graded Lie basis
(V,\natural)
such that
V=\oplus_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}V^{\hat{i}}
.

Proof.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis of
\mathcal{F}
.
By weak commutativity of
\mathcal{F}
, we deduce that
(\oplus_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}V^{\hat{i}},\natural_{|\oplus_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}V^{\hat{i}}})
is a
\nu
-graded basis of
\mathcal{F}
.
We define a Lie bracket on
\oplus_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}V^{\hat{i}}
as follows: If
v,w\in V^{\hat{i}}
, then
[v,w]
is equal to their Lie bracket in
V
.
If
v\in V^{\hat{i}},w\in V^{\hat{j}}
with
i\neq j
, then
[v,w]=0
.
One can check that
(\oplus_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}V^{\hat{i}},\natural_{|\oplus_{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]}V^{\hat{i}}})
is a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
∎

It suffices to show that for any
C_{1},C_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
0<C_{2}<2C_{1}
, (
2.39
) to a smooth map

\{(\lambda,\mu)\in[0,C_{1}]^{2\nu}:\lambda+\mu\in[C_{2},2C_{1}]^{\nu}\}\to C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. We fix
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.

Lemma 3.7.2
.

If
K_{1}\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
and
K_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{G}
are compact subsets,

\displaystyle K=\bigcup_{\{(\lambda,\mu)\in]0,C_{1}]^{2\nu}:\lambda+\mu\in[C_{2},2C_{1}]^{\nu}\}}\alpha_{\lambda}(K_{1})\ast\alpha_{\mu}(K_{2}),

then the closure of
K
in
\mathbb{G}
is a compact subset of
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
.

Proof.

Let
(\gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\overline{K}
be a sequence.
Since
\mathbb{G}
is metrizable, we fix a metric
d
that induces the topology on
\mathbb{G}
.
Let
(\gamma^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq K
be a sequence such that
d(\gamma_{n},\gamma_{n}^{\prime})<\frac{1}{n}
.
We write
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}=\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n})\alpha_{\mu_{n}}(\xi_{n})
. By passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda\in[0,C_{1}]^{\mu}
,
\mu_{n}\to\mu\in[0,C_{1}]^{\nu}
,
\eta_{n}\to\eta\in K_{1}
,
\xi_{n}\to\xi\in K_{2}
and

\lambda+\mu\in[C_{2},2C_{1}]^{\nu}
.
We write
\eta_{n}=(z_{n},y_{n},t_{n})
and
\eta=(z,y,t)
.
For the pair
\alpha_{\lambda_{n}}(\eta_{n})=(z_{n},y_{n},\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n})
and
\alpha_{\mu_{n}}(\xi_{n})
to be composable, necessarily
\xi_{n}=(y_{n},x_{n},\mu_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n})
for some
(x_{n})_{n\in N}\subseteq M
.
Since
\xi_{n}
converges in
\mathbb{G}
,
x_{n}\to x
and
\mu_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n}\to s
for some
x\in M
and
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n}=(\mu_{n}+\lambda_{n})^{-1}(\mu_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n}+t_{n})\to(\lambda+\mu)^{-1}(s+t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0

Therefore,
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}=(z_{n},x_{n},\lambda_{n}^{-1}t_{n})\to(z,x,(\lambda+\mu)^{-1}(s+t))\in M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
.
So,
\gamma_{n}
also converges to
(z,x,(\lambda+\mu)^{-1}(s+t))
.
∎

By decomposing
f
and
g
as in (
2.27
), the proof is divided into
4
-cases:

i.

If
f
and
g
are in
