Proofs of the results in Section
4.1

Proof of
4.1.1
.

By symmetry, we only consider the first integral.
By (
2.35
), we can suppose that
f=h\ast\theta_{l}(D)
for some
h\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)

such that
k-l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
By (
2.32
),

\displaystyle\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}}=\left(\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(h)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{k-l+\underline{1}}}\right)\ast\theta_{l}(D)

The integral
\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}g\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(h)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{k-l+\underline{1}}}

converges using
2.6.6
.

∎

The following identities which follow from (
2.32
) will be used repeatedly

\displaystyle I_{k}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{k}(f)),

(5.1)

\displaystyle I_{k+l}(\theta_{l}(D)\ast f)

\displaystyle=\theta_{l}(D)\ast I_{k}(f),

\displaystyle I_{k+l}(f\ast\theta_{l}(D))

\displaystyle=I_{k}(f)\ast\theta_{l}(D),

where
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. It is useful in this chapter to introduce the spaces

\displaystyle\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})=I_{k}(C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})),\quad\Psi^{\preceq k}(\mathbb{G}):=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu},l\preceq k}\Psi^{l}(\mathbb{G}),\quad\Psi^{\prec k}(\mathbb{G}):=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu},l\prec k}\Psi^{l}(\mathbb{G}).

By (
5.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\theta_{l}(\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M))\ast\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\Psi^{k+l}(\mathbb{G}),\quad\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})\ast\theta_{l}(\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M))\subseteq\Psi^{k+l}(\mathbb{G}).

(5.2)

Lemma 5.1.1
.

i.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
,

\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})^{*}=\Psi^{\overline{k}}(\mathbb{G})
and
\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})\ast\Psi^{l}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\Psi^{k+l}(\mathbb{G})
.

ii.

If
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
, then
\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq L^{1}(\mathbb{G},\omega)
for any
\omega\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}})
obtained from
2.5.2
.

iii.

If
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then
\theta_{l}(\mathrm{DO}^{l}_{c}(M))\subseteq\Psi^{\preceq l}(\mathbb{G})
.

Proof.

The identity
\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})^{*}=\Psi^{\overline{k}}(\mathbb{G})
follows from
Section

2.7.4
.
ii
.
To prove
\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})\ast\Psi^{l}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\Psi^{k+l}(\mathbb{G})
, by using (
2.33
), (
2.35
), (
5.1
) and (
5.2
),
it suffices to show that
if
k,l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
and
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
I_{k}(f)\ast I_{l}(g)\in\Psi^{k+l}(\mathbb{G})
.
For each
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
, let

\displaystyle\Lambda_{S}=\{(\lambda,\mu)\in]0,1]^{2\nu}:\forall i\in S,\lambda_{i}\leq\mu_{i},\ \forall i\in S^{c},\lambda_{i}>\mu_{i}\}.

By a simple change of variables, we have

\displaystyle I_{k}(f)\ast I_{l}(g)=\int_{]0,1]^{2\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast\alpha_{\mu}(g)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}\mu^{l+\underline{1}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\int_{\Lambda_{S}}\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast\alpha_{\mu}(g)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}\mu^{l+\underline{1}}}

(5.3)

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}I_{k+l}\left(\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda_{S}}(f)\ast\alpha_{\lambda_{S^{c}}}(g)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\tau_{S}}}\right),

where

\displaystyle\lambda_{S}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}&\text{if }i\in S\\
1&\text{if }i\notin S\end{cases},\quad\lambda_{S^{c}}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}&\text{if }i\notin S\\
1&\text{if }i\in S\end{cases},\quad\tau_{Si}=\begin{cases}k_{i}+1&\text{if }i\in S\\
l_{i}+1&\text{if }i\notin S\end{cases}.

(5.4)

By
2.8.3
, the integral
\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda_{S}}(f)\ast\alpha_{\lambda_{S^{c}}}(g)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\tau_{S}}}
defines an element of
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

5.1.1
.
i
.

Section

5.1.1
.
ii
follows immediately from (
2.25
).

To prove
Section

5.1.1
.
iii
, by (
5.2
), it suffices to show that
\theta_{0}(C^{\infty}_{c}(M))\subseteq\Psi^{\preceq 0}(\mathbb{G})
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
.
We fix
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
any invariant function such that
\alpha_{0}(g)=\theta_{0}(\delta_{f})
, see (
2.30
).
For example, after fixing a
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
, one takes
g=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{g}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
, where
\tilde{g}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
satisfies
\int_{v\in V}\tilde{g}(v,x,0)=f(x)
.
Clearly,

\displaystyle\theta_{0}(\delta_{f})=\alpha_{0}(g)=\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\frac{\partial^{\nu}}{\partial\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\partial\lambda_{\nu}}\Big((\lambda_{1}-1)\cdots(\lambda_{\nu}-1)\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda.

(5.5)

Let
S=\{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}\}\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
. We define

\displaystyle g_{S}=\left(\frac{\partial^{|S|}}{\partial\lambda_{s_{1}}\cdots\mathop{}\!\partial\lambda_{s_{n}}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\right)_{\lambda_{s_{1}}=1,\cdots,\lambda_{s_{n}}=1}.

(5.6)

By the chain rule,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{|S|}}{\partial\lambda_{s_{1}}\cdots\mathop{}\!\partial\lambda_{s_{n}}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{s_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{s_{n}}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g_{S}),\quad\forall\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}.

(5.7)

So, putting (
5.5
) and (
5.7
) together, we obtain

\displaystyle\theta_{0}(\delta_{f})=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g_{S})\prod_{s\in S}\left(1-\lambda_{s}^{-1}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S^{\prime}|}I_{\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(g_{S}),

(5.8)

where
\kappa_{S^{\prime}i}=-1
if
i\notin S^{\prime}
,
\kappa_{S^{\prime}i}=0
if
i\in S^{\prime}
.
By
2.7.6
,
g_{S}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\kappa_{S}+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Since
\kappa_{S^{\prime}}+\underline{1}\preceq\kappa_{S}+\underline{1}
, by (
2.34
),
g_{S}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\kappa_{S^{\prime}}+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So,
I_{\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(g_{S})\in\Psi^{\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\Psi^{\preceq 0}(\mathbb{G})
.
∎

Remark 5.1.2
.

By (
5.1
) and (
2.33
),
any element
\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)
can be written as sum of elements of the form
D\ast v
where
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
and
v\in\Psi^{k-l}_{c}(M)
with
k-l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
This remark will be used repeatedly to reduce theorems about positive order operators to negative order operators.

Proof of
4.1.3
.

Let us prove
Section

4.1.3
.
i
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. We fix any
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu})
such that
\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}g(t)\frac{dt}{t^{k+\underline{1}}}=1
.
So,
f(y,x)g(t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. By
Section

2.7.4
.
i
, it follows that
f(y,x)g(t)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Clearly,
I_{k}(fg)_{|\underline{1}}=f
.

Let us prove
Section

4.1.3
.
ii
. Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
\pi:\mathbb{G}\to M\times M
be the natural projection

\displaystyle\pi(y,x,t)=(y,x),\quad\pi(A,x,0)=(x,x).

(5.9)

The function
\pi
is smooth because
r,s,\pi_{\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}
are smooth.
By (
1.34
) and (
1.42
),

\displaystyle\supp\left(I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}\right)=\supp(I_{k}(f))\cap(M\times M\times\{\underline{1}\})\subseteq\pi\left(\supp(I_{k}(f))\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\pi\left(\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,1]^{\nu}}\supp(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in]0,1]^{\nu}}\pi\left(\alpha_{\lambda}(\supp(f))\right)}

\displaystyle=\pi(\supp(f)).

So,
\supp(I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}})
is compact.
To compute the wave-front set, by
5.1.2
, we can suppose that
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
We have that

\displaystyle I_{k}(C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))_{|\underline{1}}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(5.10)

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis as in
3.7.1
,
\tilde{f}
as in (
2.27
).
By (
5.10
),

\displaystyle I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}-\phi_{*}\left(\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\tilde{f}(\alpha_{t^{-1}}(a),x,t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{k+\underline{1}}}\right)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

where
\phi:V\times M\to M\times M
is the map
(a,x)\mapsto(e^{a}\cdot x,x)
.
For each
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
, let
V_{S}=\{\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}:v_{i}\in V^{\hat{i}}\}\subseteq V
.
Since
\tilde{f}
is compactly supported, it is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}\left(\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\tilde{f}(\alpha_{t^{-1}}(a),x,t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{k+\underline{1}}}\right)\subseteq\bigcup_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}N^{V\times M}_{V_{S}\times M},

where
N^{V\times M}_{V_{S}\times M}
is the conormal bundle of
V_{S}\times M
in
V\times M
.
For each
S\neq\emptyset
, the map
\phi
seen as a map
V_{S}\times M\to M\times M
is a submersion because of (
2.4
), and the map
\phi
seen as a map
V_{\emptyset}\times M\to\{(x,x):x\in M\}
is a submersion.

Section

4.1.3
.
ii
now follows from the following:

Lemma 5.1.3

(
[
25
, Eq. (3.6) on p. 332]
)
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}
be smooth manifolds,
\phi:M\to M^{\prime}
a submersion,
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{n}\subseteq M
and
Z^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,Z_{n}^{\prime}\subseteq M^{\prime}
submanifolds such that
\phi(Z_{i})\subseteq Z^{\prime}_{i}
and
\phi_{|Z_{i}}:Z_{i}\to Z^{\prime}_{i}
is a submersion,

w\in C^{-\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{1}(\ker(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi)))
a distribution.
If
\mathrm{WF}(w)\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}N^{M}_{Z_{i}}
, then
\mathrm{WF}(\phi_{*}(u))\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}N^{M^{\prime}}_{Z_{i}^{\prime}}
.

Let us prove
Section

4.1.3
.
iii
. Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By (
5.1
),
I_{l}(\theta_{l-k}(\delta_{1})\ast f)=\theta_{l-k}(\delta_{1})\ast I_{k}(f)
.
Since the restriction of
\theta_{l-k}(\delta_{1})\ast I_{k}(f)
at
t=\underline{1}
is equal to
I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}
,
Section

4.1.3
.
iii
follows.

Section

4.1.3
.
iv
follows from taking the restriction at
t=\underline{1}
in
Section

5.1.1
.
iii
and using
Section

4.1.3
.
iii
.

Section

4.1.3
.
v
follows from
Section

5.1.1
.
i
.

To prove
Section

4.1.3
.
vi
, notice that any section of
C^{\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}})
which satisfies (
1.27
) is of the form
\omega(y,x)=\mathrm{vol}_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\mathrm{vol}_{x}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for some Riemannian metric on
M
.
So, the result follows from
Section

5.1.1
.
ii
by restricting at
t=\underline{1}
.

Section

4.1.3
.
vii
is a corollary of the classical Sobolev embedding theorem together with
Section

4.1.3
.
v
.
Its proof is divided into several lemmas:

Lemma 5.1.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
,
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
.
If
\sum_{i\in S}\Re(k_{i})N_{i}^{-1}<-\dim(M)-1-\nu-n
, then

\displaystyle\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)\subseteq C^{n}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Proof.

The Sobolev inclusion,
W^{1,\dim(M)+1}(\mathbb{R}^{\dim(M)})\subseteq C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\dim(M)})
(the
L^{1}
Sobolev space of order
\dim(M)+1
is contained in
C^{0}
), implies that if
T\in L^{1}_{s}(M\times M,\omega)
satisfies
X_{1}\ast\cdots\ast X_{\dim(M)+1}\ast T\in L^{1}_{s}(M\times M,\omega)
for every
X_{1},\cdots,X_{\dim(M)+1}\in\mathcal{X}(M)
, then
T\in C^{0}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let
X\in\mathcal{X}(M)
. Since
X\in\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,N_{i},0,\cdots,0)}
.
By
Section

4.1.3
.
iv
and
Section

4.1.3
.
v
,

\displaystyle X\ast\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)\subseteq\Psi^{(k_{1},\cdots,k_{i-1},k_{i}+N_{i},k_{i+1},\cdots,k_{\nu})}_{c}(M),\quad\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)\ast X\subseteq\Psi^{(k_{1},\cdots,k_{i-1},k_{i}+N_{i},k_{i+1},\cdots,k_{\nu})}_{c}(M).

Now, the lemma follows from the Sobolev inequalities and
Section

4.1.3
.
vi
.
∎

Lemma 5.1.5
.

For any
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
, let
l_{S}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
be defined by
l_{Si}=l_{i}
if
i\in S
and
0
otherwise.
So,
l=l_{S}+l_{S^{c}}
. Then, any
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
can be written as a sum of differential operators of the form
D_{1}D_{2}
with
D_{1}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{S}}(M)
and
D_{2}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{S^{c}}}(M)
.

Proof.

We can suppose that
D
is a monomial. Furthermore, by (
2.36
), we can further suppose that
D
is a monomial of the form
X_{1}\cdots X_{n}
with
X_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{k_{i}}
and each
k_{i}
is the form

(\overbrace{0,\cdots,0}^{a},b,\overbrace{0\cdots,0}^{\nu-a-1})
for some
a\in[\![0,\nu-1]\!]
, and
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Now, to conclude we need to be able to reorder the vector fields. This follows by induction on
n
from the fact that if
X\in\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,a,0,\cdots,0)}
and
Y\in\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,b,0,\cdots,0)}
with
a
and
b
not in the same position, then

XY-YX=[X,Y]\in\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,a,0,\cdots,0)+(0,\cdots,0,b,0,\cdots,0)}=\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,a,0,\cdots,0)}+\mathcal{F}^{(0,\cdots,0,b,0,\cdots,0)}\qed

Lemma 5.1.6
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, any element
v\in\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)
can be written as sum of elements of the form
D\ast v^{\prime}
with
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
and
v^{\prime}\in\Psi^{k-l}_{c}(M)
such that
k-l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
and for any
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
, either
l_{i}=0
(which happens when
\Re(k_{i})<0
) or
-N_{i}\leq\Re(k_{i})-l_{i}
(which happens when
\Re(k_{i})\geq 0
)

Proof.

By
5.1.2
, we can suppose that
v=D\ast v^{\prime}
with
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
and
v^{\prime}\in\Psi^{k-l}_{c}(M)
and
k-l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
We now optimize this decomposition on each parameter. More precisely, staring from the last parameter, using
5.1.5
, we can suppose that
D=D_{1}D_{2}
with
D_{1}\in\mathrm{DO}^{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{\nu-1},0)}
and
D_{2}\in\mathrm{DO}^{(0,\cdots,0,l_{\nu})}
.
By writing
D_{2}
as sum of monomials, we can suppose that either
l_{\nu}=0
or
-N_{\nu}\leq\Re(k_{\nu})-l_{\nu}
. We then do the same for the other parameters.
∎

Now, let
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\Re(k_{i})N_{i}^{\sgn(\Re(k_{i}))}\leq-\dim(M)-1-\nu-\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}N_{i}^{2}-n
and
v\in\Psi^{k}(M)
.
We write
v
as in
5.1.6
.
Let
S=\{i:\Re(k_{i})<0\}
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S}\Re(k_{i}-l_{i})N_{i}^{-1}=\sum_{i\in S}\Re(k_{i})N_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq-\dim(M)-1-\nu-n-\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}N_{i}^{2}-\sum_{i\notin S}\Re(k_{i})N_{i}

\displaystyle\leq-\dim(M)-1-\nu-n-\sum_{i\notin S}l_{i}N_{i}

So, by
5.1.4
,
v^{\prime}\in C^{n+\sum_{i\notin S}l_{i}N_{i}}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Since
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
is a differential operator of classical order
\leq\sum_{i\notin S}l_{i}N_{i}
,
Section

4.1.3
.
vii
follows.

Let us prove
Section

4.1.3
.
viii
. By
5.1.2
and
Section

2.7.1
.
iii
, we can suppose that
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
h\in C^{\infty}(M\times M)
defined by
h(x,y)=g(x)-g(y)
,
\tilde{h}=h\circ\pi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
where
\pi
is defined in (
5.9
).
Since
\pi\circ\alpha_{\lambda}=\pi
,
\alpha_{\lambda}(\tilde{h})=\tilde{h}
for all
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle I_{k}(\tilde{h}f)_{|\underline{1}}=\delta_{g}\ast I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}-I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}\ast\delta_{g}.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis,
\tilde{f}
as in
2.6.1
.
We define
r\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
as follows:

\displaystyle r(v,x,t)=h(e^{\alpha_{t}(v)}\cdot x,x)\tilde{f}(v,x,t).

Notice that
\mathcal{Q}_{V_{*}}(r\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})=\tilde{h}\mathcal{Q}_{V^{*}}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})
.
So,

\displaystyle\tilde{h}f-\mathcal{Q}_{V_{*}}(r\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})=\tilde{h}(f-\mathcal{Q}_{V^{*}}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

By (
5.10
), it suffices to show that
I_{k}(\mathcal{Q}_{V_{*}}(r\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))_{|\underline{1}}\in\Psi^{\prec k}(M)
.
The function
r
vanishes at
t=0
because
h
vanishes on the diagonal.
So,
r=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}t_{i}r_{i}
for some functions
r_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
.
Hence,

\displaystyle I_{k}(\mathcal{Q}_{V_{*}}(r\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))_{|\underline{1}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}I_{k-(\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{i-1},1,\underbrace{0,\cdots,0}_{\nu-i})}(\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(r_{i}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))_{|\underline{1}}.

This finishes the proof of
4.1.3
.

∎

Proof of
4.1.4
.

If
v
and
v^{\prime}
both satisfy the condition of theorem, then
v-v^{\prime}\in\Psi^{k-(n,0,\cdots,0)}(M)+\cdots+\Psi^{k-(0,\cdots,0,n)}(M)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
It follows from
Section

4.1.3
.
vii
that
v-v^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. This proves uniqueness.

We now prove existence.
The set
S=\{l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}:l_{i}\leq\max(\Re(k_{i})+N_{i},0),\ \forall i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]\}
is finite.
By
Section

2.7.1
.
v
, we fix a family of generators of the modules
\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
for each
l\in S
.
Let
D_{1}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{1}}(M),\cdots,D_{n}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{n}}(M)
be the generators.
By
5.1.6
, each
v_{\tau}
can be written as a sum
v_{\tau}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\ast v_{\tau,i}
with
v_{\tau,i}\in\Psi^{k-l_{i}-\tau}(M)
and
k-l_{i}-\tau\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
By convention,
v_{\tau,i}=0
if
v_{\tau}=0
.
It is enough to prove the existence of the asymptotic sum
\sum_{\tau}v_{\tau,i}
for each
i
.
Here, we make use of the fact that for each
i\in[\![1,n]\!]
,
\{\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}:v_{\tau,i}\neq 0\}\subseteq\{\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}:v_{\tau}\neq 0\}
.

Therefore, without loss of generality we can suppose that
k-\tau\in\mathbb{C}_{-}^{\nu}
for every
v_{\tau}\neq 0
.
In the following argument, we only run over
\tau
’s such that
v_{\tau}\neq 0
.
By using a partition of unity argument, we can suppose that for some compact
K\subseteq M\times M
,
\supp(v_{\tau})\subseteq K
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.
There exists functions
f_{\tau}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
v_{\tau}(y,x)=I_{k-\tau}(f_{\tau})_{|\underline{1}}
.
We fix a
\nu
-graded basis
(V,\natural)
.
Let
\tilde{f}_{\tau}
be as in (
2.27
).
By (
5.10
), we can suppose that
v_{\tau}(y,x)=I_{k-\tau}(\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}_{\tau}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))_{|\underline{1}}
.
Furthermore, by
3.5.1
, we can suppose that the supports of the functions
\tilde{f}_{\tau}(v,x,t)
are uniformly compact.
Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{\nu})
equal to
1
in a neighborhood of
0
.
Consider

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(v,x,t)=\sum_{\tau\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}\chi(\epsilon_{\tau}t)t^{\tau}\tilde{f}_{\tau}(v,x,t),

where
\epsilon_{\tau}>0
are constants small enough so that the sum converges in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
.
This can be done using an elementary diagonalization argument.
Now, let

\displaystyle v=I_{k}\Big(\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\Big)_{|\underline{1}}\in\Psi^{k}_{c}(M).

It is straightforward to check that
v
satisfies the required properties, see the proof of
Section

4.1.3
.
iii
.

∎

Proof of
4.1.5
.

By (
4.6
), we can suppose that
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Clearly in the case
\nu=1
and
\mathcal{F}^{1}=\mathcal{X}(M)
, the order on differential operators defined in
Section

4.1
coincides with the classical order.
So, by
5.1.2
, we can further suppose that
k<0
.
We also choose the obvious
1
-graded basis
V=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\natural:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the obvious map whose image is the set of constant vector fields.
Notice that in this case
\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q})=V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
So, if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(V\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
(we are trivializing all densities in this proof), and
\tilde{f}=\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(f\circ\pi_{V\times M})\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then by a direct computation or using (
4.4
), we have.

\displaystyle I_{k}(\tilde{f})_{|\underline{1}}(y,x)=\int_{0}^{1}f(\frac{y-x}{t},x,t)t^{-n}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{k+1}}

By Fourier inversion formula, we have

\displaystyle f(v,x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}g(\xi,x,t)e^{i\langle\xi,v\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi,

where
g
is the Fourier transform of
f
in the first variable. Hence,

\displaystyle I_{k}(\tilde{f})_{|\underline{1}}(y,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{i\langle\xi,y-x\rangle}\int_{0}^{1}g(t\xi,x,t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{k+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

The integral
\int_{0}^{1}g(t\xi,x,t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{k+1}}
defines a classical symbol of order
k
. Furthermore, any classical symbol of order
k
can be written in this form.

∎

5.2
Proofs of the results in Section
4.2

Proof of
4.2.1
.

The set
A=M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
is a saturated subset of
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
.
Furthermore,
\mathbb{G}_{A^{c}}=\mathcal{G}
.
By
1.8.5
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{G}_{A}
and
\mathbb{G}_{A^{c}}
have the SWCP.
It is clear that the groupoid
\mathbb{G}_{A}=M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
has the SWCP.
The groupoid
\mathbb{G}_{A^{c}}
is equal to the groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
The group
V
acts on the space
\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
by the map
v\cdot(L,x)=(\natural_{x,0}(v)L\natural_{x,0}(-v),x)
.
So, we can form the crossed product groupoid
V\ltimes\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
, see (
1.14
).
We have an obvious surjective submersion map

\displaystyle V\rtimes\mathcal{G}^{(0)}\to\mathcal{G},\quad((L,x),v,(L^{\prime},x))\mapsto(\natural_{x,0}(v)L^{\prime},x)

The result now follows from
1.8.6
.

∎

Proof of
4.2.4
.

The identities (
4.10
) are rather straightforward. They come from replacing
(\xi_{n},x)
by
(\lambda\xi_{n},x_{n})
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, and
t_{n}
by
\lambda t_{n}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
.
This proves (
4.10
).

We now prove (
4.11
).
It is rather easy to see that one can replace
{(t_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
by
{(t_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
in the definition of the Helffer-Nourrigat cone. Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis.
From the definition of a
\nu
-graded basis, it follows that
\eta\in\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
if and only if there exists sequences
(\xi_{n},x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq T^{*}M
,
{(t_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0

such that
x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
, and
\xi_{n}\circ\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}\in V^{*}
converges to
\eta\circ\natural_{x,0}\in V^{*}
.

Let
\eta\in\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
.
By passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})
converges. By
2.2.2
, the limit is of the form
\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
for some
L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
.
Clearly,
\xi_{n}\circ\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})^{\perp}
. So, by
3.0.1
,
\eta\circ\natural_{x,0}\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)^{\perp}
which implies that
\eta\in L^{\perp}
.

Conversely, if
\eta\in L^{\perp}
, then there exists sequences
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq M
,
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
such that

x_{n}\to x
,
t_{n}\to 0
and
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)
.
Therefore,
\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})^{\perp}\to\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)^{\perp}
.
Since
\eta\in L^{\perp}
,
\eta\circ\natural_{x,0}\in\natural_{x,0}^{-1}(L)^{\perp}
.
By
3.0.1
, there exists
\eta_{n}\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})^{\perp}
such that
\eta_{n}\to\eta\circ\natural_{x,0}
.
Since
\eta_{n}\in\ker(\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}})^{\perp}
,
there exists unique
\xi_{n}\in T_{x_{n}}^{*}M

such that
\eta_{n}=\xi_{n}\circ\natural_{x_{n},t_{n}}
.
So,
\eta\in\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
.

The set
\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
is closed under the co-adjoint action of
\mathfrak{G}_{x}
because of
Section

2.2.10
.
ii
.

The set
\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
is closed because if
\eta_{n}\in\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
converges to
\eta\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
, then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
L_{n}\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
such that
\eta_{n}\in L_{n}^{\perp}
.
By passing to a subsequence, we can suppose that
L_{n}
converges to some
L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
. By
3.0.1
,
\eta\in L^{\perp}
.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{H\!N}_{(\xi,x)}
is closed, and closed under the co-adjoint action of
\mathfrak{G}_{x}
.
Consider the
(\nu+1)
-sub Riemannian structure of depth
(N,1)
defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{(k,0)}=\mathcal{F}^{k},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{(k,1)}=\mathcal{X}(M).

(5.11)

This structure is weakly-commutative.
Its osculating groups at
x
is equal to
\mathfrak{g}_{x}\oplus T_{x}M
, see (
2.37
).
It has its own Helffer-Nourrigat cone
\widetilde{\mathrm{H\!N}}_{x}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}\oplus T^{*}_{x}M
.
It is immediate to see that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{H\!N}}_{x}=(\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}\times\{0\})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\xi\in T^{*}_{x}M\backslash 0}\mathrm{H\!N}_{(\xi,x)}\times\{\xi\},\ \widetilde{\mathrm{H\!N}}_{(\xi,x)}=(\mathrm{H\!N}_{(\xi,x)}\times\{0\})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}}\mathrm{H\!N}_{(\xi,x)}\times\{t\xi\}

(5.12)

We have proved above that
\widetilde{\mathrm{H\!N}}_{x}
is a closed subset of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}\oplus T_{x}^{*}M
which closed under the co-adjoint action of the group
\mathfrak{G}_{x}\times T_{x}M
.
So,
\mathrm{H\!N}_{(\xi,x)}
is a closed subset of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
which is closed under the co-adjoint action of the group
\mathfrak{G}_{x}
.

∎

Proof of
4.2.5
.

Let
L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}
. By
Theorem

A.0.3
, the set of irreducible unitary representations which are weakly contained in
\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L,0}
correspond by the orbit method (
A.2
) to

\overline{\bigcup_{g\in\mathfrak{G}_{x}}(Ad(g)L)^{\perp}}/\mathfrak{G}_{x}
.
By
Section

2.2.10
.
ii
and (
4.11
), and that
\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}
is a closed subset of
\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}
, we deduce that
\mathrm{H\!N}_{x}=\bigcup_{L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}}\overline{\bigcup_{g\in\mathfrak{G}_{x}}(Ad(g)L)^{\perp}}
. The result follows.

∎

Proof of
4.2.6
.

By
4.2.5
,
\pi
is weakly contained in
\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L,0}
for some tangent cone
L
at
x
.
Let
\Xi_{(L,x,0)}
be the regular representation of the groupoid
\mathbb{G}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
, see
1.7.2
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis,
\tilde{f}
as in (
2.27
).
The space
\mathbb{G}_{(L,x,0)}=\{(gL,x,0):g\in\mathfrak{G}_{x}\}
identifies with
\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L
and
\Xi_{(L,x,0)}(f)=\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L,0}(\natural_{x,0*}(\tilde{f}(\cdot,x,0)))
.
So,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi\left(\natural_{x,0*}(\tilde{f}(\cdot,x,0))\right)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L,0}(\natural_{x,0*}(\tilde{f}(\cdot,x,0)))\right\rVert=\left\lVert\Xi_{(L,x,0)}(f)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}},

where in the first identity we used the fact that
\pi
is weakly contained in
\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L,0}
.
It follows that
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}
is well-defined and extends to
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
.
The equality

\displaystyle\pi(C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathfrak{G}_{x},\Omega^{1}\mathfrak{g}_{x}))=\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))

(5.13)

follows from the fact that given any
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathfrak{G}_{x},\Omega^{1}\mathfrak{g}_{x})
,
there exists
h^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
such that
\natural_{x,0*}(h^{\prime},x,0)=h
which implies that
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(h^{\prime}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}}))=\pi(h)
.
By
Theorem

1.7.7
applied to
\pi:C^{*}\mathfrak{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))
,
C^{\infty}(\pi)=\pi(C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathfrak{G}_{x},\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{x})))L^{2}(\pi)
. So (
4.14
) follows.
Finally, (
5.13
) and irreducibility of
\pi
imply that
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}
is irreducible.

∎

Proof of
4.2.8
.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}\mathbb{G}
is separable because
\mathbb{G}
is second countable.
So,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
is also separable.
By (
1.39
), we have a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)\to C^{*}\mathbb{G}\to C^{*}\mathcal{G}\to 0

(5.14)

The
C^{*}
-subalgebra
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
contains
\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
because
C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let

\displaystyle C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}:=\frac{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}{\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)}

which is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
C^{*}\mathcal{G}
by (
5.14
).
We have the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}\to 0.

(5.15)

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
By (
1.37
) (the
L^{1}
-estimate), if
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
vanishes on
V\times M\times\{0\}
, then
\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(g\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
.
From this observation, we can define the map

\displaystyle\phi:C^{\infty}_{c}(V\times M,\Omega^{1}(V))\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G},\quad f\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{V*}(f^{\prime}\circ\pi_{V\times\mathbb{G}^{(0)}})\bmod\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)

where
f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\dom}(\mathcal{Q}_{V}),\Omega^{1}(V))
is any smooth function such that
f^{\prime}_{|V\times M\times\{0\}}=f
.
The map
\phi
is a
*
-algebra homomorphism by
3.5.3
.
Here, the
*
-algebra structure on
V\times M
comes from the groupoid structure
V\times M\rightrightarrows M
which is the product of the groupoid
V\rightrightarrows\{\mathrm{pt}\}
and the trivial groupoid
M\rightrightarrows M
.
By the definition of the maximum
C^{*}
-algebra of
V\times M\rightrightarrows M
, it follows that
\phi
extends to a
*
-homomorphism

\displaystyle\phi:C^{*}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M)\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}.

Since any
*
-homomorphism between
C^{*}
-algebras has closed image, and
the image of
\phi
contains
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, we deduce that
\phi
is surjective.
The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M)
is of Type I. In fact, it is liminal, see
[
22
]
.
By
B.0.3
,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
is of Type I. So, by
B.0.3
once more,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
is of Type I.

We now show that (
4.15
) is a bijection.
By (
5.15
),

\displaystyle\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}=(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)\sqcup\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}}

(5.16)

and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
with its usual topology is an open subset of
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}
.
So, we need to show that
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}}=\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}
.

The groupoids are fibered over
M
.
Furthermore, the map
\phi
is a
C_{0}(M)
-
*
-homomorphism.
It follows that for any
x\in M
, the map
\phi
descend to a
*
-homomorphism between the fibers of
C^{*}(V\times M)
and
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
at
x
.
Since the groupoids
V\times M\rightrightarrows M
and
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
satisfy the SWCP, we can apply
1.8.7
.
The fiber of
C^{*}(V\times M)
is
C^{*}V
, and the fiber of
C^{*}\mathcal{G}
at
x
is
C^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
Let
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
be the fiber of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
at
x
.
Since
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
C^{*}\mathcal{G}
,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
is also a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
C^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.
This fact is folklore which follows easily from
[
56
, Proposition C.10.a and Proposition C.10.c]
.
So, we get a map
\phi_{x}:C^{*}(V)\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
.

Since
\phi
is surjective, irreducible representations of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
are the irreducible representations of
C^{*}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M)
which factor through
\ker(\phi)
.
An irreducible representation of
C^{*}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M)
is a pair
(\pi,x)
where
\pi:C^{\infty}_{c}(V,\Omega^{1}(V))\to\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))
is an irreducible unitary representation of
V
as a Lie group, and
x\in M
.
The pair
(\pi,x)
factors through
\ker(\phi)
if and only if

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi(a)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\phi_{x}(a)\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}},\quad\forall a\in C^{*}(V).

Since
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
C^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}
and the groupoid
\mathcal{G}_{x}
satisfies WCP, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}}=\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathcal{G}_{x}}=\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}\mathcal{G}_{x}}=\sup\{\left\lVert\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L}(b)\right\rVert:L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}\},\quad\forall b\in C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}_{x}.

So the pair
(\pi,x)
factors through
\ker(\phi)
if and only if

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi(a)\right\rVert\leq\sup\{\left\lVert\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L}(\phi_{x}(a))\right\rVert:L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}\},\quad\forall a\in C^{*}(V)

By density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(V,\Omega^{1}(V))
, it is enough to verify the above for
a\in C^{\infty}_{c}(V,\Omega^{1}(V))
.
If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(V,\Omega^{1}(V))
, then
\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L}(\phi_{x}(f))=\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L,0}(\natural_{x,0*}(f))
.
So, the pair
(\pi,x)
factors through
\phi
if and only if
\pi
is weakly contained in the representations
\{\Xi_{\mathfrak{G}_{x}/L}\circ\natural_{x,0*}:L\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{x}\}
.
By
4.2.5
, this set is the set of representations of
V
of the form
\pi\circ\natural_{x,0*}
where
\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}_{x}
.
This finishes the proof that
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}}=\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}
.
A careful look at the proof shows that we also showed that the map (
4.15
) is a homeomorphism between
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}
.

It remains to show that (
4.15
) is a homeomorphism.
We just need to show that if
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0
is a closed subset (with its usual Euclidean topology), then the closure of
A
in
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}
coincides with the closure of
A
in the topology in
4.2.7
.
To this end, consider the set
M\times A
as a subset of
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
. It is a saturated subset. So, its closure
\overline{M\times A}
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
is also saturated.
Therefore,
C^{*}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
is a quotient of
C^{*}\mathbb{G}
.
Let
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
be the image of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
in
C^{*}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
.
Since
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
is a quotient of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
, its spectrum is a closed subset of
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}
.
We will show that its spectrum is in fact the closure of
A
in
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}
.
To this end, it suffices to show that
A
is a dense subset of the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
.
Notice that by
1.8.5
,
\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
satisfies the SWCP.
Furthermore, by its construction
M\times A
is dense in its space of objects.
So by
1.8.1
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}}=\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{C^{*}_{r}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}}=\sup\{\pi_{t}(a):t\in A\},\quad\forall a\in C^{*}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}.

(5.17)

Since,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
is a sub
C^{*}
-algebra of
C^{*}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
, (
5.17
) remains true for
a\in C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
.
This implies that in the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
,
A
is dense.

We now compute the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
.
In fact, the groupoid
\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
is very similar to the groupoid
\mathbb{G}
. It is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}=M\times M\times A\sqcup\{(gL,x):(L,x,0)\in\overline{M\times A},g\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}\}\times\{0\},

The proof we gave above to compute the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
works equally well for
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
, to deduce that the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}_{\overline{M\times A}}
is precisely the closure of
A
in the topology in
4.2.7
.

∎

In the sequel of this chapter, is useful to remark that the set of non-singular representations have the following quite natural description.
The closure
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
in
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
will be denoted by
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
.
By
Section

2.7.4
.
ii
,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
is a two-sided ideal of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
.
So, the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}
is equal to the open subset of the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
of irreducible unitary representations of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
which don’t vanish on

C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
.

Lemma 5.2.1
.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
is separable of Type I and its spectrum is
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}\times\{0\}
.

Proof.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
is separable because it is a two-sided ideal of a separable
C^{*}
-algebra. It is of Type I by
B.0.3
.
We now compute the spectrum.
If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
f
can be written as sum of elements of the form
\theta_{k}(D)\ast g
, where
\underline{1}\preceq k
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
On
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0
,

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(D)\ast g(y,x,t)=t^{k}D_{y}g(y,x,t)

Since
\underline{1}\preceq k
,
1\leq k_{i}
for all
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
. So, if
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0
but not in
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, then
\theta_{k}(D)\ast g(y,x,t)
vanishes.
Hence,
\pi_{t}(f)
vanishes.
On the other hand, by
Section

2.7.4
.
i
, if
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, then
\pi_{t}
doesn’t vanish on
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}
.
We have thus proved that
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})}\cap\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0=(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.

By the description of the topology on
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G})}
in
4.2.8
and the fact that
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})}
is an open subset of
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}}
, it follows that
\widehat{C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}\times\{0\}
.
It remains to show that if
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
, then
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}
doesn’t vanish on
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Notice that if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathfrak{G}_{x},\Omega^{1})
and
D
is a right-invariant differential operator on
\mathfrak{G}_{x}
which satisfies
\alpha_{\lambda}(D)=\lambda^{n}D
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\nu}
, then there exists
D^{\prime}\in\mathrm{DO}^{n}(M)
and
f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
\pi(D(f))=\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\theta_{n}(D)\ast f^{\prime})
.
This clearly implies that
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}
doesn’t vanish on
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
∎

5.3
Proofs of the results in Section
4.3

Before, we proceed with the proof of
4.3.2
, we need to prove a few lemmas.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
,
u\in\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})
,
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},-k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. The limit

\displaystyle\sigma^{k}(u)\ast g:=\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}\to+\infty}\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(u)\ast g

(5.18)

exists in
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
, and thus defines a linear map
\sigma^{k}(u)\ast\cdot:C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},-k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
.

Proof.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
u=I_{k}(f)
.
By writing
f
as in (
2.35
), and
g
as in (
2.33
) and using (
5.1
), it suffices to show that if
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
with
\Re(k_{i})+l_{i}>0
for all
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}\to+\infty}\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{k}(f))\ast\theta_{l}(D)\ast g

exists in
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
.
For any
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
and
\lambda\in]1,+\infty[^{\nu}
, let

\displaystyle\Lambda_{S}^{\lambda}=\{\mu\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}:\forall i\in S,\mu_{i}\in[1,\lambda_{i}[\text{ and }\forall i\notin S,\mu_{i}\in]0,1]\}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{S}=\{\mu\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}:\forall i\in S,\mu_{i}\in[1,+\infty[\text{ and }\forall i\notin S,\mu_{i}\in]0,1]\}

By a change of variables,

\displaystyle\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{k}(f))\ast\theta_{l}(D)\ast g

\displaystyle=\int_{]0,\lambda_{1}]\times\cdots\times]0,\lambda_{\nu}]}\alpha_{\mu}(f)\ast\theta_{l}(D)\ast g\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\int_{\Lambda_{S}^{\lambda}}\alpha_{\mu}(f)\ast\theta_{l}(D)\ast g\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}

By writing
D
as in
5.1.5
, we only need to deal with integrals of the form

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{S}^{\lambda}}\alpha_{\mu}(f)\ast\theta_{l}(D_{1}D_{2})\ast g\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}=\int_{\Lambda_{S}^{\lambda}}\alpha_{\mu}(f\ast\theta_{l_{S}}(D_{1}))\ast\theta_{l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast g\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+l_{S}+\underline{1}}}

By (
2.23
),

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}\to+\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{S}^{\lambda}}\left\lVert\alpha_{\mu}(f\ast\theta_{l_{S}}(D_{1}))\ast\theta_{l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast g\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{\Re(k)+l_{S}+\underline{1}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{S}}\left\lVert\alpha_{\mu}(f\ast\theta_{l_{S}}(D_{1}))\ast\theta_{l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast g\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{\Re(k)+l_{S}+\underline{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{S}}\left\lVert f\ast\theta_{l_{S}}(D_{1})\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\left\lVert\theta_{l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast g\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{\Re(k)+l_{S}+\underline{1}}}<+\infty

The result follows.
∎

Lemma 5.3.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
u\in\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})
. If
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, then
the restriction of
\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(u)-u
to
M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
belongs to
C^{\infty}(M\times M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Furthermore, there exists
C>0
(independent of
\lambda
) such that

\displaystyle\supp(\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(u)-u)\subseteq M\times M\times[0,C]^{\nu}\backslash 0\cup\mathcal{G}\times\{0\},\quad\forall\lambda\in[1,+\infty[^{\nu}.

Proof.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
u=I_{k}(f)
.
By writing
f
as in (
2.33
), we can without loss of generality suppose that
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
.
By a change of variables,

\displaystyle\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(u)-u=\int_{]0,\lambda_{1}]\times\cdots]0,\lambda_{\nu}]}\alpha_{\mu}(f)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}-\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\alpha_{\mu}(f)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}

So,

\displaystyle\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(u)(y,x,t)-u(y,x,t)=\int_{]0,\lambda_{1}]\times\cdots]0,\lambda_{\nu}]}f(y,x,\mu t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}-\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}f(y,x,\mu t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mu^{k+\underline{1}}}

Since this integral avoids
\mu=0
(which introduces singularities from
f
being smooth on
\mathbb{G}
and not
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
) and
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{<0}
, it follows that the difference defines a smooth function.
The difference vanishes if
t
is large enough because
f
is compactly supported. So,
C
exists.
∎

Notice that
4.2.1
and
1.8.1
, and density of
M\times(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
in
\mathbb{G}^{(0)}
imply that

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}=\sup\{\left\lVert\pi_{t}(f)\right\rVert:t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\},\quad\forall f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(5.19)

The following identity is obvious

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(u)_{|t}=u_{|\lambda t},\quad\forall\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},t\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}\backslash 0,u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

(5.20)

We also recall that if
u\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\backslash 0
, then
u_{|t}
is the Schwartz kernel of
\pi_{t}(u)
. We will basically treat both objects as the same thing.

Lemma 5.3.3
.

If
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
,
u\in\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})
satisfies
u_{|\underline{1}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then for all
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},-k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\sigma^{k}(u)\ast g)=0
.
Equivalently,

\sigma^{k}(u)\ast g\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
,
see (
5.15
) and
4.2.8
.

This lemma implies that
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\sigma^{k}(u)\ast g)
only depends on
u_{|\underline{1}}
modulo
C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
g
.

Proof.

By (
5.20
) and
5.3.2
, for all
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, we have

\displaystyle u_{|t}=\alpha_{t}(u)_{|\underline{1}}=t^{k}(t^{-k}\alpha_{t}(u)_{|\underline{1}}-u_{|\underline{1}}+u_{|\underline{1}})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

Let
C
as in
5.3.2
,
\underline{C}=(C,\cdots,C)\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
There exists
h\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times[C/2,C]^{\nu},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
I_{k}(h)_{|\underline{C}}=u_{|\underline{C}}
, see the proof of
Section

4.1.3
.
i
.
By the hypothesis on the support of
h
,
I_{k}(h)_{|\lambda}=\lambda^{k}u_{|\underline{C}}
for all
\lambda\in[C,+\infty[^{\nu}
.
So, by (
5.20
) and
5.3.2
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(u-I_{k}(h))_{|\underline{C}}=(\alpha_{\lambda}(u)-\lambda^{k}u)_{|\underline{C}}=0,\quad\forall\lambda\in[1,+\infty[^{\nu}.

(5.21)

Let
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},-k+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By (
5.21
),
\pi_{\underline{C}}(\sigma^{k}(u-I_{k}(h))\ast g)=0
.
For any
\lambda,\mu\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
and
u^{\prime}\in\Psi^{k}(\mathbb{G})
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\lambda\mu}(\sigma^{k}(u^{\prime})\ast g)=\pi_{\lambda}(\alpha_{\mu}(\sigma^{k}(u^{\prime})\ast g))=\mu^{k}\pi_{\lambda}(\sigma^{k}(u^{\prime})\ast\alpha_{\mu}(g))

So,
\pi_{\lambda}(\sigma^{k}(u-I_{k}(h))\ast g)=0
for all
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
By (
5.19
),
\sigma^{k}(u-I_{k}(h))\ast g=0
. Therefore,
\sigma^{k}(u)\ast g=\sigma^{k}(I_{k}(h))\ast g
.
On the other hand, since
h
is supported away from the fiber at
0
,
using (
1.42
),
for any
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
,
\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{k}(h))\ast g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
So,
\sigma^{k}(I_{k}(h))\ast g\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.4
.

If
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
and
X\in\mathcal{F}^{l}
, then
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\theta_{l}(X))=\pi([X]_{l,x})
as maps
C^{-\infty}(\pi)\to C^{-\infty}(\pi)
.

Proof.

By density of
C^{\infty}(\pi)
in
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
, it is enough to check equality on
C^{\infty}(\pi)
.
By (
4.14
), we only need to check that both agree when composed by
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(f)
for
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded Lie basis.
We choose
V
such that there exists
v_{0}\in V^{l}
such that
\natural(v_{0})=X
(which exists by
3.1.1
).
So,
\natural_{x,0}(v_{0})=[X]_{l,x}
, and the lemma follows from
3.5.4
and the definition of
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}
in (
4.13
)
∎

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Let
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
,
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
. One has

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\pi)=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(g_{i})\xi_{i}:g_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\xi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\pi)\right\}.

Proof.

By an iterated application of
Theorem

A.0.7
together with
5.3.4
, we have

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\pi)=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\theta_{l_{i}}(D_{i}))\xi_{i}:l_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},k\preceq l_{i},D_{i}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{i}}(M),\xi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\pi)\right\}.

By applying (
4.14
) to each
\xi_{i}
, the result follows from (
2.33
).
∎

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
u\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
such that
u_{|\underline{1}}=\delta_{f}
, then for any
h\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\sigma^{0}(u)\ast h)=f(x)\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(h)
.

Proof.

By
5.3.3
, it is enough to prove the lemma for a single
u
.
We follow the notation in the proof of
Section

5.1.1
.
iii
.
So, let
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
\alpha_{0}(g)=\theta_{0}(\delta_{f})
, and for any
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
, let
g_{S}
be as in (
5.6
).
By (
5.8
) and (
5.1
),

\displaystyle\delta_{f}=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S^{\prime}|}I_{\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(g_{S})_{|\underline{1}}=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S^{\prime}|}I_{0}(\theta_{-\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(\delta_{1})\ast g_{S})_{|\underline{1}}.

So we take
u=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]}\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S^{\prime}|}I_{0}(\theta_{-\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(\delta_{1})\ast g_{S})
.
Let
h\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We will now compute
\sigma^{0}(u)\ast h
.
If
S^{\prime}\neq[\![1,\nu]\!]
, then

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{0}(\theta_{-\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(\delta_{1})\ast g_{S}))\ast h=\lambda^{-\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}\theta_{-\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(\delta_{1})\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(g_{S}))\ast h

is an invariant function which vanishes on
\mathcal{G}\times\{0\}
because
\kappa_{S^{\prime}}\neq 0
.
So, by (
1.37
) (the
L^{1}
-estimate),

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{0}(\theta_{-\kappa_{S^{\prime}}}(\delta_{1})\ast g_{S}))\ast h\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0).

Hence, the limit is also in
\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)
.
For the last term (the term with
S^{\prime}=S=[\![1,\nu]\!]
), we have by (
5.7
),

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}(I_{0}(g_{[\![1,\nu]\!]}))\ast h=\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g_{[\![1,\nu]\!]})\ast h\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}=\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\frac{\partial^{\nu}}{\partial\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\partial\lambda_{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\ast h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda.

We claim that for any
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
, there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\ast h\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\leq C\lambda_{i}^{-1},\quad\forall\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu},

(5.22)

for some constant
C>0
.
To see this, if
\lambda_{i}\leq 1
, then
\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\ast h\right\rVert
is bounded by
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}
, see (
2.23
).
If
\lambda_{i}\geq 1
by (
2.33
), we can suppose that
h=\theta_{l}(D)\ast h^{\prime}
where
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
,
h^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
,
\underline{1}\preceq l
.
By applying
5.1.5
with
S=\{i\}
, we can suppose that
D=D_{1}D_{2}
with
D_{1}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{S}}(M)
and
D_{2}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l_{S^{c}}}(M)
.
By (
2.23
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\ast h\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(g\ast\theta_{l_{S}}(D_{1}))\ast\theta_{l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast h^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\lambda_{i}^{-l_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert g\ast\theta_{l_{S}}(D_{1})\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\left\lVert\theta_{l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast h^{\prime}\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\lambda_{i}^{-l_{i}}.

Finally, since
1\preceq l
(and so
1\leq l_{i}
) and
1\leq\lambda_{i}
, (
5.22
) follows.
By (
5.22
),

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\frac{\partial^{\nu}}{\partial\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\partial\lambda_{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\ast h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda=(-1)^{\nu}\alpha_{0}(g)\ast h=(-1)^{\nu}\theta_{0}(\delta_{f})\ast h

The result follows.
∎

Proof of
4.3.2
.

By
5.3.5
and
5.3.1
, it follows that for any
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
, Limit (
4.17
) converges in the topology of
L^{2}(\pi)
and thus defines a continuous linear map
C^{\infty}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
.
To check that the image is in
C^{\infty}(\pi)
and that the convergence actually happens in the topology of
C^{\infty}(\pi)
, it suffices to use the identity
\sigma^{k+l}(\theta_{l}(D)\ast u)=\theta_{l}(D)\ast\sigma^{k}(u)
.
So,
Section

4.3.2
.
iii
follows.
By duality,
Section

4.3.2
.
i
follows.
By
5.3.3
, it follows that
\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x)
is independent of the choice of
f
such that
I_{k}(f)=v
.
This proves that the symbol is well-defined in the compactly supported case.

Section

4.3.2
.
iv
in the compactly supported case is straightforward to prove.

Section

4.3.2
.
iv
in the compactly supported case together with
5.3.6
, one deduces that the symbol is well-defined in the non-compactly supported case.
We now prove
Section

4.3.2
.
v
.
If
v\in\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)
and
k\prec l
and
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
, then

\displaystyle\sigma^{l}(v,\pi,x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}\to+\infty}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\lambda^{-l}\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{k}(f)))\xi

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\nu}\to+\infty}\lambda^{k-l}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\lambda^{-k}\alpha_{\lambda}(I_{k}(f)))\xi=0\times\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x)=0.

The rest of
4.3.2
is straightforward to prove.
∎

5.4
The
C^{*}
-algebras of operators of order
0

The goal of this section is to study the
C^{*}
-algebra of pseudo-differential operators. Ultimately, we want to show the following:

i.

Compactly supported operators (and their symbols) of order
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{\leq 0}
are bounded.

ii.

The
C^{*}
-algebra of operators of order
0
is of Type I, and its irreducible representations all come from either the principal symbol defined in
Section

4.3
or from the natural representation on
L^{2}(M)
.

iii.

The third hypothesis of
Theorem

B.0.8
is satisfied, see
5.4.9

Once we do these three steps, we can apply
Theorem

B.0.8
. All the results of
Section

4.4
,
Section

4.5
,
Section

4.6
will then follow easily.

Our first goal is to prove that operators of order
0
are bounded.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

For any
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
p\in]0,+\infty[
,

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\left\lVert f\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}^{p}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}<+\infty

Proof.

For any
S\subseteq[\![1,\nu]\!]
, let
\Lambda_{S}=\{\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}:\forall i\in S,\lambda_{i}<1,\ \forall i\in S^{c},\lambda_{i}\geq 1\}
.
We only need to show that the integral over each
\Lambda_{S}
is finite.
Let us fix
S
.
We write
f
as in (
2.35
), and
g
as in (
2.33
).
Since the sum of
L^{p}
-functions is again
L^{p}
(even for
p<1
), we can suppose that
f
and
g
are of the form
f^{\prime}\ast\theta_{k}(D)
and
\theta_{l}(D^{\prime})\ast g^{\prime}
with
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
D\in\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)
,
D^{\prime}\in\mathrm{DO}^{l}(M)
,
\underline{1}\preceq k
,
\underline{1}\preceq l
.
Hence, by (
2.32
) and (
2.31
),

\displaystyle f\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=\lambda^{k}f^{\prime}\ast\theta_{l+k}(DD^{\prime})\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(g^{\prime}).

We now apply
5.1.5
to
DD^{\prime}
.
So, we can suppose that
DD^{\prime}
is of the form
D_{1}D_{2}
with
D_{1}\in\mathrm{DO}^{k_{S}+l_{S}}(M)
,
D_{2}\in\mathrm{DO}^{k_{S^{c}}+l_{S^{c}}}(M)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle f\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(g)=\lambda^{k_{S}-l_{S^{c}}}f^{\prime}\ast\theta_{l_{S}+k_{S}}(D_{1})\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(\theta_{k_{S^{c}}+l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast g^{\prime}).

Hence, by (
2.23
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(g)\right\rVert^{p}_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\leq\left\lVert f^{\prime}\ast\theta_{l_{S}+k_{S}}(D_{1})\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}^{p}\left\lVert\theta_{k_{S^{c}}+l_{S^{c}}}(D_{2})\ast g^{\prime}\right\rVert^{p}_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}\lambda^{pk_{S}-pl_{S^{c}}}

Since
1\preceq k
and
1\preceq l
, it follows that
1\leq k_{i}
and
1\leq l_{i}
for all
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
.
If
i\in S
, then the
i
’th coordinate of
pk_{S}-pl_{S^{c}}
is equal to
pk_{i}>0
.
If
i\notin S
, then the
i
’th coordinate of
pk_{S}-pl_{S^{c}}
is equal to
-pl_{i}<0
.
So, the integral
\int_{\Lambda_{S}}\lambda^{pk_{S}-p{l}_{S^{c}}-\underline{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda
converges.
The result follows.
∎

Proposition 5.4.2
.

i.

Any
u\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})
extends to a bounded multiplier of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
.
In fact if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert I_{0}(f)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G})}\leq\sqrt{\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\left\lVert f^{*}\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}}\sqrt{\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\left\lVert f\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)^{*}\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}}

(5.23)

ii.

For any
u\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})
,
\sigma^{0}(u)
defined in
5.3.1
extends to a bounded multiplier of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sigma^{0}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G})}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G})}.

(5.24)

iii.

For any
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{\leq 0}
,
v\in\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)
,
v\ast L^{2}(M)\subseteq L^{2}(M)
and
v:L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is bounded.
Furthermore, if
\Re(k_{i})<0
for some
i
, then
v\ast L^{2}(M)\subseteq L^{2}(M)
and
v:L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is compact.

iv.

For any
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{\leq 0}
,
v\in\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)
and
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
,
\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x)\ast L^{2}(\pi)\subseteq L^{2}(\pi)
and
\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is bounded.
Furthermore, if
\Re(k_{i})<0
for all
i
, then
\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is compact.

Proof.

Let us prove
Section

5.4.2
.
i
.
Let
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
u=I_{0}(f)
.
We need to show that
u
extends to a bounded multiplier of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
.
It is already well-defined on the dense set of elements
C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. So, let
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We need to bound
\left\lVert u\ast g\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}
.
By (
5.19
), we only need to bound
\pi_{t}(u\ast g)
uniformly in
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
Formally, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{t}(u\ast g)=\left(\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\pi_{t}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}\right)\ast\pi_{t}(g).

Therefore, it suffices to show that the integrals
\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\pi_{t}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}
define bounded operators on
L^{2}(M)
whose operator norm is uniformly bounded in
t
.
By the Cotlar Stein-lemma, see
[
31
, Lemma 18.6.5]
,
it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\sup_{\mu\in]0,1]^{\nu}}\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\left\lVert\pi_{t}(\alpha_{\mu}(f)^{*}\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\right\rVert^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}<+\infty,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\sup_{\mu\in]0,1]^{\nu}}\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\left\lVert\pi_{t}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\ast\alpha_{\mu}(f)^{*})\right\rVert^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}<+\infty.

We will only consider the first. The second is dealt with similarly.
By (
2.23
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi_{t}(\alpha_{\mu}(f)^{*}\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))}\leq\left\lVert\alpha_{\mu}(f)^{*}\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}=\left\lVert f^{*}\ast\alpha_{\lambda\mu^{-1}}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\left\lVert f^{*}\ast\alpha_{\lambda}(f)\right\rVert_{C^{*}\mathbb{G}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}<+\infty.

This follows from
5.4.1
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

5.4.2
.
i
.

We now prove
Section

5.4.2
.
ii
.
By (
2.35
), if
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\alpha_{\lambda}(u)\ast g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},1}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Hence,
\sigma^{0}(u)\ast g
is in
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
.
By (
2.23
), one deduces that
\left\lVert\alpha_{\lambda}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G})}=\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{M}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G})}
.
Hence,
\sigma^{0}(u)
extends to a bounded multiplier of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G})
whose adjoint is
\sigma^{0}(u^{*})
, and (
5.24
) holds.

We now prove
Section

5.4.2
.
iii
.
By replacing
v
by
v^{*}\ast v
, we can suppose that
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
with
k_{i}\leq 0
for all
i
.
Any non-degenerate representation of a
C^{*}
-algebra induces a representation of its multiplier algebra, see
[
34
, Chapter 4]
.
The case
k=0
follows from
Section

5.4.2
.
i
using the representation
\pi_{\underline{1}}
of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathbb{G})
.
So, we can suppose that
k_{i}<0
for some
i
.
By
Section

4.1.3
.
vii
, for
n
big enough,
(v^{*}\ast v)^{n}
maps continuously
L^{2}(M)
to
H^{2nk}(M)\subseteq C^{1}(M)
. Since
v
is compactly supported, the image lies in
C^{1}_{c}(K)
for some
K\subseteq M
compact.
The inclusion
C^{1}(K)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(M)
is compact (Rellich’s theorem).
Hence,
(v^{*}\ast v)^{n}\ast\cdot:L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is bounded and compact.
It follows that
v\ast\cdot:L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is bounded and compact.

Let us prove
Section

5.4.2
.
iv
.
Again by replacing
v
with
v^{*}\ast v
, we can suppose that
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
with
k_{i}\leq 0
for all
i
.
Formally, we have

\displaystyle\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}}

We now to use Cotlar-Stein lemma on the coordinates where
k_{i}=0
and Riemann-Lebesgue lemma
Theorem

A.0.8
on those where
k_{i}<0
.
More precisely, first recall that since the
C^{*}
-algebra of nilpotent Lie groups is liminal, see
[
22
, 13.11.12]
,
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))
is a compact operator.
We divide the integral into
\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\backslash]0,1]^{\nu}}+\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}
. By
Theorem

A.0.8
, the first integral converges in norm. So, it defines a compact bounded operator.
For the second integral, let
S=\{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]:k_{i}=0\}
.
If
S=\emptyset
, then the second integral also converges in norm (because
k_{i}<0
for all
i
), so it also defines a compact operator.
If
S\neq\emptyset
, we proceed as follows:
We divide the integral into

\displaystyle\int_{]0,1]^{\nu}}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}}=\int_{]0,1]^{S}}\int_{]0,1]^{S^{c}}}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}}.

The argument we used above with Cotlar Stein works equally well to give boundedness of the integral
\int_{]0,1]^{S^{c}}}
. Furthermore, the bound of the operator norm of
\int_{]0,1]^{S^{c}}}
obtained from the Cotlar Stein lemma is uniform for in
\lambda\in]0,1]^{S}
. From this, we get that the integral converges in the strong operator topology to a bounded operator.
∎

We now pass to the second goal which is analyzing the
C^{*}
-algebra generated by compactly supported operators of order
0
.

Proposition 5.4.3
.

The map

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))\to\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)},\quad\sigma^{0}(u)\mapsto\pi_{\underline{1}}(\sigma^{0}(u)),\quad\forall u\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}),

(5.25)

is well-defined, and extends to a
*
-isomorphism between the closures
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G}))
and
\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))
.
In particular, the spectrums of
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
and
\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}
are homeomorphic.

Proof.

Let
u\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})
. We claim that

\displaystyle\pi_{\underline{1}}(\sigma^{0}(u))-\pi_{\underline{1}}(u)\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M)).

(5.26)

To see this, if
C
is as in
5.3.2
, then for any
t\in]C,+\infty[^{\nu}
,
\pi_{t}(\sigma^{0}(u))=\pi_{t}(u)
.
By equivariance of
\sigma^{0}(u)
,
\pi_{t}(\sigma^{0}(u))=\pi_{\underline{1}}(\sigma^{0}(u))

By
5.3.2
and (
5.20
),
u_{|t}-u_{|\underline{1}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Our claim follows.

By (
4.3
),
\pi_{\underline{1}}(u)\in\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)
.
By
Section

4.1.3
.
i
,
\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\subseteq\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}
.
So,
\pi_{\underline{1}}(\sigma^{0}(u))\in\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}
, and the map (
5.25
) is well-defined.
It is an isometry by (
5.19
) and equivariance of
\sigma^{0}(u)
.
It is clearly surjective.
∎

We will now analyze
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}\simeq\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}
.
By
5.2.1
and
Section

5.4.2
.
ii
, we have a map

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}\to\widehat{\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}}.

(5.27)

Since
\alpha_{\lambda}(\sigma^{0}(u))=\sigma^{0}(u)
for any
u\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, (
5.27
) descends to a map

\displaystyle\{\underline{1}\}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}=\big((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}\big)/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\to\widehat{\sigma^{0}(\overline{\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})})}.

(5.28)

Theorem 5.4.4
.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
is of Type I. Furthermore, (
5.28
) is a homeomorphism where the left-hand-side is equipped with the quotient topology.
In particular
\{\underline{1}\}
is an open dense subset of
\widehat{\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G})})}
.

Proof.

To prove this theorem, we will show that
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
is Morita equivalence to the cross-product
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\rtimes_{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
. A general reference for Morita equivalence and cross products are the books by Raeburn and Williams
[
41
, Chapter 3]
and Williams
[
56
, Chapter 2]
.
We need the following results:

i.

If
A
and
B
are Morita equivalent
C^{*}
-algebras, then the spectrum of
A
and the spectrum of
B
are homeomorphic, see
[
41
, Corollary 3.33]
.
In addition, if
A
and
B
are separable, then by
[
22
, Chapter 9]
,
A
is of Type I if and only if
B
is of Type I.

ii.

We will also need a result on the cross product of
C^{*}
-algebras due to Rieffel
[
44
]
.
The result of Rieffel has been improved by Buss and Echterhoff
[
12
]
.
We will state the version by Buss and Echterhoff.
Since, we only need their theorem for the group
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, we will state the theorem only for unimodular groups

Theorem 5.4.5
.

Let
G
be a unimodular locally compact second countable group that acts on a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
,
d\mu
a Haar measure on
G
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq A
is a dense linear subspace such that for any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
,
\int_{G}\left\lVert b^{*}\alpha_{t}(a)\right\rVert\,d\mu(t)<+\infty
.
Then, the following holds:

(a)

If
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\int_{G}\alpha_{t}(ab^{*})\,d\mu(t)
converges and defines an element of
\mathcal{L}(A)
.
1
1

1

See
[
12
, Deinition 2,2]
for the precise meaning of the convergence of the integral.

(b)

The
C^{*}
-algebra generated by
\{\int_{G}\alpha_{t}(ab^{*})\,d\mu(t):a,b\in\mathcal{A}\}
inside
\mathcal{L}(A)
is Morita equivalent to a two-sided ideal of the reduced crossed product
A\rtimes_{r}G
.

By
5.4.1
, we can apply
Theorem

5.4.5
with
A=C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}
,
\mathcal{A}=C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
G=(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
If
f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}}\alpha_{\lambda}(f\ast g^{*})\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda^{\underline{1}}}=\sigma^{0}(I_{0}(f\ast g)).

Lemma 5.4.6
.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\rtimes_{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
is separable of Type I. Furthermore, its spectrum is equal to
\{\underline{1}\}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.

Lemma 5.4.7
.

One has

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{span}(\{\sigma^{0}(I_{0}(f\ast g)):f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\})}=\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}.

5.4.7
and
Theorem

5.4.5
imply that
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
is Morita equivalent to a two-sided ideal of the crossed product
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\rtimes_{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
So, by
B.0.3
and
5.4.6
,
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
is separable of Type I, and its spectrum is an open subset of the spectrum
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\rtimes_{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
Since the spectrum of
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
already contains
\{\underline{1}\}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, it follows that
\overline{\sigma^{0}(\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{G}))}
is Morita equivalent to
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\rtimes_{r}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
. This finishes the proof of
Theorem

5.4.4
once we prove
5.4.6
and
5.4.7
.
∎

Proof of
5.4.6
.

Let
(V,\natural)
be a
\nu
-graded basis like in
3.7.1
.
Recall from the proof of
4.2.8
that we have a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}\backslash 0)\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}\to 0,

and that
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
is a quotient of the
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M)
.
The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}
is a two-sided ideal of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathbb{G}
whose spectrum by
5.2.1
is equal to
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\sqcup\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}\times\{0\}
.
It follows that
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}
lies in a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes C_{0}((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu})\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathcal{G}\to 0,

where
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathcal{G}
is the two-sided ideal of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv}}\mathcal{G}
whose spectrum is
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
.
Taking the crossed product by
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
, we have

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\otimes\Big(C_{0}((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu})\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\Big)\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathbb{G}\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathcal{G}\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}\to 0.

By the definition of crossed product,
C_{0}((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu})\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}=\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu})
.
So, it remains to show that
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathcal{G}\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
is of Type I and that its spectrum is
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
The
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathcal{G}
is the quotient of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M)
whose spectrum is
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
, see the paragraph preceding
Theorem

A.0.6
for the definition of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}(V)
.
So,
C^{*}_{\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}\mathcal{G}\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
is also a quotient of
(C^{*}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}(V)\otimes C_{0}(M))\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
The result follows from
Theorem

A.0.6
and
B.0.3
.
∎

Proof of
5.4.7
.

By (
5.24
), it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\overline{\{I_{0}(f\ast g):f,g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\}}=\overline{\{I_{0}(f):f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\}},

By writing

Following the proof of

.

(5.29)

.

(5.30)

) and the proof of

.

to the maps in

.

).
By writing the integral in (

∎

An immediate corollary of

and

is the following:

Corollary 5.4.8
.

Let
C^{*}\mathrm{H\!N}
be the quotient of
\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}
whose spectrum is
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
. Then, one has a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))\to\overline{\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)}\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}}C^{*}\mathrm{H\!N}\to 0

(5.31)

In particular, for any
v\in\Psi^{0}_{c}(M)
, if
\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
vanishes for all
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
, then
v\ast\cdot:L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is a compact operator.

We finally prove that there exists operators as in the third hypothesis of
Theorem

B.0.8
. Let
K
be as in
Theorem

B.0.8
.

Proposition 5.4.9
.

Suppose
M
is compact. There exists a family
v_{k}\in\Psi^{k}(M)

for
k\in K
such that

i.

The operator
v_{0}
is invertible in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
,

ii.

For any
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
, the map

\displaystyle k\in K\mapsto v_{k}\ast v\ast v_{-k}\in\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}

(5.32)

is continuous.

Proof.

We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4.10
.

There exists
g\in C^{\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
which is positive (as an operator
L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
) such that the operator
g\ast\cdot:C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is injective.

Proof.

Let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a dense sequence in
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. We take

\displaystyle g(y,x)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}a_{n}f_{n}(y)\overline{f_{n}(x)},

where
a_{n}
are strictly positive constants which converge to
0
sufficiently fast so that the series converges in
C^{\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
∎

There exists
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
which is positive as an element of
C^{*}\mathbb{G}
such that
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(f):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is injective for any
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
.
The proof of this is a straightforward adaptation of the proof of
[
38
, Lemma 2.17.2]
, and is left to the reader.

We define
v_{k}
by

\displaystyle v_{k}=I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}+g

Let
k=0
and
h\in L^{2}(M)
,
\xi\in L^{2}(\pi)
. The inner products
\langle v_{0}\ast h,h\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}
and
\langle\sigma^{0}(v_{0},\pi,x)\xi,\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\pi)}
are well-defined.
So we get injectivity of

from injectivity of
g
on
L^{2}(M)
, and injectivity of

on

from injectivity of
\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(f)
.
We deduce that
v_{0}
is left-invertible
B.0.1
.
Since
v_{0}^{*}=v_{0}
it is invertible in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
.

Continuity of (
5.32
) follows from (
5.23
), and the proof of
5.4.1
, and
Section

5.1.1
.
i
.
∎

By all the above, we can apply

.

Proof of

.

4.4.2
.
i
,
Section

4.4.2
.

,
Section

4.4.2
.

and

.

.

.

.

We now no longer suppose that

The Sobolev spaces are local.
More precisely, if

, then

).

Lemma 5.5.1
.

For every
x\in M
, there exists a weakly commutative weighted sub-Riemannian structure
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
of depth
N
on the
\dim(M)
-dimensional sphere
S^{\dim(M)}
, and
there exists
U\subseteq M
an open neighborhood of
x
,

U^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\dim(M)}
an open subset, and a diffeomorphism
\phi:U\to U^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{F}^{k}_{|U}=\phi^{*}(\mathcal{G}_{|U^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

Let
\phi:U^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
be any diffeomorphism from an open neighborhood
U^{\prime}
of
x
to an open subset
V^{\prime}
of
S^{\dim(M)}
.
Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(V^{\prime})
be a smooth function which is equal to
1
in a neighborhood
V
of
\phi(x)
.
We take
U=\phi^{-1}(V)
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, if
k_{i}<N_{i}
for all
i
, then we define
\mathcal{G}^{k}
to be the submodule of
\mathcal{X}(S^{\dim(M)})
generated by
\chi\phi_{*}(X)
for
X\in\mathcal{F}^{k}
.
Otherwise, we take
\mathcal{G}^{N}=\mathcal{X}(S^{\dim(M)})
.
It is clear that the family
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
consists of finitely generated modules.
Furthermore, by the identity,

\displaystyle=\chi^{2}\phi_{*}([X;Y])+\phi_{*}(X)(\chi)\chi\phi_{*}(Y)-\phi_{*}(Y)(\chi)\chi\phi_{*}(X)

(5.34)

it follows that
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
satisfies (
2.5
) for
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
i+j<N
.
Since, (
2.5
) for
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
i+j=N
is trivially satisfied, we deduce that
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
is a
\nu
-graded sub-Riemannian structure on
S^{\dim(M)}
.
Finally, since
\chi=1
on
V
,
\phi^{*}(\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}_{|V})=\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{|U}
.
∎

Using
5.5.1
, to prove
4.4.2
, we can suppose that
M
is an open subset of
S^{\dim(M)}
.
So, the theorem follows from the compact case.
This finishes the proof of
4.4.2
.

∎

Proof of
4.4.4
.

Since the statement is local at
x
, by
5.5.1
, we can suppose that
M
is compact.
The argument is identical to
4.4.2
.

Proof of

By

5.4.11
.
iii
, we can suppose that

.
The result now follows from

, and density of
C^{\infty}(\pi)
in

for the

-topology.
