Proof of
4.4.6
.

Since the statement is local at
x
, by
5.5.1
, we can suppose that
M
is compact.
By
Theorem

5.4.11
.
iii
, we can suppose that
k=l=0
.

The set
S/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
seen as a subset of
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
is equal to the set
\overline{\{(\pi,x)\}}\backslash\{(\pi,x)\}
where the closure of
\{(\pi,x)\}
is taken in
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
The set
\overline{\{(\pi,x)\}}
is a closed subset of
\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.
By the computation of the spectrum of
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
in
Theorem

5.4.4
and
5.4.3
, we can define a quotient
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}
whose spectrum is
\overline{\{(\pi,x)\}}
.
Let us denote this quotient by
A
which is of Type I by
B.0.3
.
By
[
22
, Proposition 3.3.2]
,
A
is the Hausdorff completion of
\Psi^{0}(M)
by the semi-norm
v\mapsto\left\lVert\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))}
.
Now, let
B
be the Hausdorff completion
\Psi^{0}(M)
by the semi-norm
v\mapsto\left\lVert\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))}
.
Clearly,
B
is a quotient of
A
.
In fact, since in the spectrum of
A
, the point
(\pi,x)
is dense,
B.0.4
(rather its proof) implies that the spectrum of
B
is precisely the set
S
.

By the computation of the spectrum of
B
, we deduce that if
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
, then
v
vanishes in
B
, i.e.,
L^{2}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)}L^{2}(\pi)
is compact if and only if for all
(\pi^{\prime},x)\in S
,
L^{2}(\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}(v,\pi^{\prime},x)}L^{2}(\pi^{\prime})
vanishes, which by density of
C^{\infty}(\pi)
is equivalent to vanishing of

C^{\infty}(\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}(v,\pi^{\prime},x)}C^{\infty}(\pi^{\prime})
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

4.4.6
.
i
.

To obtain
Theorem

5.4.11
, we used
Theorem

B.0.8
, where we completed
\Psi^{0}(M)
by the norm
v\mapsto\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))}
.
Here, instead, we will apply
Theorem

B.0.8
but completing
\Psi^{0}(M)
by the semi-norm
v\mapsto\left\lVert\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))}
.
By
Theorem

B.0.8
.
iii
and the computation of the spectrum of
B
above, we obtain the following:
If
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
, then
v
is left-invertible in
B
if and only if
C^{\infty}(\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}(v,\pi^{\prime},x)}C^{\infty}(\pi^{\prime})
is injective for all
(\pi^{\prime},x)\in S
.
From this, we obtain the equivalence between
Section

4.4.6
.
iia
and
Section

4.4.6
.
iic
.
The equivalence between
Section

4.4.6
.
iia
and
Section

4.4.6
.
iib
is the statement of
Theorem

B.0.8
.
i
.

Finally, we need to show that if
H^{\Re(k)+l}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x)}H^{l}(\pi)
is Fredholm, then its kernel consists of only smooth vectors (and so it doesn’t depend on
l
).
This will again be an application of
Theorem

B.0.8
. This time, we complete
\Psi^{0}(M)
by the semi-norm
v\mapsto\left\lVert\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))}
.
By the computation of the spectrum of
A
above,

Theorem

B.0.8
.
iii
implies that
v
is left-invertible in
A
if and only if
C^{\infty}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}(v,\pi^{\prime},x)}C^{\infty}(\pi)
is injective for all
(\pi^{\prime},x)\in S
, and
C^{\infty}(\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)}C^{\infty}(\pi^{\prime})
is injective.
In other words, if
H^{\Re(k)+l}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\sigma^{k}(v,\pi,x)}H^{l}(\pi)
is Fredholm, then it is left-invertible if and only if it is injective on
C^{\infty}(\pi)
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

4.4.6
.
ii
.

∎

Proof of

and

.

For technical reasons, we will first prove the results in
Section

4.6
and then prove the results in
Section

4.5
.
Let
\tilde{\mathbb{G}}\rightrightarrows\tilde{\mathbb{G}}^{(0)}
and
\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}}\rightrightarrows\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}}^{(0)}
be the tangent groupoids associated to
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\accentset{\approx}{\mathcal{F}}
,
\Phi
the map as in
2.8.4
.
Let
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\tilde{\nu}}
and
l\in\mathbb{C}^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}
.
If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},k+\underline{1}}(\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
g\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},l+\underline{1}}(\accentset{\approx}{\mathbb{G}},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, then
\Phi(f,g)\in C^{\infty}_{c,\mathrm{inv},(k,l)+\underline{1}}(\mathbb{G},\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, and we have

\displaystyle I_{(k,l)}(\Phi(f,g))=\int_{]0,1]^{\tilde{\nu}}\times]0,1]^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}}\alpha_{(\lambda,\mu)}(\Phi(f,g))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}\mu^{l+\underline{1}}}=\int_{]0,1]^{\tilde{\nu}}\times]0,1]^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}}\Phi(\alpha_{\lambda}(f),\alpha_{\mu}(g))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}\mu^{l+\underline{1}}}

So,

\displaystyle I_{(k,l)}(\Phi(f,g))_{|\underline{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{]0,1]^{\tilde{\nu}}\times]0,1]^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}}\Phi(\alpha_{\lambda}(f),\alpha_{\mu}(g))_{|\underline{1}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}\mu^{l+\underline{1}}}

(5.35)

\displaystyle=\int_{]0,1]^{\tilde{\nu}}\times]0,1]^{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}}\alpha_{\lambda}(f)_{|\underline{1}}\ast\alpha_{\mu}(g)_{|\underline{1}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu}{\lambda^{k+\underline{1}}\mu^{l+\underline{1}}}=I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}\ast I_{l}(g)_{|\underline{1}}.

We have thus proved that
\tilde{\Psi}^{k}_{c}(M)\ast\accentset{\approx}{\Psi}^{l}_{c}(M)\subseteq\Psi^{(k,l)}_{c}(M)
.

We now prove (
4.23
).
In fact this immediately from (
2.45
), see the proof of
Section

4.1.3
.
iii
.
The proof of
Section

4.6.1
.
i
is thus complete.

The proof of
Section

4.6.1
.
iii
and
Section

4.6.1
.

is straightforward and left to the reader.

We now prove
4.6.2
.
Let
k,l,f,g
as in (
5.35
)
By (
5.35
), We have

\displaystyle\sigma^{(k,l)}(I_{k}(f)_{|\underline{1}}\ast I_{l}(g)_{|\underline{1}},\pi,x)=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\tilde{\nu}}\to+\infty,\mu_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\mu_{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}\to+\infty}\lambda^{-k}\mu^{-l}\pi_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{(\lambda,\mu)}(\Phi(f,g)))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lambda_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\lambda_{\tilde{\nu}}\to+\infty,\mu_{1}\to+\infty,\cdots,\mu_{\accentset{\approx}{\nu}}\to+\infty}\lambda^{-k}\mu^{-l}\tilde{\pi}_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\accentset{\approx}{\pi}_{\mathrm{inv}}(\alpha_{\mu}(g))

\displaystyle=\sigma^{k}(I_{k}(f),\tilde{\pi},x)\otimes\sigma^{l}(I_{l}(f),\accentset{\approx}{\pi},x).

From this, (

By

5.7
Proofs of the results in Section
4.5

We will first need to modify the sub-Riemannian structure.
We will replace
\mathcal{F}
by
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
defined in (
5.11
).

4.6.1
ensures that the calculus associated to
\mathcal{F}
is contained in the calculus associated to
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
, and that the Sobolev spaces don’t change (for the appropriate indices).

4.6.2
together with (
5.12
) ensure that we don’t change the principal symbol.
So, it suffices to prove the results of this section for the calculus
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
.
To summarize, we will suppose that
\nu\geq 2
, and that
N_{(0,\cdots,0,1)}=\mathcal{X}(M)
.
This implies the following:

i.

For any
x\in M
, the union (
4.8
) is a disjoint union once one writes the union as a union of the co-sphere bundle. So, there is a projection map

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}\to(T^{*}M\backslash 0)/\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times},

(5.36)

which is continuous.

ii.

If
\chi\in\Psi^{k}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
with
k\in\mathbb{C}
, then, by
4.1.5
,
\chi\in\Psi^{(0,\cdots,0,k)}(M)
and

\displaystyle\sigma^{(0,\cdots,0,k)}(\chi,\pi,x)=\sigma^{k}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi,\xi,x),\quad\forall(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0,\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)}.

(5.37)

In particular, by
5.7.1
and
Section

4.4.2
.
vi
,
\chi\ast H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)\subseteq H^{l-(0,\cdots,0,k)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
for all
l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
.
This will be used repeatedly in this section.

We also recall the standard terminology, we say that
\chi\in\Psi^{k}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
is smoothing on a cone
\Gamma\subseteq T^{*}M\backslash 0
, if
\Gamma\subseteq\{(\xi,x):(\xi,-\xi;x,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}(\chi)\}
.
This implies that
\mathrm{WF}(\chi\ast w)\cap\Gamma=\emptyset
for all
w\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

It is straightforward to show that given any closed cones
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{1}\cap\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
, there exists
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi
is smoothing on
\Gamma_{1}
, and
\chi
is elliptic on
\Gamma_{2}
.

We now generalise
Section

4.1.3
.
viii
and (
4.23
).

Lemma 5.7.1
.

If
\chi\in\Psi^{k}_{c,\mathrm{classical}}(M)
and
v\in\Psi^{l}_{c}(M)
where
k\in\mathbb{C}
and
l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, then
[\chi,v]\in\Psi^{\prec l+(0,\cdots,0,k)}_{c}(M)
.

Proof.

The proof of this is very similar to the proof of (
4.23
), and will be left to the reader.
∎

Proof of
4.5.2
.

Section

4.5.2
.
i
follows immediately from
Section

4.4.2
.
i

Recall the following classical characterization of the wave-front set.

Theorem 5.7.2

(
[
31
, Theorem 18.1.27]
)
.

Let
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. For any
(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0
,
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}(w)
if and only if there exists
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi
is elliptic at
(\xi,x)
and
\chi\ast w\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

The implication
\implies
in
Section

4.5.2
.
v
follows immediately from
Theorem

5.7.2
and the fact that classical pseudo-differential operators preserve the Sobolev spaces
H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
for all
l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
.
For, the implication
\impliedby
in
Section

4.5.2
.
v
, we can find
\chi^{\prime}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi^{\prime}\ast\chi-\delta_{1}
is smoothing in a conic neighborhood
(\xi,x)
.
So,

\displaystyle w=\chi^{\prime}\ast\chi\ast w+(\delta_{1}-\chi^{\prime}\ast\chi)\ast w

One has
\chi^{\prime}\ast\chi\ast w\in H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
, and
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}((\delta_{1}-\chi^{\prime}\ast\chi)\ast w)
.

Let us prove
Section

4.5.2
.
vi
.
If
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi,\xi,x)\neq 0
, then
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi,\eta,y)\neq 0
on an open neighborhood of
(\xi,x)
.
So, since the complement of the space
\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)
is Lindelöf, there exists a sequence
\chi_{n}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that for any
n
,
\chi_{n}\ast w\in H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
, and for any
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)
, there exists
n
such that
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi_{n},\xi,x)\neq 0
.
Since classical pseudo-differential operators preserve Sobolev spaces,
\chi^{*}_{n}\ast\chi_{n}\ast w\in H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
.
Let
c_{n}>0
be a sequence that converges fast enough to
0
that the sum
\chi=\sum_{n}c_{n}\chi_{n}^{*}\ast\chi_{n}
converges in the space
\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
, and that
\sum_{n}c_{n}\chi_{n}^{*}\ast\chi_{n}\ast w
converges in the Fréchet space
H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
.
(Here, one might have to modify the supports of
\chi_{n}
by adding a smoothing operator so that
\chi_{n}
are uniformly properly supported, which implies that
\chi
is properly supported).
One has
\chi\ast w\in H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
and
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi,\xi,x)\neq 0
for all
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)
.

Section

4.5.2
.
ii
follows immediately from
Section

4.5.2
.
vi
.

Section

4.5.2
.
iv
follows immediately from (
4.5
) and
Section

4.4.2
.
vi
.

Let us prove that
\mathrm{WF}(w)=\overline{\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)}
. The inclusion
\supseteq
is obvious from
Theorem

5.7.2
and
Section

4.5.2
.
v
.
For inclusion
\subseteq
, let
(\xi,x)\notin\overline{\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)}
.
We can find a
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi
is elliptic at
(\xi,x)
, and
\chi
is smoothing on a conic open neighborhood of
\overline{\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)}
.

By
Section

4.5.2
.
vi
,
for each
l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
, we can find
\chi_{l}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that for any
l
,
\chi_{l}\ast w\in H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
, and
\chi_{l}
is elliptic on the complement of
\overline{\bigcup_{l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)}
.
We can divide
\chi
by
\chi_{l}
, i.e., there exists
\chi_{l}^{\prime}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi=\chi_{l}^{\prime}\ast\chi_{l}
modulo smoothing operators.
Hence,
\chi\ast w\in\bigcap_{l}H^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)=C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
. So, by
Theorem

5.7.2
,
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}(w)
.

The identity
\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w+w^{\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)\cup\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w^{\prime})
is straightforward to prove.

∎

Proof of
4.5.3
.

Let us prove
Section

4.5.3
.
i
. Since this is local in
x
, we can suppose that

is compact by

.
By

5.4.11
.

, we can suppose that

We fix

, and
\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi,\eta,y)\neq 0
for all

.
Let

as in

,
\tilde{v}=v^{*}\ast v+\chi^{*}\ast\chi+g\in\Psi^{0}(M)
. By (
5.37
),

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}(\tilde{v},\pi,y)=

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,y)^{*}\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,y)+|\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi,\eta,y)|^{2},\quad\forall\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\eta,y)}.

So,
\tilde{v}
satisfies the hypothesis of

5.4.11
.

.
By

5.4.11
.

,

.

We now show that
\Gamma
is open.
Since this is local, we can suppose
M
is compact.
By

5.4.11
.
iii
, we can suppose that
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
.
By
Section

4.5.3
.
i
,

\displaystyle\Gamma=\{(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0:\forall\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)},\ \sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)\text{ is injective}\}

Consider the
C^{*}
-algebra
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))
.
There is an obvious map
C((T^{*}M\backslash 0)/\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})\to\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))
which maps a smooth function on the cotangent sphere bundle to the corresponding classical pseudo-differential operator modulo compact operators.

5.7.1
implies that the image of

lies in the center of the

-algebra
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))
.
So, the

is fibered over
(T^{*}M\backslash 0)/\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
.
This fibration induces a fibration on the spectrum which is precisely the fibration given by (
5.36
).
In a fibered
C^{*}
-algebra, the set of points on which the fiber is left-invertible is open, see
[
7
, Proposition 2.4]
.
By
B.0.1
, it follows that
\Gamma
is open.

We now prove
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
.
We will first suppose that
M
is compact.
By
Theorem

5.4.11
.
iii
, we can suppose that
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
.
We fix
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that

is elliptic on the complement of
\Gamma
, and

is smoothing on

.
Let

.
By replacing

by

, we can suppose that

is formally self-adjoint, and that

,
\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)
is injective.
By
Theorem

5.4.11
.
ii
,
v\ast\cdot:C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
is a topological isomorphism.
Let
v^{\prime}
be its inverse. Since
v^{*}=v
,
v^{\prime*}=v^{\prime}
. By
Theorem

5.4.11
.
ii
,
v^{\prime}
maps
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
to
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
, and
H^{l}(M)
to
H^{l}(M)
.
So, by Schwartz kernel theorem
v^{\prime}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
To finish the proof of
Section

4.5.3
.

in the compact case, we need to show that

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}_{l}(v^{\prime}\ast w)=\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w),\quad\forall w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),\ l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}.

Since
v^{\prime}
is the inverse of
v
, equivalently, we need to show that
\mathrm{WF}_{l}(v\ast w)=\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)
.
By
Section

4.5.2
.
iv
, we need to show that
\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{l}(v\ast w)
.
Since
v\ast\cdot
is a topological isomorphism on Sobolev spaces, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{WF}(w)\subseteq\mathrm{WF}(v\ast w)
.
Let
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}(v\ast w)
be fixed.

Lemma 5.7.3
.

There exists a sequence
(\chi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
and
\chi_{\infty}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

i.

\chi_{n}\ast v\ast w\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

ii.

If
n,m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
m<n
, then
\chi_{n}\ast\chi_{m}-\chi_{n}
is a smoothing operator, i.e., they map
C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
to
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

iii.

\sigma^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(\chi_{\infty},\xi,x)\neq 0
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.7.2
, there exists
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi
is elliptic at
(\xi,x)
and
\chi\ast v\ast w\in C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Let
U\subseteq M
be an open chart around
x
.
We take
K_{n}\subseteq T^{*}M\backslash 0
a sequence of closed conic neighborhoods of
(\xi,x)
such that
K_{n}\subseteq\{(\eta,y)\in T^{*}U\backslash 0:\sigma^{0}(\chi,\eta,y)\neq 0\}
,
K_{n+1}\subseteq\mathrm{Int}(K_{n})
, and
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}
is a conic neighbourhood of
(\xi,x)
.
Let
\kappa_{n}\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}M\backslash 0)
homogeneous of degree
0
such that
\kappa_{n}=1
on
K_{n+1}
,
\supp(\kappa_{n})\subseteq\mathrm{int}(K_{n})
.
Let
\kappa_{\infty}\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}M\backslash 0)
such that
\kappa_{\infty}(\xi,x)=1
and
\supp(\kappa_{\infty})\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}
.
So,
\kappa_{n}\kappa_{m}=\kappa_{n}
if
m<n
for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Let
\chi_{n},\chi_{\infty}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
be classical pseudo-differential operators whose total symbols are
\kappa_{n}
and
\kappa_{\infty}
.
The first assertion follows from the fact that since
K_{n}\subseteq\{(\eta,y)\in T^{*}U\backslash 0:\sigma^{0}(\chi,\eta,y)\neq 0\}
, there exists
\chi^{\prime}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi_{n}=\chi^{\prime}\ast\chi
modulo smoothing operators.
The second assertion now follows from
[
31
, Theorem 18.1.8]
.
The third assertion is obvious.
∎

We define the Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle H^{t}_{n}(M)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu},l_{1}+\cdots+l_{\nu}\geq n}H^{t+l}(M),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},t\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}.

Equivalently,
H^{t}_{n}(M)
is equal to the sum of
H^{t^{\prime}}(M)
such that there exists a sequence
t=t_{0}\prec t_{1}\prec\cdots\prec t_{n}=t^{\prime}
.
By
Section

4.4.2
.
v
,
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}H^{t}_{n}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)
.
Also, operators in
\Psi^{0}(M)
(and thus also classical pseudo-differential operators of order
0
) preserve
H^{t}_{n}(M)
.

By
Section

4.4.2
.
i
, there exists some
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
such that
w\in H^{t}(M)
. We fix such
t
.

Lemma 5.7.4
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\chi_{n}\ast w\in H^{t}_{n}(M)
.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction.
We have

\displaystyle v\ast\chi_{1}\ast w

\displaystyle=[v,\chi_{1}]\ast w+\chi_{1}\ast v\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=[v,\chi_{1}]\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

So, by
5.7.1
and
Section

4.4.2
.
vi
,
v\ast\chi_{1}\ast w\in H^{t+k}_{1}(M)
.
Hence, by
Theorem

5.4.11
.
ii
,
\chi_{1}\ast w\in H^{t}_{1}(M)
. Since
\chi_{n}\ast\chi_{1}-\chi_{n}
is smoothing,
\chi_{n}\ast w\in H^{t}_{1}(M)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Now,

\displaystyle v\ast\chi_{2}\ast w

\displaystyle=v\ast\chi_{2}\ast\chi_{1}\ast w

\displaystyle=[v,\chi_{2}]\ast\chi_{1}\ast w+\chi_{2}\ast v\ast\chi_{1}\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=[v,\chi_{2}]\ast\chi_{1}\ast w+\chi_{2}\ast[v,\chi_{1}]\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=[v,\chi_{2}]\ast\chi_{1}\ast w+[\chi_{2},[v,\chi_{1}]]\ast w+[v,\chi_{1}]\ast\chi_{2}\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

The three terms belong to
H^{t}_{2}(M)
by
5.7.1
and
\chi_{2}\ast w\in H^{t}_{1}(M)
, and
\chi_{1}\ast w\in H^{t}_{1}(M)
.
Hence,
v\ast\chi_{2}\ast w\in H^{t}_{2}(M)
. By
Theorem

5.4.11
,
\chi_{2}\ast w\in H^{t}_{2}(M)
.
More generally,

\displaystyle v\ast\chi_{n}\ast w

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[\![1,n]\!],S\neq\emptyset}[\chi_{s_{1}},[\chi_{s_{2}},\cdots,[v,\chi_{s_{|S|}}]]\cdots]\ast\chi_{\max{S^{c}}}\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

where
s_{1},\cdots,s_{|S|}
are the elements of
S
ordered in decreasing order, and with the convention
\chi_{\max{S^{c}}}\ast w=w
if
S=[\![1,n]\!]
.
The induction is now clear.
∎

Since for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{\infty}\ast w=\chi_{\infty}\ast\chi_{n}\ast w

\displaystyle\mod C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),

it follows that
\chi_{\infty}\ast w\in\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}H^{t}_{n}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)
.
Hence, by
Theorem

5.7.2
,
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}(w)
. This finishes the proof of
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
in the case where
M
is compact.

We now prove
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
in the general case where
M
isn’t necessarily compact.

Lemma 5.7.5
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
, there exists
v_{k}\in\Psi^{k}(M)
such that
v_{k}^{*}=v_{k}
and for any
x\in M
,
\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{x}
,
\sigma^{k}(v_{k},\pi,x):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)
is injective.

Proof.

This follows from
5.5.1
and
Theorem

5.4.11
.
iii
using a partition of unity argument. The assumption of
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
is necessary because to make the partition of unity argument work, one takes a sum of positive operators.
We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
be a closed cone,
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi
is elliptic on the complement of
\Gamma
, and
\chi
is smoothing on
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
By replacing
v
by
v^{*}\ast v_{-\Re(k)}^{*}\ast v_{-\Re(k)}\ast v+\chi^{*}\ast\chi
, we can suppose that
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
satisfies
v=v^{*}
, and
for all
(\pi,x)\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}
,
\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)
is injective.

Lemma 5.7.6
.

The distribution
v
has a parametrix, i.e., there exists
v^{\prime}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that

\displaystyle\delta_{1}-v^{\prime}\ast v,\delta_{1}-v\ast v^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Proof.

By
5.5.1
and
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
in the compact case that we just proved,
we can create parametrices for
v
locally.
Furthermore, these parametrices are smooth off the diagonal.
So, by using a partition of unity argument and
Section

4.1.3
.
viii
, we can find
v^{\prime}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
such that
v^{\prime}
is smooth off the diagonal, and
v^{\prime\prime}=\delta_{1}-v^{\prime}\ast v\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
satisfies

\displaystyle v^{\prime\prime}\ast H^{k}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)\subseteq H^{\prec k}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M),\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}

(5.38)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By replacing
v^{\prime}
by
(1+v^{\prime\prime}+(v^{\prime\prime})^{2}+\cdots(v^{\prime\prime})^{n})\ast v^{\prime}
, we can suppose that

v^{\prime\prime}=\delta_{1}-v^{\prime}\ast v
decreases the order of Sobolev spaces as much as we want.
So, by taking
n
big enough, and using
Section

4.4.2
.
v
(and the classical Sobolev embedding theorem) we can suppose that
v^{\prime\prime}\in C(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
which is smooth off the diagonal because
v
and
v^{\prime}
are smooth off the diagonal.
We can thus write
v^{\prime\prime}
as
v^{\prime\prime}_{1}+v^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, where
v^{\prime\prime}_{1}\in C^{\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{2}\in C(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
extends to a bounded operator
L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
of norm is
<1
. Here, we can use Schur test to bound
v^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.
Since
v^{\prime\prime}
is properly supported, we can suppose that
v^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{2}
are properly supported.
The sum
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(v_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{n}
converges to a bounded operator
L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
.
By Schwartz kernel theorem, this operator comes from convolution by a distribution
v^{\prime\prime\prime}\in C^{-\infty}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
By writing
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
as

\displaystyle v^{\prime\prime\prime}=\delta_{1}+v^{\prime\prime}+v^{\prime\prime}\ast v^{\prime\prime\prime}\ast v^{\prime\prime},

we deduce that
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
(v^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})^{*}
map
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
to
C^{\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
(in fact inside
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
because the support is proper).
So,
v^{\prime\prime\prime}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
Finally,

\displaystyle v^{\prime\prime\prime}\ast v^{\prime}\ast v=\delta_{1}-v^{\prime\prime\prime}\ast v_{1}^{\prime\prime},

and
v^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\ast v_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is smoothing operator because
v_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is smoothing and
v^{\prime\prime\prime}\in C^{-\infty}_{r,s}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We have thus obtained a left parametrix. By taking the adjoint, we obtain a right parametrix.
∎

So, we proved that
v
has a parametrix
v^{\prime}
.
By
5.5.1
and
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
in the compact case, the parametrix
v^{\prime}
has to satisfy
\mathrm{WF}_{l}(v^{\prime}\ast w)\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)
and
\mathrm{WF}_{l}(v^{\prime*}\ast w)\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{l}(w)
for all
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
and
l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
.

The inclusion
\Gamma\subseteq\{(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0:(-\xi,\xi;x,x)\in\mathrm{WF}(v)\}
follows immediately from
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

4.5.3
.
ii
.

We now prove
Section

4.5.3
.
iv
. From
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
, (
4.19
) follows easily.
Now, suppose that
(\xi,x)\in T^{*}M\backslash 0
and
l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
satisfy

\displaystyle(\xi,x)\in\mathrm{WF}_{l+\Re(k)}(w)\iff(\xi,x)\in\mathrm{WF}_{l}(v\ast w)

for all
w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We will prove that
(\xi,x)\in\Gamma
.
Since this is a local statement, by
5.5.1
, we can suppose that
M
is compact.
The operators
v_{l}\in\Psi^{l}(M)
obtained in
Theorem

5.4.11
.
iii
satisfy (
4.19
) with
\Gamma=T^{*}M\backslash 0
by what we proved above.
So, by replacing
v
by
v_{l}\ast v\ast v_{-l-k}
, we can suppose that
v\in\Psi^{0}(M)
, and

\displaystyle(\xi,x)\in\mathrm{WF}_{0}(w)\iff(\xi,x)\in\mathrm{WF}_{0}(v\ast w).

(5.39)

Let
\chi\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
whose classical principal symbol vanishes only at
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}\cdot(\xi,x)
.
We also fix an operator
v^{\prime}\in\Psi^{(-1,\cdots,-1)}(M)
as in
Theorem

5.4.11
.
iii
.
Consider the vector space

\displaystyle H=\{w\in C^{-\infty}(M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}):v\ast w,\ \chi\ast w,\ v^{\prime}\ast w\in L^{2}(M)\}

equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{H}:=\max\left(\left\lVert v\ast w\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)},\left\lVert\chi\ast w\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)},\left\lVert v^{\prime}\ast w\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)}\right).

The space
H
is complete because
v^{\prime}\ast\cdot:H^{(-1,\cdots,-1)}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is a topological isomorphism.
We claim that
H=L^{2}(M)
as a set.
The inclusion
L^{2}(M)\subseteq H
is clear. For the other inclusion, let
w\in H
.
Since
\chi\ast w\in L^{2}(M)
,
\mathrm{WF}_{0}(w)\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}(\xi,x)
.
Since
v\ast w\in L^{2}(M)
,
\mathrm{WF}_{0}(v\ast w)=\emptyset
. So, by (
5.39
),
(\xi,x)\notin\mathrm{WF}_{0}(w)
.
Hence,
\mathrm{WF}_{0}(w)=\emptyset
. So, by
Section

4.5.2
.
ii
,
w\in L^{2}(M)
.
The inclusion
L^{2}(M)\to H
is clearly continuous. By the open mapping theorem, it is a topological isomorphism.
Hence, there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{2}(M)}\leq C\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{H}.

This means that the operator
v^{*}\ast v+\chi^{*}\ast\chi+v^{\prime*}\ast v^{\prime}:L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
is invertible.
The operator
v^{\prime*}\ast v^{\prime}
maps
L^{2}(M)
into
H^{(2,\cdots,2)}(M)
. So, as an operator
L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
, it is compact by
Section

4.4.2
.
iv
.
So,
v^{*}\ast v+\chi^{*}\ast\chi
is invertible in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))
.
By
5.4.8
,
\sigma^{0}(v^{*}\ast v+\chi^{*}\ast\chi,\pi,x):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is injective for all
\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{x}
.
Hence, by (
5.37
) and since the classical principal symbol of
\chi
vanishes at
(\xi,x)
,
\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is injective for all
\pi\in\widehat{\mathrm{H\!N}}^{\mathrm{Nonsing}}_{(\xi,x)}
. So,
(\xi,x)\in\Gamma
.
This finishes the proof of
Section

4.5.3
.
iv
.

We now prove
Section

4.5.3
.
v
.
We fix
v^{\prime\prime}\in\Psi^{k}_{c}(M)
.
Let
\Gamma_{n}\subseteq\Gamma
be a sequence of open cones such that
\bigcup_{n}\Gamma_{n}=\Gamma
, and
\overline{\Gamma_{n}}\subseteq\Gamma_{n+1}
.
For each
n
, we fix
v_{n}^{\prime}
as in
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
applied to
\overline{\Gamma_{n}}
, and
\chi_{n}\in\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)
such that
\chi_{n}
is elliptic on
\overline{\Gamma_{n-1}}
, and smoothing on the complement of
\Gamma_{n}
, and
\chi_{n}
are (formally) positive.
Since
v_{n}^{\prime}
is smooth off the diagonal, by modifying
v_{n}^{\prime}
and
\chi_{n}
outside the diagonal, we can suppose that the operators
(v_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\chi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly properly supported.

For each
n
, let
r_{n}=\delta_{1}-v^{\prime}_{n}\ast v
. So, we have

\displaystyle\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}=\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast r_{n}+\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast v_{n}^{\prime}\ast v

(5.40)

By (
4.18
) and (
4.5
) (applied to
v^{\prime\prime}
),
\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast r_{n}
is a smoothing operator, which is uniformly compactly supported because
v^{\prime\prime}
is compactly supported.
So,
\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast r_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
For each
n
, the operator
\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast v_{n}^{\prime}
maps
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
to
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
. To see this, let
w\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{WF}_{0}(\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast v_{n}^{\prime}\ast w)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{0}(v^{\prime\prime}\ast v_{n}^{\prime}\ast w)

\displaystyle\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap}{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set} \hyperref@@ii[thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set:3]{\ref*{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set}.\ref*{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set:3}}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{WF}_{\Re(k)}(v_{n}^{\prime}\ast w)

\displaystyle\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap}{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set} \hyperref@@ii[thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set:3]{\ref*{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set}.\ref*{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set:3}}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{WF}_{0}(w)

\displaystyle\text{ by \eqref{eqn:parametrix_WF}}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap}{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set} \hyperref@@ii[thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set:1]{\ref*{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set}.\ref*{thm:multi_parameter_wave_front_set:1}}}.

Furthermore, since
v^{\prime\prime}
is compactly supported, the image is actually in
L^{2}(M)
. So, the operator
\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast v_{n}^{\prime}
maps
L^{2}(M)
to
L^{2}(M)
.
Hence, it is continuous by the closed graph theorem.
Now, we take a sequence
c_{n}>0
which converges to
0
fast enough so that the sums

\displaystyle\chi=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}c_{n}\chi_{n}

\displaystyle\text{ converges in }\Psi^{0}_{\mathrm{classical}}(M)

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}c_{n}\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast r_{n}

\displaystyle\text{ converges in }C^{\infty}_{c}(M\times M,\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle v^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}c_{n}\chi_{n}\ast v^{\prime\prime}\ast v_{n}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{ converges in }\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(M))

By (
5.40
), we have

\displaystyle\chi\ast v^{\prime\prime}=r+v^{\prime}\ast v.

(5.41)

From this, (
4.20
) follows.
If
\Gamma=T^{*}M\backslash 0
, then there is no need to do the above. One simply applies
Section

4.5.3
.
iii
with
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma
.
The details are left to the reader.
This finishes the proof of
Section

4.5.3
.
v
.

We finally prove
Section

4.5.3
.
vi
.
By
Theorem

5.4.11
.
iii
, we can suppose that
k=0
.
By
Section

4.5.3
.
i
,
\sigma^{0}(v,\pi,x)
is injective on
L^{2}(\pi)
.
So, by (
5.31
) and
B.0.1
,
v
is left invertible in
\overline{\Psi^{0}(M)}/\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(M))
, i.e., it is left-invertible modulo compact operators.

∎

Proof of
4.5.5
.

It suffices to apply
4.5.3
to
v^{*}\ast v
and
v\ast v^{*}
.

∎

Appendix A
Kirillov’s orbit method

Let
G
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra.
So, the exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
is a diffeomorphism.
For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, let
B_{\xi}:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{R}
be the antisymmetric bilinear form
B_{\xi}(v,w)=\xi([v,w])
, and
\ker(B_{\xi})=\{v\in\mathfrak{g}:B_{\xi}(v,w)=0,\ \forall w\in\mathfrak{g}\}
.
Since
B_{\xi}
is antisymmetric,
\codim(\ker(B_{\xi}))
is even.
Let
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie subalgebra such that
B_{\xi}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h})=0
,
H\subseteq G
the Lie subgroup integrating
\mathfrak{h}
.
The map
\exp
restricts to a diffeomorphism
\mathfrak{h}\to H
, so in particular
H
is closed.
We define the Hilbert space

L^{2}(G/H,\xi)
to be the space of measurable functions
f:G\to\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}(\frac{\mathfrak{g}}{\mathfrak{h}})
such that
f(x\exp(y))=f(x)e^{i\xi(y)}
for all
x\in G
and
y\in\mathfrak{h}
, and
\int_{G/H}|f|^{2}<+\infty
. We have a unitary representation of
G
on
L^{2}(G/H,\xi)
given by

\displaystyle\Xi_{G/H,\xi}(f)\ast g(x)=\int_{G}f(y)g(y^{-1}x),\quad f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G,\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{g})),g\in L^{2}(G/H,\xi).

(A.1)

The main theorem on Kirillov’s orbit method can be summarized as follows:

Theorem A.0.1

(
[
16
, Section 2.2]
)
.

i.

For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, there exists a Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
which satisfies
B_{\xi}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h})=0
, and
\codim(\mathfrak{h})=\codim(\ker(B_{\xi}))/2
.
We call
\mathfrak{h}
a polarizing Lie subalgebra for
\xi
.

ii.

If
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{h}
a polarizing Lie subalgebra, then
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
is an irreducible unitary representation.
Furthermore, any irreducible unitary representation of
G
is unitarily equivalent to
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
for some
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{h}
a polarizing Lie subalgebra.

iii.

If
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
are polarizing Lie subalgebras for
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
, then
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
is unitarily equivalent to
\Xi_{G/H^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}
if and only if
there exists
g\in G
such that
\xi=\Ad^{*}(g)\xi^{\prime}
.

By taking
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
in
Theorem

A.0.1
.
iii
, it follows that the equivalence class of
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
doesn’t depend on
\mathfrak{h}
.
Thus, we have a bijective map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G\to\hat{G},\quad[\xi]\mapsto[\Xi_{G/H,\xi}],\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}

(A.2)

where
\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G
is the quotient of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by the co-adjoint action.

Theorem A.0.2

(Brown’s theorem
[
11
]
)
.

The map (
A.2
) is a homeomorphism when
\mathfrak{g}/G
is equipped with the quotient topology and
\hat{G}
is equipped with the Fell topology.

Theorem

A.0.1
gives irreducibility of the representations
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
when
\mathfrak{h}
is polarizing. The following theorem due to Ludwig
[
36
]
analyzes the representations
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
in the general case.

Theorem A.0.3
.

Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{h}
a Lie subalgebra which satisfies
B_{\xi}(\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h})=0
. Then, the set of irreducible unitary representations of
G
which are weakly contained in
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}

correspond by the bijection (
A.2
) to the set

\displaystyle\overline{\bigcup_{g\in G}\{\Ad^{*}(g)\eta\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}:\eta_{|\mathfrak{h}}=\xi\}}/G

Smooth vectors

Smooth vectors of the representation
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
in the irreducible case have a very simple characterization:

Theorem A.0.4

(
[
16
, Corollary 4.1.2]
)
.

Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
a polarizing Lie subalgebra, then if one identifies
L^{2}(G/H,\xi)
with
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k})

for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
using any weak Maclev basis (see
[
16
]
), then the space of smooth vectors of
\Xi_{G/H,\xi}
is equal to the space of Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Graded Lie-algebra

We say that
\mathfrak{g}
is
\nu
-graded if
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
with
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=0
, and

[\mathfrak{g}_{k},\mathfrak{g}_{l}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{k+l}
for all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
.
Such an algebra admits an
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\nu}
-dilations
\alpha_{\lambda}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
, see (
2.6
).

Decomposition of the spectrum:

We will need the following result by I. Beltita, D. Beltita, and J. Ludwig
[
6
, Theorem 4.11 and Proposition 4.5, and definition 2.9]
on the structure of the space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G
.

Theorem A.0.5
.

There exists a finite sequence of open subsets
\emptyset=V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{n}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
for some
n
such that

i.

For any
i\in[\![0,n]\!]
,
V_{i}
is closed under the co-adjoint action, and closed under the canonical vector space
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times}
-dilation.

ii.

For all
i\in[\![0,n-1]\!]
, the quotient
(V_{i+1}\backslash V_{i})/G
is a Hausdorff locally compact space (in fact a semi-algebraic space).
Furthermore,
V_{n}\backslash V_{n-1}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]^{\perp}
. Note that the co-adjoint action on
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]^{\perp}
is trivial.

iii.

If
I_{i}
denotes the closed two-sided ideal of
C^{*}G
whose spectrum is
V_{i}
, then one has

\displaystyle 0=I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\cdots\subseteq I_{n-1}\subseteq I_{n}=C^{*}G,

and

\displaystyle I_{i+1}/I_{i}\simeq C_{0}((V_{i+1}\backslash V_{i})/G)\otimes\mathcal{K}(l^{2}(\mathbb{N})),

\displaystyle\forall i\in[\![0,n-2]\!]

\displaystyle I_{n}/I_{n-1}\simeq C_{0}(V_{n}\backslash V_{n-1})

iv.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is
\nu
-graded by
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\nu}
, then one can suppose that each
V_{i}
is invariant by the dilations
\alpha_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.

Theorem

A.0.5
.
iv
isn’t explicitly stated in
[
6
]
, but it follows easily from the construction of the sets
V_{i}
, see
[
6
, Section 4.1 and Section 4.2]
(one has to choose a Jordan-Holder basis which consist of pure elements).

Direct sum:

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, if
G_{i}
are simply connected nilpotent Lie groups,
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
their Lie algebras,
\xi_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{i}
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{i}
are polarizing Lie subalgebras, then

\mathfrak{h}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{h_{2}}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is a polarizing Lie subalgebra for
\xi_{1}\oplus\xi_{2}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\Xi_{(G_{1}\times G_{2})/(H_{1}\times H_{2}),\xi_{1}\oplus\xi_{2}}\simeq\Xi_{G_{1}/H_{1},\xi_{1}}\otimes\Xi_{G_{2}/H_{2},\xi_{2}}.

(A.3)

Some auxiliary results in graded Lie algebras:

Motivated by (
2.37
), we say that a
\nu
-graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is weakly-commutative if
\mathfrak{g}_{k}=0
whenever
|\{i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]:k_{i}\neq 0\}|>1
.
We define
\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{i}}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mathfrak{g}^{(\overbrace{0,\cdots,0}^{i-1},j,\overbrace{0\cdots,0}^{\nu-i})}
.
So, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{i}},\quad\forall x\in M.

(A.4)

Furthermore,
[\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{i}},\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{j}}]=0
for all
i,j\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
with
i\neq j
.

The set
\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}:\exists i\in[\![1,\nu]\!],\ \xi_{|\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{i}}}=0\}
is closed under the co-adjoint action.
We say that an irreducible unitary representation is singular if it corresponds by (
A.2
) to a co-adjoint orbit which is contained in the above set.
Otherwise, we say that it is non-singular.

We denote by
C^{*}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}G
the closed ideal of
C^{*}G
whose spectrum is the set of non-singular irreducible unitary representations.
We denote by
\hat{G}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}
the equivalence classes of non-singular irreducible unitary representations of
G
, i.e., the spectrum of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}G
.

Theorem A.0.6
.

The crossed product
C^{*}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}G\rtimes(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra of Type I whose spectrum is homeomorphic
\hat{G}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}/(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

A.0.5
.
One has to check that the action of
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}
on the open subset
\hat{G}_{\mathrm{nonsing}}\cap\left((V_{i+1}\backslash V_{i})/G\right)
of the locally compact space
(V_{i+1}\backslash V_{i})/G
is free and proper.
The details are left to the reader.
∎

Theorem A.0.7
.

If
\pi
is a non-singular representation of
G
, and
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
is a smooth vector, then for any
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
, there exists some
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{i}}
and
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\pi)

such that

\xi=\pi(X_{1})\xi_{1}+\cdots+\pi(X_{n})\xi_{n}
, where
\pi(X_{i})
is defined using the differential of
\pi
.

Proof.

By writing
G
as a product of the subgroups that integrate
\mathfrak{g}^{\hat{i}}
, the statement becomes the following:
If
G
is a simply connected nilpotent Lie group,
\pi
is a non-trivial irreducible unitary representation, then for some
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
, one has

\xi=\pi(X_{1})\xi_{1}+\cdots+\pi(X_{n})\xi_{n}
.
This is elementary. By Dixmier-Malliavin theorem, we can suppose that
\xi=\pi(f)\eta
for some
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
,
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
.
The fact that
\pi
is non-trivial implies that the trivial representation isn’t weakly contained in
\pi
.
So, there exists
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
such that
\int g=1
but
\left\lVert\pi(g)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))}<1
.
Hence,
{\hbox{Id}}-\pi(g):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is invertible. So,
\eta=\eta^{\prime}-\pi(g)\eta^{\prime}
for some
\eta^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\pi)
.
Hence,
\xi=\pi(f-f\ast g)\eta^{\prime}
. Since
\int f-f\ast g=0
, we can write it as a sum of functions of the form
X_{i}\ast f_{i}
, where
X_{i}\ast f_{i}
is the action of the right-invariant vector field associated to
X_{i}
on
f_{i}
, see
[
3
, Proposition 2.1]
.
So,
\xi
is a sum of elements of the form
\pi(X_{i})\pi(f_{i})\eta^{\prime}
.
∎

Theorem A.0.8

(Riemann-Lebesgue lemma)
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
and
\pi
is a non-singular irreducible unitary representation of
G
, then for all
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi(\alpha_{\lambda}(f))\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))}\leq\frac{C}{\lambda_{i}^{n}},\quad\forall\lambda\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\times})^{\nu}.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward adaptation of
[
38
, Lemma 2.11]
.
∎

Appendix B
Abstract theorem of maximal hypoellipticity

Proposition B.0.1

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
A
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. An element
a\in A
is left invertible if and only if
\pi(a):L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
is injective for every irreducible representation
\pi
of
A
.

Definition B.0.2

(Kaplansky and Glimm)
.

A
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is called

•

Liminal if for every irreducible representation
\pi
of
A
,
\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\pi))=\pi(A)
.

•

Type I if for every irreducible representation
\pi
of
A
,
\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\pi))\subseteq\pi(A)
.

We refer the reader to
[
22
, Chapters 4 and 9]
for a detailed discussion of liminal and Type I
C^{*}
-algebras.
The examples of liminal
C^{*}
-algebras that will be of interest to us are the
C^{*}
-algebras of simply connected nilpotent Lie groups, and of Type I
C^{*}
-algebras are the closure pseudo-differential operators of order
0
in our calculus.

Proposition B.0.3

(
[
22
, Proposition 4.3.5, Proposition 4.3.4, and the main theorem of Chapter 9]
)
.

If
I
is a two-sided ideal of a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, then
A
is of Type I if and only if
I
and
A/I
are of Type I.

Proposition B.0.4
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra of Type I,
\pi
an irreducible representation of
A
such that the singleton
\{\pi\}
is an open dense subset of
\hat{A}
.
Then, for any
a\in A
,
\pi(a)\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\pi))
if and only if
\pi^{\prime}(a)=0
for all
\pi^{\prime}\in\hat{A}\backslash\{\pi\}
.

Proof.

Since
\{\pi\}
is dense in
\widehat{A}
,
\pi:A\to\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))
is injective, see
[
22
, Proposition 3.3.2]
.
So, if we consider
A
as a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\pi))
, then
J:=\{a\in A:\pi(a)\in\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\pi))\}
is equal to
\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\pi))
.
So the spectrum of
J
is a singleton. At the same time since
J
is a closed two-sided ideal of
A
, its spectrum is an open subset of
\hat{A}
consisting of irreducible representations which don’t vanish on
J
.
Clearly
\pi
doesn’t vanish on
J
. So
\hat{J}=\{\pi\}
.
∎

Definition B.0.5
.

A
C^{*}
-calculus (with
\nu\geq 1
-parameters) is a family of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces
(\mathcal{A}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}
such that for each
k,l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, we have a product map
\mathcal{A}_{k}\times\mathcal{A}_{l}\to\mathcal{A}_{k+l}
which is bilinear and associative, i.e.,
(ab)c=a(bc)
if
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k_{1}}
,
b\in\mathcal{A}_{k_{2}}
,
c\in\mathcal{A}_{k_{3}}
.
There is a unit element
0\neq 1\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, i.e., 1a
=a1=a
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
.
We also have an involution map
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\mapsto a^{*}\in\mathcal{A}_{\bar{k}}
which is anti-linear and satisfies
1^{*}=1
and
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k_{1}}
,
b\in\mathcal{A}_{k_{2}}
.
We also have a
C^{*}
-semi-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}
on
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, i.e., a semi-norm such that
\left\lVert ab\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}\leq\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}
and
\left\lVert a^{*}a\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}=\left\lVert a\right\rVert^{2}_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
.
We denote by
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
the Hausdorff completion of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

We don’t suppose that we can add elements of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
and
\mathcal{A}_{l}
if
k\neq l
.
For our applications, there is no need to suppose that.
Also, the sum of classical pseudo-differential operators on a smooth manifold of order
k\in\mathbb{C}
and
l\in\mathbb{C}
is a classical pseudo-differential operator if and only if
k-l\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition B.0.6
.

A representation of a
C^{*}
-calculus
(\mathcal{A}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}
consists of a non-zero
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
C^{\infty}(\pi)
equipped with a positive definite sesquilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:C^{\infty}(\pi)\times C^{\infty}(\pi)\to\mathbb{C}
,
and a family of linear maps
\pi_{k}:\mathcal{A}_{k}\to\mathrm{End}(C^{\infty}(\pi))
for every
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
such that the following hold:

i.

If
k,l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
,
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
,
b\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
,
\xi,\eta\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
, then

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(1)=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{\infty}(\pi)},\quad\pi_{k}(a)\pi_{l}(b)=\pi_{k+l}(ab),\quad\langle\pi_{k}(a)\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle\xi,\pi_{\bar{k}}(a^{*})\eta\rangle.

ii.

If
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}_{\leq 0}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
, then
\pi_{k}(a)
extends to a bounded operator
L^{2}(\pi)\to L^{2}(\pi)
, where
L^{2}(\pi)
is the Hilbert space completion of
C^{\infty}(\pi)
with respect to the norm
\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi)}^{2}:=\langle\xi,\xi\rangle
.
We also suppose that if
a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\pi_{0}(a)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}.

(B.1)

So,
\pi_{0}
extends to a representation of
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
.

iii.

The space
C^{\infty}(\pi)
equipped with the topology generated by the family of semi-norms
\left\lVert\pi_{k}(a)\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi)}
for
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
is complete.

The representation is called irreducible if it is irreducible as a representation of the
C^{*}
-algebra
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
, i.e., if
L\subseteq L^{2}(\pi)
is a closed subspace which is invariant by
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, then
L=0
or
L=L^{2}(\pi)
.

We denote by
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
the space of continuous anti-linear functionals on
C^{\infty}(\pi)
.
The action of
\xi\in C^{-\infty}(\pi)
on
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
is denoted by
\langle\eta,\xi\rangle
.
The space
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
is equipped with the topology generated by the semi-norms
\xi\mapsto|\langle\eta,\xi\rangle|
for every
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\pi)
.
We have obvious continuous linear inclusions
C^{\infty}(\pi)\subseteq L^{2}(\pi)\subseteq C^{-\infty}(\pi)
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, we extend
\pi_{k}
to a linear map
\pi_{k}:\mathcal{A}_{k}\to\mathcal{L}(C^{-\infty}(\pi))
by the formula

\displaystyle\langle\eta,\pi_{k}(a)\xi\rangle:=\langle\pi_{\bar{k}}(a^{*})\eta,\xi\rangle,\quad\forall a\in\mathcal{A}_{k},\xi\in C^{-\infty}(\pi),\eta\in C^{\infty}(\pi).

There are various ways to define Sobolev spaces associated to the representation
\pi
.
We will use here the most restrictive one. Our main theorem implies that they coincide with the least restrictive one, see (
B.5
).
Let
H^{k}(\pi)
be the set of
\xi\in C^{-\infty}(\pi)
such that
there exists a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(\pi)
such that
\xi_{n}\to\xi
in
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
, and for any
l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
such that
\Re(l)\leq k
, one has
\pi_{l}(a)\xi\in L^{2}(\pi)
, and
\pi_{l}(a)\xi_{n}\to\pi_{l}(a)\xi
in
L^{2}(\pi)
.
We equip
H^{k}(\pi)
with the topology generated by the family of semi-norms
\xi\mapsto\left\lVert\pi_{l}(a)\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\pi)}
for all
l\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
such that
\Re(l)\leq k
.
Clearly,

\displaystyle H^{0}(M)=L^{2}(\pi),\quad H^{l}(\pi)\subseteq H^{k}(\pi),\quad\text{if }k\leq l,

and if
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
,
l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
, then
\pi_{k}(a)(H^{l}(\pi))\subseteq H^{l-\Re(k)}(\pi)
, and the map
H^{l}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\pi_{k}(a)}H^{l-\Re(k)}(\pi)
is continuous.
The spaces
C^{\infty}(\pi)
,
C^{-\infty}(\pi)
,
H^{k}(\pi)
are called the space of smooth vectors, distribution vectors and Sobolev spaces associated to the representation
\pi
respectively.

Proposition B.0.7

(
[
38
]
)
.

One has

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\pi)=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}_{+}}H^{k}(\pi).

(B.2)

In the following theorem,
K
is the set of
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
such that
\Re(k_{i}),\Im(k_{i})\in[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
for all
i\in[\![1,\nu]\!]
, and
k_{i}\neq 0
for at most one
i
.

Theorem B.0.8

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A})_{k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}
be a
C^{*}
-calculus,
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
a set of representations of
(\mathcal{A})_{k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}
such that the following hold:

i.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
is of Type I.

ii.

Any irreducible representation of
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
is unitarily equivalent to
\pi_{0}
for some
\pi\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
.

iii.

We can find a family of elements
v_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
for
k\in K
such that:

(a)

The element
v_{0}
is invertible in
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
.

(b)

For any
a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, the map

\displaystyle k\in K\mapsto v_{k}av_{-k}\in\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}

(B.3)

is continuous, where
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
is equipped with the norm topology from
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}}
.

Then following hold:

i.

Let
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
. If for any
\pi\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
,
C^{\infty}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\pi_{k}(a)}C^{\infty}(\pi)
and
C^{\infty}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\pi_{k}(a^{*})}C^{\infty}(\pi)
are injective, then for every representation
\pi
of
(\mathcal{A})_{k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}
(not necessarily irreducible and not necessarily in
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
), the maps

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(a):C^{-\infty}(\pi)\to C^{-\infty}(\pi)

(B.4)

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(a):H^{l}(\pi)\to H^{l-\Re(k)}(\pi)

\displaystyle\forall l\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}

\displaystyle\pi_{k}(a):C^{\infty}(\pi)\to C^{\infty}(\pi)

are topological isomorphisms.
Furthermore, for any
k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
, there exists
v\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
which satisfies the above.

ii.

For any representation
\pi
of
(\mathcal{A})_{k\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}}
,
H^{k}(\pi)
is a Hilbertian space, and

\displaystyle H^{k}(\pi)=\{\xi\in C^{-\infty}(\pi):\pi_{k}(a)\xi\in L^{2}(\pi),\ \forall a\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\}

(B.5)

and
C^{-\infty}(\pi)=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}}H^{k}(\pi)
.

iii.

If
a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, then
a
is left-invertible in
\overline{\mathcal{A}_{0}}
if and only if for all
\pi\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
,
C^{\infty}(\pi)\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}(a)}C^{\infty}(\pi)
is injective
